Content, metadata and logs are kept 30 days in the US whatever region you choose, and Resend pauses sending above 4% bounces or 0.08% spam and stops at 5 times the monthly quota (https://resend.com/docs/knowledge-base/account-quotas-and-limits, accessed 2026-09-29). So never put vault secrets, bearer tokens, sign-in links or approval links in an email. The only links allowed are protective ones that can only reduce access (freeze, recovery cancel, turn off auto-renew) and the emailed recovery code: each is stored hashed, single use, bound to one event and expiring, and a GET only shows a confirm page (4.5). Resend auto-revokes exposed `re_` keys (GitHub secret-scanning partner since 2026-07-16, per the Resend changelog as recorded in the recipes dossier's verification notes), so the runtime must survive a sudden 401 with a re-mint path. Domain quota: Free 3, Pro 10 (https://resend.com/pricing, accessed 2026-09-29) |
| A cloud KMS | **AWS KMS** | Symmetric AES-256 keys in us-east-1, reached through Vercel OIDC federation (`AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity`), no static keys (https://vercel.com/docs/oidc/aws, accessed 2026-09-29). KEK layout: one set per deployment environment (production, staging, development); inside production two KEKs and two IAM roles, `vault-prod` for customers' `prod` secrets and `vault-nonprod` for their `dev` and `preview` secrets; the encryption-context field `env` carries the customer's project environment (4.3b Secrets). Only AWS documents IAM and key-policy conditions on the encryption context (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/encrypt_context.html, accessed 2026-09-29) and has a server-side `ReEncrypt` (https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/APIReference/API_ReEncrypt.html, accessed 2026-09-29). Cost is USD 1 per key-month plus USD 0.03 per 10,000 requests (https://aws.amazon.com/kms/pricing/, accessed 2026-09-29); the rotation surcharge (USD 1 a month after each of the first two rotations, https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/rotate-keys.html, accessed 2026-09-29) takes four keys from about USD 4 to about USD 12 a month by year three. Three further production keys (PII, MAC, backup) and optional multi-Region replicas (USD 1 a month each; https://docs.aws.amazon.com/kms/latest/developerguide/multi-region-keys-overview.html, accessed 2026-09-29) are listed in 4.3b Loss radius and D-026. GCP Cloud KMS is the runner-up; Azure Key Vault Standard has no AAD; Vercel's own KMS is signing-only and beta |
| Strict CSP, no third-party scripts | **Confirm, and a working policy exists** | `default-src 'none'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' blob:; font-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; worker-src 'self'; manifest-src 'self'; base-uri 'none'; object-src 'none'; form-action 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'; upgrade-insecure-requests; report-uri /api/csp-report; report-to csp`. No nonce, no `unsafe-inline`, no `unsafe-eval`; verified in Chromium 141 with Vite 8.3.1, React 19.3.0 and three 0.186.1 (directive semantics: https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Reference/Headers/Content-Security-Policy/script-src, accessed 2026-09-29). Only Chromium 141 was run; Firefox and Safari behaviour is read from MDN and compatibility data. Traps: `form-action 'self'` blocks Stripe's form-POST-then-303 pattern (use `fetch` then `location.assign(url)`); `connect-src` needs `blob:` once GLB models load (the brief allows no model files); `@react-three/drei` defaults load from CDNs and spawn Blob workers that this CSP blocks, so Mosshatch does not use drei; Trusted Types is feasible and is enforced before the Nest ships (Report-Only header in the table below). **Boot sentinel.** The fallback that delivers "never a blank screen" cannot be an inline script under this policy. It is `/boot.js`, an external classic script (no `defer`, no `async`, not a module) placed before the module script so its `error` listener and timeout check exist before the app loads. The fallback panel is pre-rendered `[hidden]` markup in `index.html`; the script only removes `hidden` and sets `textContent`, never `innerHTML`, which keeps Trusted Types quiet. The panel stays legible if its stylesheet 404s. `/boot.js` is not hashed, so it is served with revalidation and excluded from immutable caching. Exit test: the fallback works with the CSP enforced and zero violations |
| Response headers (D-015) | **Set in `vercel.json`, checked in CI** | The full set is the table under this one. Referrer-Policy is `strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, not `no-referrer` |
| Self-hosted fonts | **Confirm** | Young Serif (Google Fonts v3.003, Regular 400) and Atkinson Hyperlegible **Next** are SIL OFL 1.1 with no Reserved Font Name, so self-hosting and subsetting are allowed (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/google/fonts/main/ofl/youngserif/OFL.txt and https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googlefonts/atkinson-hyperlegible-next/main/OFL.txt, accessed 2026-09-29). The brief names Atkinson Hyperlegible; Next is used because one variable file (wght 400 to 700, 20.4 kB) replaces two static files (22.2 kB), and the original family is the fallback (40.7 kB for the pair; D-029). Measured subset: 38.8 kB WOFF2 for both, 36% under Google's prebuilt latin pair. Ship the OFL text |
| Test that fails the build if a secret reaches the bundle | **Confirm; a prototype exists** | Vite exposes only `VITE_*` and has no other leak check. The prototype scanner (356 lines, Node built-ins only) found 41 of 43 planted fakes in minified output and 43 of 43 where source names are visible, and reported 0 errors and 11 warnings across 59 builds of overlapping code (all warnings in deliberately hostile stress bundles: a `jose` label constant and placeholder connection strings in the Neon driver). The filters were tuned on the same corpus, so the false-positive rate on new code is unmeasured, and it was run only by its author. Limits: it cannot find an unknown-format secret in a local variable of a built bundle, so it is one layer. Wiring: it runs inside the build command Vercel runs, not in CI alone (`vite build && node scripts/scan-client-bundle.mjs dist ...`); `--require-env` names the server variables that must be visible in the build and exits 2 if any is missing, so the server-value search cannot pass on an empty environment; it scans `apps/web/dist`, `apps/cards/dist`, the published CLI tarball and all source maps. Whether Vercel fails a deployment on a non-zero build command, and whether Secret-type variables are present during the build, are unverified until a deliberately failing preview proves them in Phase 1 |
| Session in HttpOnly, SameSite cookies | **Confirm, `Lax`** | A `SameSite=Strict` cookie is not sent on any request of a cross-site-initiated navigation (verified in Chromium; RFC 6265bis), so the return from Stripe would look logged out. Use a `__Host-` `Secure; HttpOnly; Path=/; SameSite=Lax` cookie. Guard for every non-GET request to a cookie route, including `/auth/*`: `Sec-Fetch-Site` absent or `same-origin` (not `same-site`, which admits sibling subdomains), `Origin` equal to the app origin, JSON content type, and header `X-MH-Client: web`; ceremony endpoints also carry a session-bound CSRF token, and login options set a short-lived `__Host-mh_pre` cookie that verify must present. No handler changes state on GET, cookie routes ignore bearer tokens and bearer routes ignore cookies (a request with both is rejected, 4.5). Passkey-bound approvals guard money and secrets |
| MCP server | **Confirm; target the 2026-07-28 spec** | Stateless Streamable HTTP (https://modelcontextprotocol.io/specification/2026-07-28/basic/transports/streamable-http, accessed 2026-09-29), `@modelcontextprotocol/server` 2.2.0 (https://registry.npmjs.org/@modelcontextprotocol/server; the v1 `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` 1.31.0 is legacy), SDK handler performs no Origin or auth checks, so we add them. Only Claude Code is documented to speak 2026-07-28; Claude apps, Cursor and VS Code are unverified, so the server keeps the SDK default `legacy: 'stateless'` for 2025-era clients and does not set `legacy: 'reject'` until each client is tested in Phase 5. Claude Code, Cursor and VS Code accept a static bearer header; claude.ai and Desktop need OAuth. Elicitation is not a control, so "needs human approval" is server-enforced (`pending_human_approval` plus a first-party passkey page) |
| Repository tooling | **npm workspaces, Node 24** | The toolset's combined engine floor is Node 22.19 or later. npm 11.19 and 12.1 block dependency install scripts by default: the scaffold needs an explicit `allowScripts` policy. Vercel's default install for a `package-lock.json` project is `npm install`, so set `installCommand` to `npm ci`. Do not install `@vercel/node` (its pinned transitive dependencies carry advisories; fetch handlers need no types package) and add an npm `overrides` entry for `drizzle-kit`'s `@esbuild-kit` esbuild: the recommended manifest audited at 0 vulnerabilities in the Phase 0 scratch install (D-013). Production builds probably hold the same OIDC identity as production functions (4.3b Secrets), so the install policy, `npm ci` and reviewed lockfile updates protect the build as well as the CI |

**Response headers.** Set in `vercel.json` for every route of `web` and `cards`, and asserted by a CI test that fetches each route class. Function-set headers override `vercel.json`, so secret and session functions set `Cache-Control` themselves. Sources: the header dossier's Chromium 141 probe (`docs/research/tech-fonts-csp-headers.md`), MDN and the OWASP HTTP Headers cheat sheet (https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OWASP/CheatSheetSeries/master/cheatsheets/HTTP_Headers_Cheat_Sheet.md, accessed 2026-09-29).

| Header | Value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| `Content-Security-Policy` | the policy in the CSP row | Enforced |
| `Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only` | `require-trusted-types-for 'script'; trusted-types; report-uri /api/csp-report; report-to csp` | Enforced (merged into the policy above) before the Nest ships: zero violations in the end-to-end suite and 14 quiet days in staging (own target). Firefox before 148 and Safari before 26 ignore the directive, so it is defence in depth (https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTTP/Reference/Headers/Content-Security-Policy/require-trusted-types-for, accessed 2026-09-29) |
| `Reporting-Endpoints` | `csp="/api/csp-report"` | Without it `report-to csp` is dropped. The relative form delivered over HTTPS in Chromium 141 (the verifier's test); Firefox and Safari are untested, so `report-uri` stays. The handler accepts `application/csp-report` and `application/reports+json` arrays, caps the body at 8 KB, rate limits, logs no full bodies, drops `blocked-uri` query strings and extension schemes, and treats every report as attacker-controlled |
| `Strict-Transport-Security` | `max-age=63072000` | Vercel's default on custom domains. `includeSubDomains` only after auditing subdomains; `preload` only deliberately. Vercel documents that the header can be overridden; whether an explicit value replaces or duplicates the default is untested |
| `X-Content-Type-Options` | `nosniff` | |
| `Referrer-Policy` | `strict-origin-when-cross-origin` | `no-referrer` makes Chromium send `Origin: null` on same-origin form POSTs, which would defeat the Origin check if a real form is ever added. No secret is ever in a URL, so the header carries none, and the Stripe return URL's `session_id` is not sent to third parties |
| `Permissions-Policy` | `publickey-credentials-get=(self), publickey-credentials-create=(self), camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=(), payment=(), usb=(), serial=(), hid=(), midi=(), display-capture=(), xr-spatial-tracking=()` | Enforced by Chromium only; unknown tokens (`bluetooth`, `browsing-topics`) are pruned because Chromium logs them |
| `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy` | `same-origin` | No popup flows are planned |
| `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy` | `same-origin` | The public card-image route on `cards` sends `cross-origin` from its own function |
| `X-Frame-Options` | `DENY` | Legacy twin of `frame-ancestors 'none'` |
| `Cache-Control` | `no-store, private` on every `/api/*` route | Set again inside every session, reveal and read function; `/boot.js` uses `no-cache` (revalidate) |
| `Clear-Site-Data` | `"cookies", "storage"` on logout | Plus an explicit expiring `Set-Cookie`, because Safari before 17 ignores the header |

Not verifiable in this container: Firefox and Safari behaviour of the CSP, Trusted Types, SameSite, `__Host-` and `Reporting-Endpoints` results (only Chromium 141 was run), and the merge order of overlapping `vercel.json` header rules on a live Vercel deployment.

### 4.3b Architecture

#### Repository layout (Phase 1 scaffold)

```
apps/web            Vite + React + TypeScript. React owns the overlay only. Vercel project `web`
  public/boot.js    external boot sentinel (unhashed; see the CSP row in 4.3a)
  src/world/        three.js, imperative: renderer, camera rig, sky, pool, trees, lanterns,
                    particles, creatures/, shaders/*.glsl, snapshot.ts, quality.ts
  src/store/        Zustand. View state only (see "The store contract" below)
  src/ui/           header, find, hatch sheet, detail panel tabs, ledger, rescue, fallback
  src/reveal/       the only browser module that ever holds a secret value
  src/audio/        WebAudio synthesis, off by default
  debug.html        the ?debug=states entry; its own entry, excluded from production builds
apps/registrar      Vercel project `registrar`: the signed RPC endpoint that wraps RegistrarAdapter, nothing else
apps/cards          hatchkind.com, Vercel project `cards`: static/SSR public pages (separate origin, no cookies)
packages/core       Pure TypeScript shared by server and client: deriveTraits(), deriveCreatureState(),
                    money, pricing, recipes schema, scope vocabulary, wire.ts (strict response schemas).
                    No I/O, no secrets.
packages/registrar  RegistrarAdapter interface, MockRegistrar, contract test suite, real adapters
packages/vault      The only server module that may import the KMS client, call createDecipheriv or define SecretValue
packages/db         Drizzle schema, migrations, roles and grants
packages/api        Route handlers (deployed by apps/web), auth, step-up, orders, jobs, webhooks
packages/cli        `mosshatch` CLI
packages/mcp        MCP server (Streamable HTTP) sharing handlers with the REST API
scripts/            scan-client-bundle.mjs, fonts.sh
e2e/                Playwright + axe
docs/               PLAN.md, DECISIONS.md, COMPLIANCE.md, research/
```

Workspace tooling and exact versions are pinned in Phase 1 from the verified table in the plan. Performance budgets are measured on the production `dist/`, not on the debug entry; the build fails if `dist/` contains a fixture name or the string `debug=states`.

#### Deployment topology

Three Vercel projects on one Pro team (D-005).

| Project | Runs | Holds | Never holds |
|---|---|---|---|
| `web` | The app, REST, MCP, Stripe and Resend webhooks, the cron tick and jobs, the vault module | The KMS OIDC role, Stripe and Resend keys, the runtime, vault and cron database roles, the RPC signing key | Registrar credentials |
| `registrar` | A signed internal RPC that wraps `RegistrarAdapter`; the authoritative-nameserver DNS checks if UDP/53 is unavailable | The registrar credentials (Production scope only), Static IPs, the RPC verification key, a database role that can only count and record fuse events | The KMS role, Stripe keys, the vault, customer data |
| `cards` | hatchkind.com public pages | A read-only database role over a view of published cards | Cookies, any write role, any secret |

`web` and the jobs call `registrar` with a request signed over method, body hash, timestamp and nonce, verified inside a short window. `registrar` accepts only the commands the adapter needs, validates their arguments and enforces a velocity fuse (4.3b Egress). The search handlers in `web` call a lookup-only method. Cost of the split: one more internal interface that must be authenticated. Benefit: code execution in `web` reaches the registrar only through the allow-listed methods and their fuses, and does not yield the registrar key. Vercel Pro covers all three projects; Static IPs (USD 100 per project per month plus Private Data Transfer) apply to `registrar` only. The tick and its 100-cron limit belong to `web`.

#### Environments and what each may touch

Mosshatch's own environments are called deployment environments here; a customer's `dev`, `preview` and `prod` are project environments. Custom environments such as `staging` are available on Pro (https://vercel.com/docs/deployments/environments, last updated 2026-09-17, accessed 2026-09-29). Decision D-025 (Proposed default).

| | Local | Preview (per pull request) | Staging (custom environment `staging`) | Production |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hostname and passkey RP ID | `localhost` | `*.vercel.app` is never used for passkeys; fixture login only, a build flag production refuses to boot with | Its own hostname on a separate registrable domain, its own RP ID | `mosshatch.com` |
| Database | Local Postgres | Neon child of an anonymised `seed` branch, 1-day expiry, deleted on pull-request close by the Neon GitHub Action | Own Neon project, seeded data only | Production Neon project |
| Stripe | Test keys | Test keys, webhooks off | Test keys, own webhook endpoint | Live keys |
| Registrar | `MockRegistrar` | `MockRegistrar`; the `registrar` project gets no credentials in preview | OpenSRS Horizon | OpenSRS live through Static IPs |
| KMS | Development KEK (or a stub in unit tests) | Vault disabled: Nest routes return 503 | Staging KEK in the staging AWS account | Production KEKs in the production AWS account |
| Email | Log only | Log only | Resend test domain, internal recipients | Live |
| Jobs | `npm run tick` by hand | None (cron runs on production deployments only) | GitHub Actions schedule calls `/api/cron/tick` with the staging `CRON_SECRET` | Vercel cron |

Rules. (1) **Mode guard.** `livemode boolean not null` on `orders`, `payments`, `stripe_customers`, `webhook_events` and `domains`; a Stripe event whose `livemode` differs from the order is rejected and alerted. A boot guard, `assertModeConsistency()`, is called by the Stripe client constructor and the adapter factory: the process exits unless the Stripe key is `sk_test_` or `rk_test_` and the adapter `capabilities().mode` is `mock` or `sandbox`, or the key is `sk_live_` or `rk_live_`, the adapter is live and `VERCEL_ENV` is `production`. It also compares the database host and KMS alias with the deployment environment. `Money` and `Availability` from `MockRegistrar` carry `source: 'sample'`, and checkout refuses a sample amount when `livemode` is true. So no live key exists outside production, a preview never touches a real registrar or real money, and a start with a live key and the mock adapter fails to boot and `POST /orders` returns 503. (2) No Production-scoped variable is also scoped to Preview or Development; Production and Preview secrets are Sensitive and the team policy "Enforce Sensitive Environment Variables" is on. (3) Nothing is tested through staged production deployments. (4) The Neon-Vercel integration's automatic preview branching stays off, because a child branch inherits registrant PII and vault ciphertext. (5) Registrar credentials are never scoped to Preview or Development.

#### The store contract ("the creature is a view, never a vault")

- The Zustand store holds only: current view, selected domain id, camera goal, calm/sound flags, a list of `CreatureView` objects, and transient UI flags. `CreatureView` is `{ domain, mood, armored, sentence, charms: {service, label}[], secretCount: number, ageDays }`.
- Enforced in four layers, not by a name check alone. (1) Boundary: every server response that feeds the store is parsed with a `z.strictObject` schema from `packages/core/wire.ts`; unknown keys reject, and the `/nest` schema has no field that can carry a value. (2) Opaque type: plaintext exists only as `SecretValue`, a class with a private field whose `toString`, `toJSON` and `util.inspect.custom` return `[secret]` and whose only accessor is `expose(fn)`; it is defined in `src/reveal/` (browser) and `packages/vault` (server) and nowhere else. (3) Import boundaries: ESLint `no-restricted-imports` plus dependency-cruiser forbid `store/`, `world/` and `packages/core` from importing `reveal/`, forbid `world/` from importing anything but `packages/core` types and store selectors, allow only `packages/vault` to import the KMS client or call `createDecipheriv`, and allow only `reveal/` to fetch the reveal route. (4) Runtime: Zustand `persist` uses `partialize` to exactly `{calm, sound, rehideSeconds}` (unit-tested), and test builds wrap `set()` with a deep scan that throws on a canary or scanner-rule match. The compile-time test (no `string` field named like `value`, `secret`, `token`, `plaintext`) stays as a fifth, weaker check.
- After a reveal of a seeded canary the canary walk serializes the store, traverses the scene graph (names, `userData`, uniforms), scans the DOM outside `[data-reveal]`, `Object.keys(window)`, captured console output and `performance.getEntries()`, and asserts the canary is absent. After hide and a forced GC (CDP `HeapProfiler.collectGarbage`) a heap snapshot must show it retained by nothing in the store, `world/` or the React fiber tree; any retention that remains is reported honestly.
- The reveal component keeps the plaintext in a `useRef`-backed buffer scoped to that component, renders it into a single DOM node, and on hide (pointer or key release, timer, `blur`, `visibilitychange` to hidden, `pagehide`, `freeze`, route change, `beforeunload`) removes the node and drops the reference. JavaScript strings cannot be zeroed, so this is best effort and the plan says so plainly. It calls `fetch` directly (no shared client, no retry, no cache, no error reporter), decodes with `TextDecoder`, and discards `error.message` on failure; ESLint bans `console` and error-reporter imports in `reveal/`. Re-hide is 10 seconds by default; "Keep showing for 30 more seconds" is an explicit button per reveal with a total cap of 100 seconds (the WCAG 2.2.1 adjustment; one number in `localStorage`).
- The primary copy action copies the `mosshatch run` command, which holds no secret. Copying a value is on for `dev` and `preview` and off for `prod` until the person turns it on; the page never calls `readText()`, and says plainly that clipboard history and sync may keep the value.
- The reveal request is a POST with `Cache-Control: no-store`; the service worker (if any) has an explicit deny rule for `/api/*`. Every server route, webhooks, cron and the CSP report included, lives under `/api/` (only `/mcp` sits outside it), so that deny rule and the CSRF guard key on one prefix. Whether a sandboxed cross-origin reveal frame is worth its cost is evaluated in Phase 4 and, if adopted, gets its own decision.
- CI artifacts: `trace`, `video` and `screenshot` are off for any spec that reveals; artifact retention is 7 days; fixtures use only a documented `mh_test_CANARY_` prefix.

#### RegistrarAdapter: what the sketched interface is missing

The sketch in the brief covers the happy path. The product also needs these, so the interface in Phase 1 is a superset. Each is exercised by MockRegistrar and by the shared contract tests.

| Addition | Why the product needs it |
|---|---|
| `setNameservers(domain, ns[])` | "Changing nameservers" is a step-up action (`domain.nameservers.change`), so there must be a method to gate. It refuses when a DS record is present and the target DNS is unsigned (see DNS changes) |
| `setAutoRenew(domain, false)` only | Forces registrar-side auto-renew off; renewal is driven by Mosshatch so that price and consent are ours. The Overview auto-renew switch creates or revokes a `renewal_mandates` row (`mandate.sign`) and calls nothing at the registrar |
| No `setWhoisPrivacy` | WHOIS privacy is a read-only display: `getDomain` returns `privacyStatus` (`redacted_default`, `not_available`, `exposed`). There is no toggle and no paid add-on (D-020); copy per 4.7 |
| `restore(domain)` | Redemption-period restore for the "Sleeping" state. The OpenSRS API redeems `.com`, `.dev` and `.studio` only, so `capabilities().restore` is per extension and `.app`, `.ai` and `.io` restores go through OpenSRS support; the panel says so |
| `cancelTransfer(id)` | Rescue needs cancel for transfer-in where the provider has a cancel command. OpenSRS has no start, approve or cancel for outbound transfers, so the capability is false there and the Gate says who can end it |
| `getTransferOutStatus(domain)` | Transfer-out is initiated elsewhere; Traveling must be derived from the adapter |
| `listDomains(cursor)` | Inventory for the unattributed-change detector and for restore reconciliation |
| `getFundingStatus()` | Returns available `Money` (balance minus held) or `'unsupported'`; drives the balance alert and the sell gate |
| `pollEvents(since)` | Registrar-pushed verification and transfer events |
| `health()` | `{ status, maintenanceUntil? }`; feeds `registrar_unavailable` |
| `getDnssec(domain)`, `setDnssec(domain, ds[])` | Read for the preflight; `getDomain` returns `dsPresent`. DS changes are not offered to customers in v1 |
| `capabilities()` | `{ mode: 'mock'\|'sandbox'\|'live', dnsHosting, dnssec, webhooks, idempotentRegister, sandbox, authCodeModel, restore, funding, inventory, events }` so the app degrades explicitly instead of guessing |
| Typed errors | `RegistrarError { kind: 'maintenance'\|'unavailable'\|'insufficient_funds'\|'rate_limited'\|'rejected'\|'unknown', retryable, outcomeUnknown, code }`. `outcomeUnknown` (timeout after submit, or a worker that died after sending) forces the order into reconciliation by polling, never a blind retry |
| `Availability.kind` | `available`, `taken`, `premium`, `reserved`, `unknown` with an optional price; the UI must never show "available" for `unknown`, and `premium` renders as "not available" (D-031) |
| `Money` | `{ minor: bigint, currency, source: 'live'\|'sample' }`; no floating point anywhere in pricing |

Rules for every adapter: (1) `register` and `renew` are at-most-once per operation. A pre-check of `getDomain` and our ledger is not enough (two workers can both pass it), so the caller inserts an `order_operations` intent in the same transaction as the state change, before any network call, and an operation in state `sent` is never re-sent by any worker, only reconciled; (2) every response is normalized to Mosshatch's `DomainStatus`, and the raw registry status codes are passed through untouched for display; (3) no adapter method returns before the provider has actually accepted the request; anything asynchronous returns a handle that `get*` can poll, and a response that is accepted but not completed (OpenSRS `forced_pending`, code 250 or `async_reason`) is `accepted_pending`, never registered; (4) the OpenSRS adapter sends explicit parameters: `period` from a server-side per-extension table (`.ai` minimum 2; the default of 2 when omitted would bill a one-year order for two), `handle=process`, `auto_renew=0`, `f_whois_privacy=0` for gTLDs, `f_lock_domain=1`, a unique `reg_username` and a random `reg_password`; it sends `no_cache=1` on the checkout availability check; it refuses any quote with `is_registry_premium` (D-031); and after every register and renew it compares the upstream debit with `quote.wholesale`, and a difference above USD 0.01 pages and pauses sales of that extension.

#### MockRegistrar rules

`MockRegistrar` is the adapter for Phases 1 and 2, so it has fidelity rules. (1) Profiles: `mock:opensrs` reproduces the chosen provider's constraints from `capabilities()` (replace-all DNS without TTL or CAA, no idempotency key, one lookup per call, `.io` codes via a person, outbound transfer by emailed approval, `.ai` two-year minimum), so the app cannot depend on features the real provider lacks. (2) A fault-injection API for property tests: `timeoutAfterAccept`, `workerDeath`, `duplicateSubmit`, `sameNameTwoUsers`, `insufficientFunds` (`forced_pending`), `async250`, `registryMaintenance`, `renewDraft`, `defaultPeriod2`, `rateLimited`, `unknownAvailability`, with asynchronous completion on a controllable clock. States change only through the adapter, never through UI timers; the Arrival demo is the labelled exception. (3) Honesty: every mock `Money` and `Availability` carries `source: 'sample'` and the UI cannot drop the label; availability is generated from a seeded table that includes taken, reserved, premium and unknown names, so nothing is "free forever"; checkout refuses sample amounts when `livemode` is true. (4) When Phase 3 starts, real Horizon responses are recorded for each contract test and replayed in CI, so the mock's shapes are checked. (5) A nightly job runs the contract suite against Horizon with unique random names and 1-year terms (Horizon cannot register one name twice, even across resellers) and a monthly check of the test credit. (6) `docs/registrar-parity.md` lists behaviours the mock cannot model, and every contract test is tagged `both`, `mock-only` or `sandbox-only`; it is reviewed at each phase report.

#### Order state machine (register, renew, transfer-in)

Every transition is written to `order_events` with its cause. Nothing moves on a timer alone; timers only trigger a re-check against Stripe or the adapter. The payment path is **authorize, register, then capture** (decision D-002), which changes the brief's gate from "verified `payment_intent.succeeded`" to "verified `requires_capture`"; `payment_intent.succeeded` then marks the order paid. Checkout sheet rows and assent capture are D-024; each acceptance is a `consents` row.

```
draft ─▶ checkout_open ─▶ authorized ─▶ registering ─▶ registered ─▶ capturing ─▶ captured (paid)
            │    │             ▲            │  │  │                     │
            │    └▶ review_hold ┘            │  │  └▶ registrar_unavailable ─▶ registering | canceling
            ▼                                │  └▶ outcome_unknown ─▶ registered | registration_failed | canceling
   checkout_expired, payment_failed          ▼                                  capturing ─▶ capture_failed ─▶ captured
                                     registration_failed ─▶ canceling ─▶ voided (authorization cancelled, no fee kept)
captured ─▶ refund_pending ─▶ refunded | partially_refunded | refund_failed      overlay: dispute_open ─▶ dispute_won | dispute_lost
paid_before_registration ─▶ register-or-refund path (unexpected capture)
renewals: captured ─▶ renewing_upstream ─▶ renewed          transfers-in: captured at submission (below)
```

- **Why not the brief's order.** A refund does not return Stripe's processing fee (2.9% + USD 0.30), while cancelling an uncaptured authorization is free and is not reported to the card networks as fraud. Registration is the step that can fail, so it happens while the money is only held. Hosted Checkout supports this with `payment_intent_data.capture_method=manual` for cards, Apple Pay, Google Pay and Link; ACH does not support manual capture and is disabled. The default hold is seven days (Visa merchant-initiated 4 days 18 hours); an uncaptured PaymentIntent auto-cancels at seven days.
- **Guards the verifier found missing from the first design, now requirements.** (1) Fulfilment is driven by webhooks and also by the success page calling the same idempotent reconcile after checking that the Checkout Session belongs to the signed-in user's order. (2) Two payable Sessions for one order (a second tab, a retry) are prevented: the `authorized` transition must match the order's current session and attempt, expire the other Session (expiry fails if the customer just paid) and cancel any extra PaymentIntent. (3) An unexpected capture (`payment_intent.succeeded` while the order is `authorized` or `registering`, for example a Dashboard capture) goes to `paid_before_registration`, which registers or refunds, and the PaymentIntent is re-fetched before any cancel call. (4) After a 500 from Stripe on capture, `GET` the PaymentIntent and, if it is still `requires_capture`, capture with a **new** idempotency key (Stripe replays cached 500s under the same key). (5) `capture_before` is nullable on cards and absent for Link; use a fallback deadline of authorization plus four days and keep Link off until its window is documented. (6) The Session pins its payment methods explicitly so Dashboard changes cannot add auto-capture methods. (7) **Authorized-amount guard.** Stripe Tax computes tax in Checkout from an address entered after approval, so the authorized amount is never compared for equality with a pre-Checkout estimate. On `authorized` the order requires `status=requires_capture`, currency `usd`, `metadata.order_id` equal to the order, no open review, and `quote.subtotal_minor <= amount_capturable <= quote.subtotal_minor + tax_ceiling_minor`; any other value cancels the PaymentIntent and alerts. (8) Cancelling a Checkout-created PaymentIntent is documented inconsistently (the Session page says expire the Session instead), so the cancel step is sandbox-tested in Phase 2 before it is relied on.
- **Authorization-window rules.** `authorized` moves to `registering` only if `capture_before` minus now is at least 24 hours, else `canceling(reason=auth_window)`. `outcome_unknown` polls until `capture_before` minus 24 hours; if the name is ours, go to capture; if nothing is found at the deadline, cancel the PaymentIntent, tell the customer, and keep a `late_registration_watch` on the name for 14 days (if it appears as ours, enter `capture_failed`). After `registered`, capture at once. `capture_failed` (registered but unpaid) pages immediately: retry the same key for 6 hours on 5xx or timeout; if the PaymentIntent is cancelled or expired, charge a saved payment method off-session if one exists, otherwise email a pay link valid 7 days and keep the domain; unpaid at the deadline, delete inside the add-grace period and book the loss. An alert fires when any order is within 36 hours of `capture_before` and neither captured nor cancelled.
- **Quote.** `checkout_open` freezes a quote with an expiry (default 30 minutes). The expiry is never shown as a ticking timer; the sheet says "Price checked at 14:02. We check again when you pay." At fulfilment the quote is re-fetched; if the total has risen by any amount the order is voided and the person is told, so no one is ever charged more than the total shown. **Price guard (D-031):** before payment, a quote that is registry-premium (`is_registry_premium`), or that differs from the extension's standard price for register, renew or transfer-in, refuses the order with a plain message.
- **Registrar writes.** OpenSRS has no client reference on `SW_REGISTER`, and `GET_ORDERS_BY_DOMAIN` filters only by domain, date, status and type. So an order is claimed as ours only when all three hold: the `reg_username` generated for this order before the call (stored on the order) is the profile `GET DOMAIN` shows; a `type=new` order exists with `order_date` no earlier than `sent_at` minus 5 seconds; and the registrant contact matches. A domain that exists in our reseller account under any other profile resolves to `registration_failed(taken_by_other)`, never `registered`. A partial unique index on `orders(fqdn_ascii)` for register orders in `authorized`, `registering`, `outcome_unknown` or `registered` means a second customer who pays for the same name is voided at `authorized` with "someone else just bought this name".
- **`outcome_unknown`** is the dangerous one: any registrar write whose request was sent and whose answer was not seen, including a worker that died after sending. A sweeper moves `registering` orders whose operation has been `sent` for over 90 seconds (own target) to `outcome_unknown`. Resolution is by polling `getDomain` and the provider's order lookup on a backoff schedule under the claim rule above, up to the window in the authorization rules, then human review with the authorization untouched.
- **Registrar unavailable.** `RegistrarError.kind` `maintenance` or `unavailable` (or `health()` reporting a window) moves an authorized order to `registrar_unavailable`: the authorization is held, the order retries with backoff until `capture_before` minus 24 hours, then voids and emails. The sentence is "Registrations are paused while our registrar is in maintenance. Nothing was charged." A `maintenance_windows` table, fed daily from the upstream status page and by hand, is read by the adapter. Existing domains cannot fail over: CentralNic is a fallback for new registrations and per-extension routing (D-001), and leaving OpenSRS is an ICANN transfer with each registrant's code and approval.
- **Empty prepaid balance.** A negative OpenSRS balance is a material breach (MSA 12.1) and a depleted one blocks all services (MSA 12.7); a forced-pending order waits for a top-up. The float is sized in 4.2 (Working capital). Sell gate: `POST /orders` and the renewal scheduler refuse ("Registration is paused for a short while. Nothing was charged.") when available funds minus reserved wholesale fall below USD 250 (own target), where reserved is orders in `authorized` or `registering`, renewals already charged and not yet renewed upstream, and renewals due within 14 days; renewal reservations are senior to new registrations. A daily cap on registrations is a database limit (`flags`). On `registration_failed`, void or customer cancel, the adapter runs `PROCESS_PENDING` cancel for every upstream order found for the domain with status pending or waiting, confirms it, and only then cancels the PaymentIntent; an upstream order that completes later is booked as a loss and alarmed.
- **Agent-approved purchase.** An agent's `register.propose` or `renew.propose` creates an `agent_requests` row in one transaction under a per-user advisory lock: an idempotent insert on (binding, request hash) while pending; a reservation against the binding (`reserved_minor + spent_minor + quoted <= spend_cap_minor`, else 403 `cap_exceeded`); pending limits of 3 per binding and 10 per user and 5 proposals an hour per binding (own targets, the MCP dossier's baseline); expiry of 72 hours (own target), never later than the binding's own expiry. Approval is the passkey step-up `agent.purchase.approve`, bound to request id, binding, domain, years, subtotal, tax ceiling, currency, quote hash and terms hash. It sets `pending` to `approved` only if the quote is unchanged, moves the reservation to spent, and creates the order in `checkout_open` with one hosted Checkout Session. **It binds no payment method and charges nothing.** The person pays on Stripe next; the Checkout URL goes only to the approving cookie session, is never stored, and is never returned to the agent. Off-session charging of a stored card is not used for agent purchases in v1 (it also applies to `renew.propose`). The creature stays an egg until the person pays and registration succeeds. The agent sees a state derived from the order (`approved_awaiting_payment`, `payment_authorized`, `registering`, `registered`) and never "paid" before `registered`. A price change before approval, or a total above the tax ceiling at authorization, voids the request, releases the reservation and lets the agent re-propose. Decline, expiry, void and failure each release the reservation; a nightly check compares `reserved_minor` with the sum of pending quotes.
- **Renewals** reuse the machine but charge first: an off-session PaymentIntent at C = E-10 days, captured immediately because the mandate is the authorization (notices at E-43, E-32, C-8 and E+1, D-008), then the upstream renewal, because renewals are final at the registrar. `renewing_upstream` retries every 15 minutes for 24 hours, then hourly to E-1 (own targets), running `PROCESS_PENDING` cancel on any draft renewal before each retry and paging on the first failure. Refund only if the customer asks or the name is still unrenewed at E-1. If a customer with a paid renewal lapses because of a Mosshatch failure (balance, outage, stuck job), Mosshatch renews at its own cost at once (D-008).
- **Transfers-in** are not held: the upstream debits the +1 year at submit and a transfer can take about two weeks, longer than any authorization. The order is captured at submission and refunded on failure, which loses Stripe's fee on a failed transfer (4.2 carries that in the model).
- **Review, refund and dispute.** `review_hold` waits on `review.closed`. `captured` moves to `refund_pending` on a refund; the processing fee is not returned. `charge.dispute.created` or `radar.early_fraud_warning.created` sets the account to `risk_state = review` (no new order above USD 50 wholesale, own target; renewal charges wait for a manual look) and assembles the evidence pack. A payment dispute never blocks unlock or code release (Transfer Policy I.A.5.4). The first two orders of an account and any order above USD 100 request 3-D Secure (`request_three_d_secure=any`; own targets). An account in its first 30 days is limited to USD 300 of wholesale exposure in flight and 5 registrations a day (own targets), which bounds what stolen cards can drain from the prepaid balance before a dispute arrives. Refunds are capped at 3 per account per 30 days (own target) and go only to domains with no DNS records and no connections.
- **Stripe idempotency keys** follow the Stripe dossier: customer `cust:{user}`; Session `cs:{order}:{attempt}`; capture `cap:{order}`; cancel `cancel:{order}`; expire `expire:{order}:{attempt}`; refund `refund:{order}:{seq}`; off-session renewal `renew:{domain}:{term_start}:{try}` (`try` increments only after a determined failure). Keys live 24 hours, so the durable guards are ours: unique `orders.stripe_checkout_session_id` and `stripe_payment_intent_id`, a `request_hash` stored beside `orders.idempotency_key` (the same key with different parameters returns 409), and a partial unique index on (domain, kind, target expiry year) for non-terminal renew and restore orders, so the scheduled charge and a "Renew now" click produce one PaymentIntent. Before reusing a renewal try, search Stripe by `metadata.renewal_id`. The stripe-node package is pinned and the API version is set explicitly on both the client and the webhook endpoint.
- **Mode consistency** is enforced at boot and on every event; see Environments.

#### Jobs

A `jobs` table plus a one-minute cron tick. A tick claims work with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, runs each job with a deadline, records attempts with exponential backoff, and dead-letters after `max_attempts`. Webhook handlers enqueue and then call the tick opportunistically (`waitUntil`) so latency is normally seconds rather than a minute. Jobs are at-least-once; registrar writes are at-most-once through `order_operations`. A claim sets `locked_until` to now plus the job's maximum runtime plus 30 seconds and an `attempt_id`; completion is `UPDATE ... WHERE id = $1 AND attempt_id = $2`, and registrar and Stripe calls record the `attempt_id`, so a job that outlived its lease cannot commit. `dedupe_key` is unique only while a job is queued or running. Payloads carry ids only: never secrets, and never an authorization code.

| Kind | Cadence or trigger | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Priority 0 (money)** | | |
| `order.fulfil`, `order.reconcile` | Webhook or tick; reconcile every 5 minutes for open orders older than 2 minutes | Move orders; re-fetch Stripe and the adapter |
| `action.execute` | After a step-up commit; one queued or running job per domain | Registrar effects of `domain.unlock`, `domain.nameservers.change`, `domain.transfer_out`: pre-read state, send only if it differs, re-read to confirm, reconcile timeouts |
| `renewal.charge`, `refund.retry` | C = E-10; retries | Off-session renewal charge; refunds |
| `webhook.replay` | On failure | Replay stored provider events |
| `stripe.reconcile` | Daily | Last 30 days of events and PaymentIntents against `orders` |
| **Priority 1** | | |
| `domain.sync`, `transfer.poll` | Per domain; transfers every 5 minutes | Adapter truth into `domains`; `GET_TRANSFERS_AWAY` for pending statuses |
| `dns.verify`, `dns.drift`, `dns.dangling_scan` | After a write; hourly for domains with a step-up in the last 30 days and daily otherwise; weekly | Shedding checks; drift against the registrar's live records; dangling targets |
| `renewal.notice`, `expiry.notice`, `expiry.lastchance`, `price_change.notice` | E-43, E-32, C-8, E+1; ICANN expiry notices regardless; E+7, E+21, E+35; 21 days before an affected charge | Notices from the `notices` ledger. A wholesale change that affects a renewal is emailed as soon as known and repeated 21 days before the charge; a flat-fee change is announced at least 60 days ahead as an informational notice |
| `quote.refresh` | Daily | Renewal quote in the panel; a change triggers the price-change notice |
| `contact.verify`, `registration_data.reminder` | Day 10 reminder, day 15 upstream `clientHold` request, bounces re-verify within 15 days; annual | Registrant verification and the Registration Data Reminder |
| `registrar.reconcile`, `registrar.posture`, `registrar.balance` | Daily; nightly; hourly | Inventory diff and the unattributed-change detector (writes `reconciliation_findings`; the diff covers lock, nameservers, DS, contact email hash, `auto_renew`, `let_expire` and privacy state); per-domain posture; funding |
| `agent_request.expire`, `email.send` | Sweeper; on demand | Expire requests and release reservations; mail |
| `audit.anchor`, `audit.verify`, `audit.kms_reconcile` | Daily | Anchor chain heads; verify chains; match CloudTrail Decrypt events to audit rows |
| `account.close`, `account.export`, `retention.purge` | On request; daily | See Account closure |
| `heartbeat` | Every tick | Dead-man's switch |

Priority 0 kinds spend the first 60% of a tick's time budget; per-kind concurrency caps (`domain.sync` 5, `dns.verify` 3) are own targets. A money job that dead-letters pages the operator and applies a default: `order.fulfil` cancels the PaymentIntent if the authorization deadline is near, and `renewal.charge` keeps retrying to E-1. `GET /api/health/ticks` returns the age of the last completed tick; an external monitor pages at 3 minutes, and a second trigger from an external scheduler calls the tick endpoint. An alarm fires when the oldest runnable priority 0 job is older than 60 seconds. Vercel cron is best-effort (duplicated or missed runs are possible and failed runs are not retried), so correctness lives in the table: every job is safe to run twice and claimable by any tick.

#### Creature state derivation (pure function in `packages/core`)

`deriveCreatureState(snapshot, jobs, orders, now)` returns `{ mood, armored, sentence, reason, action?, asOf, source }`. Precedence, highest first:

1. **egg**: an order for this name is authorized, registering or `registrar_unavailable`, or the adapter reports the domain as pending registration.
2. **traveling** (owner-started): a non-terminal transfer exists and a committed `domain.transfer_out` action or a Rescue order for this domain exists in the last 7 days, or the adapter reports `pendingTransfer` for a transfer-in we started.
3. **sleeping**: adapter status is expired, in auto-renew grace, redemption, or pending delete. The sentence states which stage and the date the next stage begins. Registry stages come from registry data; the auction date and the expired-nameserver switch come from a per-registrar policy profile labelled "Tucows policy" (`tld_policy`), not from registry data. When `GET DOMAIN` returns not found for a domain we own, the adapter falls back to `GET_DELETED_DOMAINS` and maps it to redemption with the restore price instead of a sync error. The panel warns that email at the domain stops working and that other services may send password resets to it.
4. **needs attention**: one specific blocking item, in this order: an unrequested transfer (a non-terminal transfer-away with no matching committed action: "A transfer to {gaining registrar} started {time}. You did not ask for it. Decline it in the email from OpenSRS, or press Stop this transfer."), payment failed on a renewal, registrant email unverified with the verification deadline near or passed, registry or registrar hold status, a dispute lock, missing verification record for a wired service, a dangling record, sync error older than 15 minutes (own target; "We cannot confirm this domain's state right now").
5. **shedding**: a DNS write intent that is not yet visible at the authoritative nameservers.
6. **drowsy**: inside the renewal window (default 30 days) with no blocking item.
7. **thriving**: none of the above.

`armored` is an independent overlay driven by the lock flag; registry-imposed locks (for example a post-registration or post-transfer lock) show the plate with a different sentence ("Locked by the registry until 12 Nov") because the person cannot toggle them.

Every result carries `asOf` and `source` (`adapter`, `job`, `order`); the panel prints "Confirmed by the registrar 4 minutes ago". If `synced_at` is older than the staleness bound (15 minutes, own target), the sentence says the state could not be confirmed instead of guessing.

#### Secrets: envelope encryption design (Phase 4)

- **Key service.** AWS KMS, symmetric `SYMMETRIC_DEFAULT` keys, single Region us-east-1 (Vercel `iad1`; `AWS_REGION` is set explicitly on every function and in the client, because Vercel notes it can change under failover). One set of keys per deployment environment (production, staging, development). Inside production there are two KEKs and two IAM roles: `vault-prod` for customers' `prod` secrets and `vault-nonprod` for their `dev` and `preview` secrets. The key policy requires the matching key for the `env` value in the encryption context, and the class is derived from the stored `secrets.env`, never from the request. Both roles trust the same OIDC `sub`, so the split protects against logic bugs and scope confusion in the dev, preview, CLI and agent paths (a `prod` wrapped key sent to `vault-nonprod` gets AccessDenied); it does not stop an attacker with code execution in `web`, who can assume both. Vercel functions authenticate by OIDC federation (`AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity`); there are no static AWS keys anywhere. The role trust policy is pinned to the Vercel Team issuer, to `sub = owner:<team>:project:web:environment:production`, and to a random 32-byte custom audience (a Sensitive variable, registered as the IAM provider's only audience) so that a leaked `x-vercel-oidc-token` header or a reused team slug is not enough. The role's maximum session is 3,600 seconds and requests use `durationSeconds` of 900. A team or project rename needs a paired trust-policy change (a team rename also needs a new IAM provider), and CloudTrail alarms fire on `AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity` with an unknown `sub` or `aud`. The role may call only `GenerateDataKey` and `Decrypt` on its KEK ARN (`KeyId` always passed, encryption-context conditions), with explicit denies on `CreateGrant`, `PutKeyPolicy`, `ScheduleKeyDeletion` and `DisableKey`; re-wrap runs under a separate operator role that Vercel cannot assume. The `x-vercel-oidc-token` header is a live two-hour credential and is never logged or forwarded to error reports.
- **Per-secret data key.** Each secret write calls `GenerateDataKey` (AES_256) with `EncryptionContext = { app, env, owner_id, secret_id }`, using opaque ids only because the context is logged in plaintext in CloudTrail. The value (at most 16 KiB, enforced at the API with 413) is encrypted locally with AES-256-GCM using a fresh random 96-bit nonce, `authTagLength: 16` on both sides, and AAD that is the length-prefixed concatenation of `format_version || alg_id || domain_id || secret_id || name || env || version` (never the KEK ARN, so re-wrap keeps it valid). The name is in the AAD so a database writer cannot swap two secrets' values. One data key encrypts one value, so NIST's 2^32 invocation limit is irrelevant. The wrapped key, nonce, ciphertext and 16-byte tag go to `secret_versions`; the plaintext data key is zeroed after use (`Buffer.alloc`, not `allocUnsafe`). Nothing is cached. `secrets.pointer_mac` is a KMS HMAC (`GenerateMac`, checked with `VerifyMac` on every read) over `secret_id || name || env || current_version`, so a database writer cannot re-point a secret at an older still-decryptable version; the MAC key is one of the non-vault production keys in Loss radius.
- **Simpler variant to decide in Phase 4.** For values up to 4,096 bytes, direct `kms:Encrypt` and `Decrypt` with the same encryption context costs the same number of KMS requests as `GenerateDataKey` plus `Decrypt`, removes local crypto handling and keeps `ReEncrypt`, at the price of a hard 4 KB ceiling and plaintext travelling to KMS over TLS. The brief specifies a per-secret data key, so that stays the default.
- **Two read paths (D-019).** (1) Web session: every reveal is a `secret.reveal` step-up, one secret per assertion. (2) Bearer binding: a binding created by step-up (`agent.token.create`, or `device.approve` for the CLI) with `secrets.read:<domain>:<env>` reads that domain and environment through `POST /domains/{fqdn}/secrets/{env}/read` without a per-read ceremony (4.5): `:prod` must be named explicitly, every read is audited, rate limited by token id and summarised by email, and a missing scope is 403, never an empty list. Reveal sequence for path 1: transaction T1 consumes the challenge, verifies the assertion, inserts the audit row `secret.reveal.authorized` and commits, and if the insert fails nothing is decrypted; then KMS `Decrypt` runs outside any transaction (3-second timeout, no retry loop); then T2 inserts `secret.reveal.released` or `.failed`, and a T2 failure raises an alert instead of blocking. Path 2 writes one audit row per secret id before decrypting, and if that write fails no value is returned. The notification email is detection, not prevention.
- **Fail closed and limits.** Any KMS error returns 503 `vault_unavailable` with a request id and no detail; the CLI exits 75; no path decrypts without KMS. A read of at most 100 secrets per request, concurrency 10 and a p95 budget of 300 ms for 20 secrets are own targets measured in Phase 4 from an `iad1` function. Customers are told not to use `mosshatch run` as the only source of production configuration; for production, `pull --out` into the host's own secret store.
- **Third-party credentials for recipes.** The Vercel, Neon and Resend credentials a recipe uses live in `connection_credentials`, encrypted through the same vault path (AAD adds `connection_id || service`). Only least-scope credentials are accepted (a Vercel project or team token, never a full-account token; a Neon project-scoped key; for Resend a `sending_access` key). No route, MCP tool or CLI command ever returns one; only `recipe.apply` and `connection.check` jobs inside the vault module decrypt them. They are destroyed on disconnect. Revoking bindings stops agent use without touching them. Holding these carries liability and partner-terms questions for counsel (4.7).
- **Rotation and re-wrap.** `ReEncrypt` re-wraps stored data keys (and can change the encryption context) server-side without touching values. Automatic KEK rotation adds a surcharge (USD 1 per month after each of the first two rotations), so the four vault KEKs cost about USD 4 a month and about USD 12 by year three with rotation (D-004, D-026).
- **Blast radius, stated plainly.** Anyone who can run code in the production `web` project, including an operator with production deploy rights, can call `Decrypt` for any wrapped key they can read. KMS does not prevent that; it gives revocation, an independent audit trail (CloudTrail) and makes a database-only leak useless. Vercel (TLS termination, function memory) and AWS (data keys in KMS) can technically observe plaintext in normal operation; a value on screen can be seen by extensions, clipboard managers and screen capture; a value returned to an agent or printed by the CLI leaves Mosshatch's control; deleted secrets remain in encrypted database history for the retention window; and loss of the key is loss of every secret (Loss radius). Hardening: Vercel Deployment Policies restrict which repositories and deployment mechanisms can deploy to production; human IAM principals are denied `kms:Decrypt` in the key policy; the job `audit.kms_reconcile` joins CloudTrail Decrypt events (about 5 minutes late, and the trail must not exclude KMS events) to audit rows and alerts on any Decrypt with no matching row; optional Static IPs enable an `aws:SourceIp` condition; alarms on Decrypt volume and unexpected principals. The strongest structural option is to move the Decrypt-capable identity into its own small Vercel project with a minimal dependency set; it is deferred to Phase 4 and recorded under D-026 with its cost (one more project, an authenticated internal path, duplicated authorization logic). **Emergency stop:** remove the trust policy or identity provider, or `DisableKey` (which takes down every customer's reveals at once); a key-policy Deny on `kms:EncryptionContext:owner_id` blocks one tenant without an outage. AWS's "revoke active sessions" does not deny sessions assumed after the revocation, so it is not the primary stop.
- **Deletion.** Deleting a secret nulls `ciphertext` and `wrapped_dek` at once and sets `destroyed_at`; encrypted copies persist in Neon point-in-time history for up to the retention window (7 days on Launch, 30 on Scale) and the delete dialog says so. Key destruction is the only real delete.
- **Not verifiable without a live account:** KMS latency from Vercel (benchmark from an `iad1` function in Phase 4), whether Vercel can revoke an issued OIDC token, team-slug reuse behaviour (a Vercel support question in Phase 2), and the `sub` of a build token (production builds probably hold the same OIDC identity as production functions; decode a throwaway production build's token in Phase 4).

#### Loss radius

Losing the production KEK, or the AWS account that holds it, permanently destroys every customer secret. The Nest is not a backup of record and the Terms say so (D-026, Proposed default). Controls: (1) each production KEK is a multi-Region key with a replica in us-east-2 (USD 1 a month per replica, so USD 2 for the two production KEKs; not in the four-key figure), and the application retries `Decrypt` against the replica on a regional failure. (2) The key policy denies `kms:ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `kms:DisableKey` and `kms:PutKeyPolicy` to every principal except a break-glass role that needs hardware-key MFA (the emergency stop), and scheduled deletion always uses the 30-day window. (3) Alarms on the CloudTrail events `ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `DisableKey`, `PutKeyPolicy` and `CreateGrant`. (4) AWS Organizations with separate production, staging and log-archive accounts; the root user on a hardware key with no access keys; CloudTrail with log-file validation delivered to the log-archive bucket with Object Lock, KMS events never excluded. (5) A monthly drill restores last night's off-Neon backup into a scratch project and decrypts a canary secret. (6) Production also holds three non-vault keys, about USD 3 a month: a PII key (for `contacts`), an HMAC key (audit chain and secret pointers) and a backup key (in the log-archive account); staging and development reuse their single KEK with distinct encryption-context values. (7) A second-cloud KEK (a multi-keyring wrap) is a later option. All in: four vault KEKs plus three other keys is seven keys, about USD 7 a month before rotation, and about USD 9 with the two replicas. The AWS account is needed from Phase 2, not Phase 4, because `contacts` are field-encrypted and the audit anchor lives there.

#### Egress to the registrar

OpenSRS accepts live API calls only from allow-listed addresses: five rules by default (more on request to OpenSRS Support), /25 to /32; changes take up to 15 minutes. Vercel functions have no fixed egress by default. Vercel Static IPs are one address pair per region, shared with a small group of other customers, cost USD 100 per project per month plus Private Data Transfer from USD 0.15 per GB, and route all outbound traffic of that project including builds through those addresses (https://vercel.com/docs/networking/static-ips, last updated 2026-06-30, accessed 2026-09-29). They apply to every environment of the project, so they cannot be isolated to production. The allow-list is therefore a narrowing control, not a second factor: a leaked key is harder to use from elsewhere but not useless, and the allow-list protects only against a key that leaks outside Vercel, not against code running in the project, including preview deployments. Decision D-005 (recommendation: Static IPs for simplicity, with a gateway named as the stronger option).

- **Recommended path.** Put every registrar-calling function in the `registrar` project (Deployment topology): registrar credentials set for Production only and never in preview, the project holds adapter code only, it shares no project with the vault, and it stays small because all its traffic is metered. The Horizon sandbox needs no allow-list, so the decision is needed before the live rehearsal, not before Phases 1 to 3.
- **Compensating controls, because the key is one reseller-wide key that cannot be scoped** (sub-users cannot authenticate to the API): `registrar` accepts only the commands the adapter needs (`LOOKUP`, `GET_PRICE`, `SW_REGISTER`, `RENEW`, `GET`, `MODIFY` for status, lock and nameservers, the DNS zone commands, `GET_TRANSFERS_*`, `GET_BALANCE`, `PROCESS_PENDING`) with argument validation, and a velocity fuse across all customers (own targets: authorization-code issue 5 an hour, unlock 10, nameserver change 20, contact change 10; raising a limit is a config change that needs a passkey). The unattributed-change detector (Jobs, Gate) is the detective control.
- **Stronger alternative.** A small self-run gateway on a dedicated /32 that holds the registrar credentials, so they are never in Vercel, and enforces the same allow-list and fuse outside the Vercel runtime. Vercel Secure Compute (Enterprise) is another option. Both cost more to run.
- **Failover.** Register both the Static IP pair and the fallback gateway address in the OpenSRS IP rules (five are allowed) so failover needs no allow-list change, and test it quarterly.

#### Availability search at scale

- RDAP and DNS can only prove a name is **taken**. An RDAP 404 or a DNS NXDOMAIN also occurs for registry-reserved names (observed live for `example.dev`, `example.app`, `example.studio`) and NXDOMAIN occurs for registered names on hold. Only the registrar's EPP-backed check is authoritative, and OpenSRS mandates its own lookup (rate-limited, and "excessive use may result in a fee"). Premium names look taken unless the premium tier is enabled on the account; it is not (D-031).
- None of the three operators behind the six extensions (Verisign, Google Registry, Identity Digital) publishes a numeric RDAP limit, and all three restrict high-volume automated use (Google bans it outright; Verisign and Identity Digital restrict using the data to drive it). `.io` has no IANA bootstrap entry and is answered by Identity Digital's host without documentation. Identity Digital answered HTTP 429 to 5 of 13 test requests. RDAP therefore stays off the per-keystroke path and is never used to declare a name available; it is at most an optional low-volume "taken" confirmer for settled input.
- Design (D-017): debounce to settled input; singleflight per label; one server call fanning out to the six extensions. The Find view shows one egg per **available** extension, so the registrar's check runs for each extension on every settled query (up to six lookups; OpenSRS accepts one name per call and may charge for excessive use), trimmed by a cache ("taken" for hours, "not proven taken" for at most five minutes; SOA negative TTLs are 300 to 3,600 seconds). The chip price comes from a quote call, and the authoritative check runs again at checkout with `no_cache=1`. ASCII labels only in the first release (IDN and homograph handling later). Search text is not stored beyond the cache.
- **Limits (own targets, tuned after OpenSRS answers).** Anonymous: 30 searches per 10 minutes per IP and 100 an hour per /24, and a self-hosted proof-of-work challenge after 20 searches in a session. Signed-in verified users: 300 an hour. `tlds` must be a subset of the six launch extensions (400 otherwise); `name` is ASCII, at most 63 characters, lowercased and trimmed before it becomes a cache key. The global lookup budget is split 70% anonymous search and 30% checkout, renewals and agents, and the checkout check can never be starved. A daily ceiling sits at 50% of the fee threshold agreed in writing with OpenSRS, with an alarm at half the ceiling. When the anonymous budget is spent the Find view shows cached prices with "availability unconfirmed" chips (kind `unknown`, no egg). Abuse counters store an HMAC of the IP with a 24-hour TTL, not search text. Search handlers run in `web` and call a lookup-only method of `registrar`.
- **Front-running commitments to publish:** never register, reserve or hold a name because it was searched; never sell or share search data; no durable storage of search text; a plain-language notice at the search box; name every party that sees queries. (ICANN SAC 022 and 024 found no proven case; the one documented registrar practice was Network Solutions' four-day hold on searched names, settled in 2009.)

#### DNS changes and the Shedding state

A registrar API "success" is not propagation (Porkbun explicitly accepts writes for zones nobody queries; Route 53 only claims INSYNC across its own servers). Shedding therefore lasts until, in order: (a) the adapter accepted the write; (b) every authoritative nameserver answers the desired record set (recursion off, authoritative flag set); (c) sampled Cloudflare and Google DNS-over-HTTPS answers match on N = 3 consecutive samples 30 seconds apart (own target). Remaining TTL is not a countdown (the same resolver returned 206, 2823, 2909, 2700 and 3230 seconds for one name), so it is used only as an upper bound: `max(old TTL, SOA negative TTL)`. Never probe a name at public resolvers before it is written (negative caching, RFC 2308). For `.dev` and `.app`, which are HSTS-preloaded, "spread" also requires a valid certificate. Whether Vercel functions may send raw UDP/53 is undocumented; if not, step (b) runs in the `registrar` project or is replaced by DoH-to-authoritative checks. When a domain's nameservers are not the registrar's, the DNS tab is read-only and says where DNS is hosted.

- **Write safety.** OpenSRS zone writes are replace-all (whether they overwrite the whole zone or per record type is undocumented, so the adapter must be correct under either). Every DNS write takes a `pg_advisory_xact_lock` on the domain, reads the live zone, computes the desired set, refuses when the change deletes more than 5 records or removes any MX, TXT or SRV record the intent does not name, writes, reads back and compares a hash, and stores the pre-write zone in `dns_snapshots` (30 days) with a one-click rollback. TXT values above 254 characters are split or rejected with a plain message. Whole-zone versus per-type semantics are settled by a Horizon test before Phase 3 exit, and the contract suite runs the adapter against `MockRegistrar` in both modes.
- **Sensitive records.** An agent's or a recipe's change to a sensitive record is a pending approval, `dns.sensitive.approve` (D-011). Classification is by owner name first: any label starting with an underscore at any position (`_dmarc`, `*._domainkey`, `_acme-challenge`, `_mta-sts`, `_smtp._tls`), the apex, `www`, any wildcard, and the hosts `autoconfig`, `autodiscover` and `mta-sts`; then by type: MX, NS, DS, SRV, apex A, AAAA and CNAME; the TXT prefixes `v=spf1`, `v=DMARC1`, `v=DKIM1` and provider verification tokens are only a backstop, because `v=` is optional in DKIM records. The classification is computed server-side on the difference between a fresh adapter read and the desired zone, re-checked at commit, and the assertion binds the hash of the normalized before and after record sets. It applies only when `dns_hosted_here` is true. Residual risk: this design gates agents and recipes, not a human cookie session, so a stolen session can still edit these records; that gap is carried in the threat model (4.6, D-011).
- **DNSSEC.** A signed domain whose nameservers change to unsigned DNS stops resolving on validating resolvers, and a transfer-in of a signed domain carries its DS over. `setNameservers` and `startTransferIn` therefore refuse when `dsPresent` and the target DNS is unsigned ("Your domain has DNSSEC records that would make it unreachable. Remove them first."), and Rescue reads RDAP `secureDNS.delegationSigned` at step 1. DS records appear in the unattributed-change diff. DNSSEC is not advertised until the OpenSRS fee conflict is answered (4.1 question).
- **Dangling records.** `dns.dangling_scan` resolves the target of every CNAME, A or ALIAS a recipe created and probes it with the provider fingerprint kept in the versioned recipe registry (an NXDOMAIN target, or the provider's not-found response); a recipe may not ship without a fingerprint. A hit sets needs attention ("This record points to a service that no longer answers. Remove it or attach it again."). Disconnecting a service deletes the recipe's CNAME, A and verification TXT records after showing the plan, instead of only listing them. The panel warns when a domain's own nameservers sit under a domain that expires within 60 days.

#### The Gate with a wholesale upstream

The brief's Gate ceremony (passkey step-up, cooling-off, cancel any time) meets the upstream's real mechanics. With OpenSRS there is no API to start or approve an outbound transfer: the person unlocks the name and takes the authorization code from Mosshatch, the gaining registrar starts the transfer, and OpenSRS emails the registrant a link to approve or decline, with silence for five days counting as approval. That email is the last checkpoint and sits outside the passkey gate; the control Mosshatch owns is the passkey on unlock and code issue, backed by a notice with a freeze link on every unlock and code issue. Mosshatch therefore (1) performs unlock and code issue behind the step-up, (2) shows the code once, (3) derives Traveling from `GET_TRANSFERS_AWAY` and the domain's `transfer_away_in_progress` flag, never from a timer, and (4) treats "cancel" as re-locking and issuing a new code until the code is used, and afterwards as declining in OpenSRS's email, which the panel says plainly. The optional security delay (D-009) is an owner-set setting, off by default, kept inside five calendar days, and applied alike to contact and nameserver changes (Transfer Policy I.A.5.3). `.io` codes are issued by OpenSRS support, so the `.io` Gate is a request that waits on a person. The transfer timing copy is "A transfer can take several days and sometimes about two weeks. It stays Traveling until the registry confirms." (for `.ai` and `.io`: "The registry sets the timing and we cannot predict it."), never "instant", never a fixed cap; `tld_policy` stores the registry window (5 calendar days for `.com`, `.dev`, `.app`, `.studio`) apart from the longest seen through OpenSRS (about two weeks). Each of the open points is a written question to OpenSRS (4.1).

- **(5) The OpenSRS end-user management interface is a second control plane.** Every registrant profile can sign in to it, see the current authorization code and change it. It is switched off before the first live registration (Reseller Control Panel, Branding, End-user management interface: access off with a redirect to the Mosshatch domain page; if that is refused, auth codes hidden and password resets blocked). `reg_password` is 20 random characters per registration, stored field-encrypted under the PII key and never shown or emailed. `SEND_AUTHCODE` is never called. Mosshatch issues its own code (`MODIFY domain_auth_info` to a fresh random value) at each unlock, re-randomises it at re-lock and after 24 hours, and never reads back a stored code. The nightly `registrar.posture` job asserts, for every domain, `auto_renew=0`, the expected lock state and privacy state, and that a login for a Horizon test profile redirects.
- **(6) Hostile transfer-out detection and stop.** `transfer.poll` runs every 5 minutes: one `GET_TRANSFERS_AWAY` for statuses `pending_admin`, `pending_owner` and `pending_registry` with `req_from` yesterday, plus a mailbox rule on the reseller technical-contact address for OpenSRS's transfer-request notice. A hit with no matching committed action pages the operator and emails the customer within 10 minutes. Runbook "Stop a hostile transfer": the operator asks Tucows support for a NACK under Transfer Policy I.A.3.7.1 (fraud) inside the pending window; the Stop button re-locks, re-randomises the code and opens that ticket; the panel says only the owner's decline link or Tucows can end a pending transfer. Whether a reseller can place a registrar hold is a written question. A payment dispute never blocks unlock or code release (I.A.5.4).
- **(7) Freeze.** The "this was not me" link (a GET shows a confirm page; a POST with a single-use token acts, 4.5) locks the domain, re-randomises the code, revokes sessions and pauses (does not delete) bindings; it never deletes passkeys, and it opens the stop ticket when a transfer is pending. A dispute lock (UDRP, registrar instruction only) freezes contact edits and transfer-out and raises a 24x7 alert.

#### Account closure, export and erasure

Designed in Phase 2, built in Phases 3 and 4, because the chain format, PII columns and export shape are fixed in Phase 2 (D-030, Proposed default).

- **States** `active -> closing -> closed -> purged`. `closing` blocks new orders, offers transfer-out first for every domain (deletion within 45 days, C-28), and revokes all sessions, bindings and mandates.
- **Audit stays pseudonymous.** `actor_id` and `resource_id` are opaque ids; no email, name, contact field or domain string goes into `detail` (domain ids, not names). Erasure deletes the identifying tables (`users` PII, `contacts`, notification addresses) and the per-user audit key, and leaves the chain intact. A closed account's chain is sealed by a `chain.closed` row whose hash goes to the external anchor; deleting a sealed chain after the longest applicable retention needs a separate `retention` role and writes a tombstone to the anchor store, never the app role.
- **Restore safety.** An `erasure_ledger` (hashed user id, timestamp) lives outside the database; every point-in-time restore or snapshot restore ends with "re-run purge from the ledger". The Privacy Notice says erased data may persist in database history for up to the restore window (7 days Launch, 30 days Scale) and in Resend logs for 30 days.
- **Export.** `account.export` (requested from a signed-in session; the link needs a signed-in session too) produces JSON and CSV of profile, domains, DNS records, orders and receipts, the account's audit trail, and secret names and version metadata. Secret values are available only through the CLI `pull` or a step-up reveal. Delivered as an expiring signed link within 30 days of the request.
- **Third parties.** Delete the Stripe customer, remove the Resend contact, unpublish the card and invalidate its CDN cache. OpenSRS keeps registration and payment records for 3 years (MSA 3.9), and the Privacy Notice says so.
- **Released names.** A domain that leaves us (registry deletion, completed transfer-out, auction sale, refund deletion, closure, UDRP outcome) gets `released_at` in one transaction on adapter confirmation: bindings scoped to it are revoked, pending requests cancelled, mandates revoked, cards unpublished, connections ended, queued jobs cancelled, and secret ciphertext destroyed after a 30-day hold during which only the owner can export by domain id with a step-up. Nothing is released while the name can still be restored. Re-registration inserts a new row, so the previous tenant's scopes and Nest never attach to the next owner.

#### Backup, restore and regional failure

Neon is a single provider for money records (Launch carries billing support only), so backups are ours. Decision D-027 (Proposed default).

| Target (own targets) | Value | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| Recovery point | 5 minutes | Neon point-in-time restore |
| Recovery time | 4 hours for reads, 8 hours for writes | A restore is an overwrite; verification takes time |
| Regional outage | Honest read-only mode: "We cannot confirm this domain's state right now", registrations paused, no SLA promised in the Terms | Pro has no SLA; the iad1 outages in 4.3a |
| Off-platform copy | Nightly `pg_dump` (custom format) encrypted with the backup key, written to the log-archive bucket with Object Lock for 35 days | Neon history is not a second copy |
| Continuity kit | Nightly encrypted export of domain, expiry, registrant contact ids and lock state, held off-platform | If OpenSRS terminates, every customer can be emailed transfer steps within 24 hours |

Restore runbook: set `registrar_writes_paused`; restore to time T on a new branch (Neon keeps `{branch}_old_{timestamp}`); write a `restore` event to the audit log and the external anchor; replay Stripe events since T (`GET /v1/events` covers the last 30 days) and re-run `order.reconcile`; compare OpenSRS inventory (`GET_DOMAINS_BY_EXPIREDATE`) and `GET_ORDERS_BY_DOMAIN` with `domains` and `orders`; re-run `retention.purge` from the erasure ledger; verify the chain to the last anchor and expect a gap between T and the restore; lift the flag. A restore rewinds orders, payments and the audit chain while Stripe and OpenSRS keep their newer truth, so a restore is an audited incident. A monthly drill on a scratch project restores, verifies the chain, decrypts a canary and records the time. Egress failover is in Egress.

#### Operations: alerts, logs and on-call

Severity S1 is money, keys or domains; S2 is next business day. Thresholds are own targets (D-028, Proposed default).

| Alert | Threshold | Sev |
|---|---|---|
| Cron heartbeat | Older than 3 minutes, from an external dead-man's switch | S1 |
| Order stuck | `authorized` or `registering` over 10 minutes; `outcome_unknown` over 30 minutes; any authorization within 36 hours of `capture_before` | S1 |
| Money mismatch | `paid_before_registration`, `capture_failed`, or a Stripe/orders mismatch | S1 |
| Registrar | Balance below the sell gate, adapter circuit open, unrequested transfer-away, or an unexplained change from the detector | S1 |
| Stripe | Webhook failures 3 in 10 minutes; dispute ratio at 0.5% | S1 |
| Keys | Decrypt with no audit row, Decrypt volume or secret diversity, `ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `DisableKey`, `PutKeyPolicy`, unknown OIDC `sub` | S1 |
| Audit | Chain verification failure or anchor older than 25 hours | S1 |
| Mail | Bounce above 2%, spam above 0.04% (Resend pauses at 4% and 0.08%), daily quota above 80% | S2 |
| Brand domains | Expiry under 60 days, TLS under 21 days (S2); NS, MX or apex A differs from the stored expectation (S1) | S1, S2 |
| Jobs, budgets | A dead-lettered job (S2; a money kind is S1); budget alerts at 75% | S2 |
