S1 goes to a phone push plus email to two addresses. If nobody acknowledges within 30 minutes, "auto-safe mode" sets `registrar_writes_paused` in the `flags` table (a database row, not an environment variable, since deploys may be unavailable) and the UI says "New registrations are paused." It pauses new registrations, transfers and sensitive changes; renewals and reads continue. The runtime role may set the flag; clearing it is a documented SQL statement run by the operator. The founder is the sole on-call; a second trusted person holds the emergency sheet and read-only alert access. **Logs:** a Vercel Log Drain (Pro) to storage we own, structured allow-listed fields only, 30-day retention. **Status page:** on a provider other than Vercel and AWS, updated by hand, linked from the footer and from mail. **Mandatory notices** (expiry notices, receipts) have a fallback if Resend pauses sending: an in-app banner, OpenSRS's own reminders (90, 60, 30 and 5 days before expiry and 3 and 10 days after), and a second mail provider kept ready. **Field telemetry:** first-party cookieless counters (quality tier, WebGL fallback reason, DPR step-downs, checkout step drop-off) posted to `/api/telemetry` under `connect-src 'self'`, no identifiers, disclosed in the Privacy Notice.

#### Platform secrets: inventory and rotation

Rotate on schedule (90 days where an overlap exists), on any suspected exposure, and whenever a person with access leaves.

| Secret | Held in | If leaked | Rotation |
|---|---|---|---|
| OpenSRS API key | `registrar` project, Production Sensitive variable (Horizon key in staging) | Every domain in the reseller account; one reseller-wide key, no scoping, no sub-user access | Zero overlap (a new key invalidates the old at once): set `registrar_writes_paused`, generate in the panel, update the variable, redeploy, smoke `GET_BALANCE`, lift. After any egress IP change wait 15 minutes. On compromise tell OpenSRS within 4 hours (MSA 2.8). Rehearsed against Horizon before launch |
| OpenSRS IP rules | OpenSRS panel | Narrows a leaked key; the pair is shared | Five rules by default: Static IP pair plus the fallback gateway |
| Stripe restricted key, webhook secret | `web`, Sensitive | Charges and refunds; forged webhooks | Restricted key with least privilege, rolled with Stripe's overlap of up to 7 days; webhook secret rolled with the 24-hour overlap |
| Resend key | `web`, one per deployment environment | Mail as Mosshatch | 90 days; Resend revokes keys found on GitHub |
| `CRON_SECRET` | `web`; GitHub Actions for staging | Trigger ticks | 90 days |
| Neon role passwords (runtime, vault, cron, migrator) | Vercel variables; migrator only in CI | Data access per grants | Password reset; 90 days |
| RPC signing key (`web` to `registrar`) | Private key in `web`, public key in `registrar` | Calls within the allow-list and fuse | 90 days, two public keys accepted during the overlap |
| OIDC custom audience | `web` Sensitive variable and the IAM provider | Assume the KMS role with a leaked header | On suspicion |
| Counter pepper (HMAC of IP for rate limits) | `web` | Reverse the 24-hour counters | Versioned; on suspicion |
| GitHub Actions secrets, npm | GitHub | Deploy and publish | The CLI is published only from CI with npm trusted publishing (no long-lived token) |
| AWS break-glass role | Hardware-key MFA | Key deletion or disable | Tested yearly |

Provider-account protection is the strongest 2FA the provider offers (hardware keys where possible); whether OpenSRS offers hardware-key 2FA, a per-login IP restriction or a second credential is a written question (4.1), and registry lock is not offered through OpenSRS in any document read.

#### Support and abuse operations

Before any outside user (Phase 2): real inboxes at mosshatch.com for support@, abuse@, legal@, privacy@, security@, dmca@ and postmaster (not Resend inbound only), with macros for refund (5-day window), receipt, lost passkey (points to the recovery flow in 4.5) and dispute. Rules: support sees metadata only (never secret values or ciphertext); support cannot unlock, transfer, change nameservers or contacts, add a passkey or bypass the recovery delay; an identity claim by email or phone never replaces a passkey ceremony; every support action writes `audit_log` with `actor_kind = 'support'`. Before the first live registration (before the live rehearsal, not after launch): an abuse form that needs no login, acknowledgement within 2 business days and a decision within 3 (the C-21 internal targets), registrar-directed holds and legal requests acknowledged within 4 hours, a dispute-lock intake (C-23), and a DMCA and DSA Article 16 route for cards. `abuse_reports` records channel, received, acknowledged, evidence, decision and actor. The founder is the sole on-call, and the second person from Operations backs the alerts (D-028).

#### Release, migrations and rollback

Migrations are expand-then-contract, so deployment N-1 runs against schema N. They run in CI on a Neon branch, then on production before promotion, with the `migrator` role, and migration files carry monotonic timestamps. Production deployments are staged (auto-assignment of production domains off) and promoted after a smoke test. Feature flags and kill switches (`registrar_writes_paused`, `orders_paused`, `agent_purchases_paused`) are rows in `flags`, read on every write path and changed by a documented SQL statement, so they need no deploy; assume deploys may be impossible during an incident. Instant Rollback does not update crons, so it is followed by a check that `vercel.json` crons match. Only Deployment Policies-approved paths deploy to production.

### 4.4 Data model

#### Tables and rules

Conventions: every table has `id uuid` (v7, time-ordered), `created_at`, `updated_at` unless append-only. Money is integer minor units plus an ISO currency code, never floats. All timestamps `timestamptz`, UTC. Vault ciphertext appears in exactly two tables, `secret_versions` and `connection_credentials` (envelope encryption, the vault role); no other table holds vault ciphertext or a plaintext secret. Field-encrypted values under the PII key, apart from the vault, are `contacts` and `registrar_profiles` (the contacts role, C-50), the raw IP in `consents` and `orders.checkout_ip` (with a `retain_until`), and the `audit_log.pii` column (under a per-user audit key wrapped by the PII key). Everywhere else an IP is stored only as a prefix. `livemode boolean not null` is on `orders`, `payments`, `stripe_customers`, `webhook_events` and `domains` (4.3b Environments). Every state change is a conditional update (`WHERE id = $1 AND state = $expected`).

| Table | Purpose | Key columns and rules |
|---|---|---|
| **Accounts and sessions** | | |
| `users` | Account | `email` (citext, unique), `email_verified_at`, `webauthn_user_handle` (random 32 bytes, immutable, never the email or the uuid), `billing_country`, `terms_version`, `terms_accepted_at`, `status` (pending, active, closing, closed, purged), `frozen_at`, `risk_state` (normal, review), `closed_at`. A `pending` row holds no credential and expires after 24 hours; a later verified sign-up for the same address replaces it |
| `notification_addresses` | Who is told about security events | `user_id`, `address` (citext), `kind` (login, second, registrant), `verified_at`, `removed_at`, `added_by_action_id`. Changes notify every address and are refused during a recovery hold (4.5) |
| `passkeys` | WebAuthn credentials | `user_id`, `credential_id` (unique), `public_key`, `alg`, `sign_count`, `transports`, `backup_eligible`, `backup_state`, `label`, `last_used_at`, `revoked_at`, `suspended_at`, `suspended_by_recovery_id` (credentials replaced by a recovery are suspended for 30 days, not deleted) |
| `sessions` | Server sessions | `id_hash` (SHA-256 of cookie value), `user_id`, `created_at`, `last_seen_at`, `expires_at`, `idle_expires_at`, `revoked_at`, `auth_credential_id`, `ip_prefix`, `ua_family`; the cookie is `__Host-` prefixed, HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax. A new id is issued at login, on any credential change and after recovery |
| `webauthn_challenges` | One-time ceremony state | `purpose` (register, login, stepup), `challenge`, `user_id`, `session_id`, `action_id` (nullable), `expires_at`, `consumed_at`. Consumed in its own committed statement before verification |
| `actions` | Step-up bound actions | `user_id`, `session_id_hash`, `type` (exactly the thirteen dotted ids: `secret.reveal`, `domain.nameservers.change`, `domain.unlock`, `domain.transfer_out`, `domain.contact.change`, `agent.purchase.approve`, `agent.token.create`, `agent.token.widen`, `dns.sensitive.approve`, `device.approve`, `passkey.add`, `card.publish`, `mandate.sign`), `params_hash` (SHA-256 of canonical JSON), `resource_id`, `state` (prepared, committed, dispatching, executed, failed, outcome_unknown, expired, cancelled), `expires_at` (120 s), `committed_at`, assertion evidence (`credential_id`, `uv`, `be`, `bs`, `client_data_json`, `authenticator_data`, `signature`; not secret), `terms_hash` (only for actions that carry an acceptance), `expected_post_state` (registrar effects). The challenge is `SHA-256(JCS{action_id, type, params_hash, user_id, session_id, nonce, exp})`, so an assertion cannot be replayed for another action |
| `recovery_codes` | Passkey-loss recovery | `user_id`, `code_hash` (SHA-256 for 128-bit codes; a shorter code would need a salted password hash), `used_at` |
| `recovery_requests` | Recovery and its holds | `user_id`, `path` (codes_email, email_only), `status`, `cooling_off_until`, `hold_until`, `cancelled_by`, `completed_at` |
| `email_action_tokens` | Protective email links | `token_hash` (SHA-256 of at least 128 bits), `purpose` (freeze, recovery_cancel, auto_renew_off), `event_id`, `user_id`, `expires_at`, `used_at`. Single use; a GET never consumes one (4.5) |
| **Consent and compliance** | | |
| `consents` | What was accepted, when, by whom | `kind` (terms, registration_agreement, tld_addendum, fee_page, auto_renew_mandate, express_request), `document_hash`, `version`, `accepted_at`, `ip` (encrypted), `ua_family`, `assertion_action_id`, `order_id`, `domain_id`, `actor_kind`, `retain_until`. One row per registration and per mandate; an agent token cannot accept an agreement (C-12, C-14, C-31, C-38, C-40) |
| `document_versions` | The legal document set | `kind`, `version_hash`, `effective_at`, `retired_at` |
| `tax_regions` | Where Mosshatch is registered | `state`, `registered_at`, `stripe_registration_ref`, `threshold_state`; read server-side before a Checkout Session is created (C-42) |
| `sales_ledger` | Nexus monitoring | `state`, `period` (month), `gross_minor`, `txn_count`; a rolling 12-month view alerts at 60% and 80% of each state's threshold (C-43) |
| `sanctions_screenings` | Screening log | `subject_kind`, `subject_ref`, `list_version`, `result`, `checked_at` (C-25) |
| `notices` | Compliance notice ledger (expiry notices, verification reminders, price-change notices) | `kind`, `domain_id`, `user_id`, `sent_at`, `template_version`, `email_log_id`, `retain_until`, `legal_hold` |
| `abuse_reports` | Inbound abuse, takedown and legal-request queue | `target_kind` (domain, card), `reporter_ref`, `channel`, `received_at`, `acked_at`, `evidence`, `decision`, `actor`, `state`, `resolution`, `actioned_at` |
| **Domains and money** | | |
| `contacts` | Registrant contact | `user_id`; name, street, city, region, postal code, country, phone and email are field-encrypted under the PII key (per-field envelope encryption, access logged, its own role); `email_verified_at`, `verification_state`, `verification_deadline_at` (ICANN 15-day verification), `bounced_at` |
| `domains` | Owned domain | `user_id`, `fqdn_ascii`, `tld`, `registrar` (adapter id), `registrar_ref`, `state` (mirror of adapter status), `registered_at`, `registry_created_at` (moss age; true age for transfers), `expires_at`, `locked`, `privacy_status` (redacted_default, not_available, exposed), `auto_renew` (true only while an active mandate exists), `nameservers[]`, `registry_statuses[]`, `ds_present`, `dns_hosted_here`, `dispute_lock_state`, `livemode`, `released_at`, `release_reason`, `synced_at`, `sync_error`. Unique on `fqdn_ascii` where `released_at is null`; re-registration always inserts a new row. Species, palette and traits are NOT stored; they are recomputed from the name |
| `registrar_profiles` | Per-registration registrant profile at the upstream | `order_id`, `domain_id`, `username`, `password_enc` (field-encrypted under the PII key; never shown or emailed) |
| `orders` | Money-spending intents | `user_id`, `kind` (register, renew, transfer_in, restore), `fqdn_ascii`, `domain_id`, `years`, `state` (the states in 4.3b), `idempotency_key` (unique per user) with `request_hash`, `quote` jsonb (wholesale, renewal wholesale, flat fee, tax estimate, total, currency, quoted_at, expires_at, source), `subtotal_minor`, `tax_ceiling_minor`, `total_minor`, `stripe_checkout_session_id` (unique), `stripe_payment_intent_id` (unique), `attempt`, `capture_before`, `target_expiry_year`, `agent_request_id` (unique when set), `checkout_ip` (encrypted), `livemode`, `failure_code`. Partial unique indexes: register orders per `fqdn_ascii` in `authorized`, `registering`, `outcome_unknown` or `registered`; renew and restore orders per (`domain_id`, `kind`, `target_expiry_year`) while not terminal |
| `order_operations` | Write-ahead record of every registrar and Stripe side effect | `order_id`, `kind`, `seq`, `request_hash`, `state` (intent, sent, resolved), `sent_at`, `registrar_order_id`, `response_code`, `attempt_id`; unique on (`order_id`, `kind`, `seq`). An operation in `sent` is never re-sent, only reconciled |
| `order_events` | Append-only order transitions | `order_id`, `from_state`, `to_state`, `cause` (webhook, job, user, agent), `stripe_event_id`, `detail` |
| `payments` | Mirror of Stripe money movement | `order_id`, `stripe_payment_intent_id` (unique), `amount`, `tax_minor`, `currency`, `status`, `captured_at`, `refunded_amount`, `dispute_state`, `billing_state` (US state, feeds `sales_ledger`), `livemode`. No card data, ever |
| `refunds` | Refund ledger | `order_id`, `payment_id`, `stripe_refund_id`, `amount`, `reason`; counted per account for the 30-day cap |
| `stripe_customers` | Link | `user_id`, `stripe_customer_id`, `livemode` |
| `renewal_mandates` | Opt-in auto-renew consent | `domain_id`, `user_id`, `stripe_payment_method_ref`, `price_ceiling_minor`, `charge_days_before_expiry` (10), `text_hash`, `term_years`, `signed_action_id` (`mandate.sign`), `consent_id`, `accepted_at`, `retain_until` (3 years after the last renewal), `revoked_at`, `next_charge_at` |
| `wholesale_prices` | Effective-dated price table | `registrar`, `tld`, `kind` (register, renew, transfer, restore), `amount`, `effective_from`, `source`; a quote is pinned to the row it used |
| `tld_policy` | Per-extension and per-registrar lifecycle facts | `registrar`, `tld`, `registry_window_days` (transfer, 5 for `.com`, `.dev`, `.app`, `.studio`), `longest_seen_days` (about 14 through OpenSRS), `expiry_grace_days`, `redemption_days`, `auction_from_day` (labelled Tucows policy), `restore_via_api`, `min_term_years`, `source` |
| `maintenance_windows` | Upstream maintenance | `registrar`, `starts_at`, `ends_at`, `source`; fed daily and by hand, read by the adapter |
| `flags` | Kill switches, limits, heartbeat | `name`, `value` jsonb, `updated_by`: `registrar_writes_paused`, `orders_paused`, `agent_purchases_paused`, `limits.daily_registrations`, `heartbeat.tick`. Read on every write path; changed by a documented SQL statement |
| `webhook_events` | Dedupe and replay | `provider` (stripe, resend, opensrs, github), `event_id` (unique with provider), `livemode`, `type`, `received_at`, `processed_at`, `error` |
| **Jobs, transfers and DNS** | | |
| `jobs` | Durable work queue | `kind`, `priority` (0 money, 1 other), `run_at`, `state` (queued, running, done, failed, dead), `attempt_id`, `attempts`, `max_attempts`, `locked_until`, `dedupe_key` (unique while queued or running), `payload` jsonb (ids only, never secrets or authorization codes), `last_error` |
| `transfers` | Transfer in and out | `domain_id`, `direction`, `state`, `initiated_by` (owner, unknown), `matching_action_id`, `registrar_transfer_ref`, `submitted_at`, `expected_by`, `last_polled_at`, `auth_code_issued_at`, `cancel_deadline_at`, `terminal_reason`. The authorization code itself is never stored |
| `dns_records` | Cache of registrar-hosted DNS | `domain_id`, `adapter_record_id`, `type`, `name`, `value`, `ttl`, `priority`, `source` (user, recipe, agent), `sync_state` (synced, pending_write, pending_delete, drift, error), `last_verified_at` |
| `dns_write_intents` | In-flight DNS changes | `domain_id`, `op`, `record`, `state` (submitted, applied, visible_at_authoritative, failed), `job_id`. Drives the Shedding state |
| `dns_snapshots` | Pre-write zone for rollback | `domain_id`, `taken_at`, `zone` jsonb, `expires_at` (30 days) |
| `connections` | Services wired to a domain | `domain_id`, `service` (vercel, neon, resend, ...), `external_ref`, `status`, `recipe_application_id`, `ended_at` |
| `connection_credentials` | Customer's third-party credentials for recipes | `connection_id`, `kind` (pasted_token, oauth_refresh), `scope_summary`, the envelope columns of `secret_versions` (AAD adds `connection_id` and `service`), `last_used_at`, `revoked_at`. No route, tool or command returns them |
| `recipe_applications` | Wire-it history | `domain_id`, `recipe_id`, `recipe_version`, `plan` jsonb (records and variable names, no values), `plan_hash`, `state`, `applied_by` |
| **The Nest** | | |
| `secrets` | Secret identity (no value) | `domain_id`, `env` (dev, preview, prod), `name` (CHECK `^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{0,127}$`; rejected when it matches a client-bundle scanner rule, with a generic 422 that does not echo the input); unique on the triple; `current_version_id`; `pointer_mac` (KMS HMAC over `secret_id`, `name`, `env` and `current_version`, checked on every read) |
| `secret_versions` | Ciphertext | `secret_id`, `version`, `format_version`, `alg` (`A256GCM`), `ciphertext` (at most 16 KiB), `nonce`, `tag` (16 bytes), `wrapped_dek`, `kek_ref`, `created_by_kind`, `created_by_id`, `destroyed_at` (deletion nulls `ciphertext` and `wrapped_dek`). The AAD is recomputed at decrypt, not stored (4.3b Secrets) |
| `bindings` | Agent and CLI tokens | `user_id`, `kind` (agent, cli), `name` (chosen by the owner under step-up; a bearer cannot rename it), `token_prefix`, `token_hash` (unique), `scopes` jsonb (capability, resolved `domain_id` or `*`, env; the fqdn is a display label only), `spend_cap_minor`, `reserved_minor`, `spent_minor` (both CHECK >= 0), `expires_at` (default 30 days, maximum 90; own targets), `revoked_at`, `paused_at`, `last_used_at`, `created_by_action_id`; for CLI bindings a rotating refresh token (`family_id`, `refresh_hash`, `rotated_at`; reuse of a rotated token revokes the family) |
| `agent_requests` | Pending approvals | `binding_id`, `user_id`, `kind` (register, renew, dns_change; `transfer_in` is not offered to agents in v1), `params` jsonb (a closed typed schema per kind; unknown keys are rejected and there is no free-text field), `request_hash` (unique per binding while pending), `currency`, `quoted_minor` (the frozen server quote, tax included, for the longer of the years asked and the extension's minimum term), `tax_ceiling_minor`, `quote_hash`, `terms_hash`, `state` (pending, declined, expired, approved, voided, failed), `decision_reason`, `decided_by_action_id` (unique), `order_id` (unique when set), `expires_at` (72 hours, own target, never later than the binding's expiry). After approval, progress is derived from `orders.state`; there is no second state machine |
| **Audit, mail and operations** | | |
| `audit_log` | Append-only, MAC chained | `chain_id` (per user), `seq` (assigned inside the chain-head lock on `audit_heads`, so a gap means tampering), `at`, `actor_kind` (user, agent, cli, system, support), `actor_id`, `action`, `resource_kind`, `resource_id`, `pii` (encrypted `ip_prefix` and `ua_family`, under a per-user audit key wrapped by the PII key and destroyed on erasure), `detail` jsonb (no values, no names, opaque ids), `retention_class`, `legal_hold`, `prev_mac`, `mac` (KMS HMAC over the canonical row plus `prev_mac`). The app role has INSERT and SELECT only, does not own the table and has no TRUNCATE; row UPDATE, row DELETE and statement TRUNCATE triggers are `ENABLE ALWAYS`; reveal and read rows are written by the vault module's own role. Chain heads are anchored daily to a write-once bucket in a separate account |
| `audit_heads` | Chain-head row lock | `chain_id`, `seq`, `head_mac` |
| `cards` | Published creature cards | `domain_id`, `slug` (unique among unreleased, published rows), `published_at`, `unpublished_at`, `snapshot_ref`, `takedown_state`, `indexable` (false until the owner opts in). Private by default; the public site reads a view exposing only published rows |
| `email_log` | Idempotent mail | `kind`, `dedupe_key` (unique), `user_id`, `provider_message_id`, `status` (queued, sent, delivered, bounced, complained, suppressed), `bounced_at`, `complained_at`. No body and no address: the recipient is resolved at send time. A hard bounce to a registrant email nulls `contacts.email_verified_at` and starts the 15-day clock; a complaint never suppresses mandatory notices |
| `reconciliation_findings` | Detector output | `run_id`, `domain_id`, `kind` (unexplained_change, missing_domain, extra_domain, mismatch), `detail`, `state` (open, explained, dismissed), `found_at` |
| `alerts` | Operations | `severity`, `kind`, `subject`, `state`, `raised_at`, `acked_at`, `acked_by` |
| `account_exports` | Export requests | `user_id`, `requested_at`, `ready_at`, `expires_at`, `storage_ref` |
| `rate_counters` | Rate limits | `key_hash` (HMAC, never the IP or search text), `window_start`, `count`; 24-hour TTL; counters live in Postgres because in-memory limits are useless on Vercel functions |

Design notes:
- The creature is a pure function of (domain name, adapter snapshot, jobs, orders); no table stores "creature state". `packages/core` exports `deriveCreatureState()` and `deriveTraits()` and is imported by both the server and the world module.
- `domains.state`, `expires_at`, `locked`, `nameservers`, `registry_statuses` and `ds_present` are caches of adapter truth with `synced_at`. The UI shows "confirmed by registrar 3 minutes ago" from `synced_at`, and never advances a state on its own.
- Names of secrets are plaintext metadata (the creature knows how many); the name grammar above is enforced for every writer (web, CLI, MCP, recipes). Secret values exist in plaintext only inside a request handler during reveal, write, or CLI pull, and in the caller's memory. Transfer authorization codes are shown once and never stored, logged or put in a job payload.
- **Tenant isolation.** Ids identify, they never authorize: authorization is the ownership join. Every handler resolves the parent first (fqdn to the caller's `domain_id`, else 404) and loads children by `id AND domain_id`. Row-level security uses `set_config('app.user_id', $1, true)` inside every transaction through one `withUser(principal, fn)` wrapper, and fails closed when the setting is unset; job handlers set it from a `user_id` in the payload. The runtime, vault, cron, contacts and migrator roles are separate, created with SQL (Console roles bypass RLS); only the cron role, which claims jobs across tenants, may bypass it.
- **Retention and erasure.** `retain_until` and `legal_hold` carry the retention classes of C-19 on `consents`, `notices` and `audit_log`; the audit chain stays pseudonymous and erasure works as described in 4.3b (Account closure, export and erasure). `secret_versions` are destroyed 30 days after a domain is released.

### 4.5 API, CLI and MCP surface

One handler layer serves three front doors: the web app (cookie session), the REST API and CLI (bearer token), and MCP (bearer token). Authorization is always decided server-side from the principal and the requested (capability, resource, environment); no client-side check counts. All routes live under `/api/v1` except `/mcp`, `/api/cron/tick`, `/api/csp-report`, `/api/health/ticks` and `/api/telemetry`, so the service-worker deny rule and the CSRF guard key on one prefix.

**Principals and route declarations.** `user-session` (browser, passkey-authenticated), `cli-binding` (created by device flow, approved with a passkey), `agent-binding` (created in the Bindings tab, passkey to create or widen), `cron`, and `webhook:<provider>`. Every route declares its allowed principals; a handler with no declaration fails registration and the build (deny by default). A request carrying both a session cookie and an `Authorization` header is rejected 400 before any handler runs (stricter than ignoring one). `Authorization` is parsed by route: on `/api/cron/*` it is compared only with `CRON_SECRET`; elsewhere a value that is not an `mh_` token is 401. Classes: **anonymous** (search, sign-up, login, recovery use, `/oauth/device/code`, `/oauth/token`, `/oauth/revoke`; each carries its own proof such as an assertion, code or signature); **session-only** (everything under `/actions`, approvals decisions, bindings create, patch, delete and revoke-all, passkey and address changes, `/oauth/device/approve`, card publish, mandate, nameservers, contact, transfer-out, `PATCH /domains/{fqdn}`), where a bearer gets 401 or 403 with every scope; **bearer carve-outs** for a named capability on the caller's own resources only (whoami, the state of approvals the calling binding created, the capability routes below); **cron** and **webhook**. A binding can never start or complete a passkey ceremony.

**Step-up actions.** Fifteen dotted ids (the thirteen of Phase 2, plus `account.close` and `account.export` added with account closure, migration 1050), and `actions.type` uses exactly these. Held (H) means refused during the recovery holds below. Narrowing a token is free and is not an action. Bound parameters are derived on the server from current state; the client supplies only `{type, target_id, user_input}` where `user_input` is limited to what the person must supply (the new nameserver list, the token scopes) under a per-id schema, and the person sees a summary rendered from the server-prepared canonical params.

| Id | Gates | Bound by the assertion | Held |
|---|---|---|---|
| `secret.reveal` | Web reveal of one secret | secret id, env, version | H |
| `domain.nameservers.change` | Nameserver change | domain, before and after list hash | H |
| `domain.unlock` | Unlock | domain | H |
| `domain.transfer_out` | Unlock and authorization-code issue together (D-009) | domain, gaining-registrar note | H |
| `domain.contact.change` | Registrant, admin or technical contact change (parity with Transfer Policy I.A.5.3) | domain, before and after contact hash | H |
| `agent.purchase.approve` | Approving an agent's proposal | request id, binding, domain, years, subtotal, tax ceiling, currency, quote hash, terms hash | H |
| `agent.token.create` | Creating a binding (agent) | name, scopes, cap, expiry | H |
| `agent.token.widen` | Widening a binding, or resuming a paused one | binding, before and after scope, cap and expiry hash | H |
| `dns.sensitive.approve` | An agent's or recipe's change to MX, NS, DS, apex A/AAAA/CNAME, SPF/DMARC TXT and the wider classes in 4.3b (D-011, D-034), and a recipe step that creates a billable provider resource | request or plan hash, before and after record-set hashes | H |
| `device.approve` | Approving a CLI device flow | request id, typed code, exact scopes shown | H |
| `passkey.add` | Adding a passkey | credential label, options hash | H |
| `card.publish` | Publishing a card on `hatchkind.com` | domain, card hash | H |
| `mandate.sign` | The auto-renew mandate (D-008) | domain, price ceiling, text hash, term | not held, so renewals keep working |
| `account.close` | Closing the account (4.3b Account closure, export and erasure; added with the closure module, migration 1050) | account, the names still registered and the choice to delete them, cooling-off days | H |
| `account.export` | Asking for a copy of the account's data (4.3b; the download also needs a signed-in session and a one-time ticket) | account, export format | H |

Removing a passkey, regenerating recovery codes and changing a notification address are session changes in v1: each emails every address, an address change is refused during a hold, and whether to make them step-ups is recorded in D-016.

**Step-up protocol.**
1. `POST /actions/prepare {type, target_id, user_input}` returns `{action_id, webauthn_options}` with `allowCredentials` set to the user's non-revoked passkeys. The challenge is `SHA-256(JCS{action_id, type, params_hash, user_id, session_id, nonce, exp})`, single use, **120 seconds** (own target; the research proposed 300 and an expired prompt offers Try again with a new challenge, so nobody must finish inside the window, WCAG 2.2.1). WebAuthn `timeout` is 120000 ms.
2. The browser calls `navigator.credentials.get()` with `userVerification: "required"`. An endpoint marked (step-up) returns 403 `step_up_required` naming the action type unless called with a committed `action_id`; `/commit` executes the action and its body is the endpoint's normal response (for a reveal, the value with `Cache-Control: no-store, private`; for registrar effects, the action's state, since those run in a job after commit).
3. `POST /actions/{id}/commit {assertion}`: (a) consume the challenge in its own committed statement (`UPDATE webauthn_challenges SET consumed_at = now() WHERE id = $1 AND consumed_at IS NULL AND expires_at > now() AND session_id = $2 RETURNING *`; zero rows ends the request), before verification, so a failed attempt also burns it; (b) check the session and user match the `actions` row, `response.id` was in the issued `allowCredentials`, load the credential with `WHERE credential_id = $1 AND user_id = actions.user_id AND revoked_at IS NULL` (any other account's credential fails closed), `userHandle` equals `users.webauthn_user_handle`, `expectedOrigin` is the configured constant list and `expectedRPID` is `mosshatch.com`, verify with SimpleWebAuthn (`verified` and `userVerified`, attestation `none`) and fail closed and alert if the backup-eligible flag differs from the stored one; (c) in one transaction re-derive `params_hash` from current state, compare, move `actions` from `prepared` to `committed` (a compare-and-set, not an insert), apply the resource's compare-and-set (for approvals, `agent_requests` from `pending` to `approved` with the binding unrevoked), create any order with an idempotency key derived from the request, and queue an `action.execute` job (one queued or running per domain, so commit order is execution order); (d) the job pre-reads registrar state, sends only if it differs, re-reads, and sends timeouts to reconciliation by polling (4.3b Jobs and Gate). The library is stateless (a valid assertion verified twice passed both times), so single use is ours to enforce. There is no reusable elevated session, so a stolen cookie cannot ride an earlier step-up. The transfer authorization code is never placed in a job payload; it is read after the unlock job reports executed and shown once.
4. `signCount` is stored but enforced only for credentials whose backup-eligible bit is 0. Backup-eligible and backup-state bits are tracked to detect "only one device-bound credential" and a flip from 1 to 0. Registration pins `supportedAlgorithmIDs` to `[-7, -257]` and stores `alg`. Each committed action stores the assertion evidence (credential, flags, client data, authenticator data, signature; not secret) as dispute evidence.
5. Bearer tokens never reach `/actions/*`. A route walk over the route table fails the build if any handler calls the gate with an id outside this table or any id has no gated route.

**Reveal sequence and the two secret read paths (D-019, for you to veto).** (1) *Web session:* every reveal is a `secret.reveal` step-up, one secret per assertion; transaction T1 consumes the challenge, verifies, inserts the audit row `secret.reveal.authorized` and commits (if the insert fails nothing is decrypted); then KMS `Decrypt` runs outside any transaction (3-second timeout, no retry loop); then T2 records `released` or `failed` (a T2 failure alerts and does not block). `POST /secrets/{id}/reveal` is an alias that creates the action. (2) *Bearer binding:* a binding created by step-up (`agent.token.create`, or `device.approve` for the CLI) with `secrets.read:<domain>:<env>` reads that domain and environment through `POST /domains/{fqdn}/secrets/{env}/read` (bearer only; a cookie session is rejected) without a per-read ceremony. The passkey at binding creation is the delegated step-up for the reads it allows. `:prod` must be named explicitly; a missing scope is 403, never an empty list; one audit row per secret id is committed before decrypt and if that write fails no value is returned; limits by token id 20 a minute and 500 a day, and 6 an hour on `prod` (own targets); an hourly digest email; the response is `no-store, private`, at most 100 secrets, and a KMS error is 503 `vault_unavailable` (the CLI exits 75). `pull`, `run` and the MCP tool `secrets_get` all use this route. Any value returned to an MCP client is assumed stored by it (transcripts, tool-result files).

**Sessions and recovery.** Opaque 256-bit session token in a non-persistent `__Host-` cookie (`Secure; HttpOnly; Path=/; SameSite=Lax`; not Strict, see Stack verdicts), hash in the session table with `created_at`, `last_seen_at`, `revoked_at` and the credential used. Idle timeout 15 minutes (the bottom of OWASP's 15 to 30 minute low-risk range; the 2 to 5 minute range for high-value applications is covered by per-action step-up) and absolute 8 hours. A new session id is issued at login, on any credential change and after recovery; logout deletes the row and sends `Clear-Site-Data: "cookies", "storage"`. CSRF: every non-GET request to a cookie route, including `/auth/*`, needs `Sec-Fetch-Site` absent or `same-origin`, `Origin` equal to the app origin, a JSON content type and `X-MH-Client: web`; ceremony endpoints also carry a session-bound token and login options set a short-lived `__Host-mh_pre` cookie that verify must present; no handler mutates on GET.

*Sign-up* first proves control of the email with an emailed one-time code; only then are registration options issued, and an unverified row holds no credential, expires in 24 hours and is replaced by a later verified sign-up for the same address (this closes account pre-hijacking). *Notification addresses:* the login email, a second address on a different mail domain, and the registrant email when it differs. A second address or a downloaded recovery code is required before the first domain purchase or stored secret, because the login mailbox alone is not an independent channel (a warning is not enough once money and domains are involved); the registrant email defaults to an address different from the login email and the panel warns when they match. NIST SP 800-63B-4 requires the service to support two notification addresses; requiring enrolment is our policy.

*Recovery.* At least two credentials are asked for at sign-up (or a clear warning), ten single-use recovery codes of 128 bits (26 base32 characters, stored hashed; ten attempts an hour without blocking passkey sign-in) are shown once, and an emailed code (valid at most 24 hours) works with a code or a second credential. **Holds on the actions marked H:** recovery by codes plus emailed code, no cooling-off and a 24-hour hold; email-only recovery, a 72-hour cooling-off that any legitimate sign-in cancels, then a 72-hour hold (the research proposed 7 days; 72 hours keeps unlock and code release inside ICANN's five calendar days, counsel to confirm); a passkey added while signed in, a 24-hour hold on `secret.reveal` only. Email-only recovery is offered only where a second verified channel exists that is not the recovery mailbox, and starting or completing it emails every address and the registrant contact and shows a banner at every sign-in. Completion revokes all sessions and bindings, suspends the old credentials for 30 days instead of deleting them, and a user-verified assertion from a suspended credential restores them, revokes what the recovery created and re-applies the hold (a deliberate departure from NIST's presumption that a lost authenticator is stolen; hardened mode is the mitigation). At most one pending request per account. Sync-provider takeover is real (NIST 800-63B-4 Appendix B; Unit 42, 3 August 2026: malware on a Windows endpoint can take over accounts protected by Google-synced passkeys, and the Silver and Golden variants defeat user verification for synced credentials), so an opt-in **hardened mode** requires device-bound (backup-eligible 0) credentials with direct attestation for security keys, keeps no weaker fallback, and notifies on every credential change.

*Email action links* (recovery cancel, freeze, turn off auto-renew): a GET or HEAD shows a page saying what the link would do and changes nothing (`Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`, `X-Robots-Tag: noindex`, `Cache-Control: no-store`); a confirm button sends a POST with the token in the body through the same guard as other mutations. The token is at least 128 bits, bound to one purpose and one event, single-use, stored as SHA-256 in `email_action_tokens`, and expires (freeze 72 hours, recovery cancel at the end of the cool-off plus 24 hours; own targets). Freeze locks the domain, re-randomises the code, revokes sessions and pauses (does not revoke) bindings; it never unlocks or changes nameservers and does not block renewals; unfreezing needs a passkey sign-in, and each paused binding is resumed through `agent.token.widen`.

**Rate-limit keys and budgets (own targets, tuned in Phase 2).** Never key a limit on an identity an attacker can exhaust or share; each limit is an alarm or a block, and a block never touches a cancel, revoke or freeze path. Counters live in Postgres and hold an HMAC of the address, not the address.

| Path | Key | Starting value |
|---|---|---|
| Recovery start | Per account: one active request, mail at most 1 per 15 minutes; per source prefix (/24 or /48) 10 an hour | Redeeming an emailed code and cancelling are never blocked by account or source; a code dies after 5 wrong tries |
| Mail an unauthenticated request can trigger (class A) | Per target address 3 an hour and 10 a day; a global class A ceiling | Security notices about an existing account (class B) have their own bucket, are never dropped and are coalesced into a digest under flood, except that recovery-started is always sent at once |
| Step-up prepare | Per (user, session) 10 in 10 minutes | Freeze, revoke-all, session or binding revoke and narrowing sit outside; freeze is limited only per link token |
| Bearer authentication | Authenticated calls per token id; failures per (source address, presented-token prefix) | Traffic from a connector's address range (for example 160.79.104.0/21 for claude.ai) raises alarms and never a shared address-only block |
| Web reveal | Per user and session 5 a minute and 30 an hour; per secret 3 a minute | Agent traffic never counts against the human's bucket |
| Bearer secret read | Per token id 20 a minute and 500 a day; `prod` 6 an hour | See two read paths |
| Search and quote | Anonymous per address and /24, then a proof-of-work step; verified users 300 an hour; a global budget split 70% search and 30% checkout, renewals and agents | 4.3b Availability search at scale |
| Approval proposals | 3 pending per binding, 10 per user, 5 proposals an hour per binding | Below |

**REST (v1), grouped.** Paths are relative to `/api/v1`.

| Area | Endpoints |
|---|---|
| Auth | `POST /auth/signup/start` and `/signup/verify` (emailed code first); `/auth/register/options` and `/verify`; `/auth/login/options` and `/verify`; `/auth/logout`; `GET /me`; passkey list, add (`passkey.add`) and revoke; recovery-code issue; `/auth/recovery/start`, `/redeem` and `/cancel`; notification addresses list, add and remove |
| Step-up | `POST /actions/prepare`; `POST /actions/{id}/commit`; `GET /actions/{id}` (session only; bearer tokens are rejected) |
| Search and quote | `GET /search?name=&tlds=` (unauthenticated, rate limited, cached; `tlds` is a subset of the six launch extensions); `GET /quote?domain=&years=` |
| Orders | `POST /orders` (idempotency key required; the same key with different parameters is 409) returns a Stripe Checkout URL; `GET /orders/{id}` |
| Domains | `GET /domains`; `GET /domains/{fqdn}`; `PATCH /domains/{fqdn}` (session only, classified per field: `lock=true` and `auto_renew=false` are allowed, `lock=false` is `domain.unlock`, contact fields are `domain.contact.change`, `auto_renew=true` is not a PATCH field); `POST /domains/{fqdn}/renew`; `POST /domains/{fqdn}/nameservers` (step-up); `POST /domains/{fqdn}/contact` (step-up); `POST /domains/{fqdn}/mandate` (`mandate.sign`) and `DELETE` (free) |
| DNS | `GET/POST /domains/{fqdn}/dns`, `PATCH/DELETE /domains/{fqdn}/dns/{id}`; responses carry `sync_state`; a sensitive record from an agent or recipe returns a pending approval |
| Nest | `GET /domains/{fqdn}/nest?env=` (names, versions, counts, connections; never values); `PUT /domains/{fqdn}/secrets/{env}/{name}` (name grammar and reserved list enforced, 422); `DELETE`; `POST /secrets/{id}/reveal` (step-up alias, `no-store`); `POST /domains/{fqdn}/secrets/{env}/read` (bearer only); `GET /domains/{fqdn}/audit` |
| Recipes | `GET /recipes`; `POST /domains/{fqdn}/recipes/{id}/plan` (dry run: exact records and variable names); `POST .../apply` |
| Connections | `GET /domains/{fqdn}/connections`; `DELETE /connections/{id}` |
| Bindings | `GET/POST /bindings` (create is step-up, session only); `PATCH /bindings/{id}` (session only; a narrowing needs no step-up, anything else is `agent.token.widen`); `DELETE /bindings/{id}`; `POST /bindings/revoke-all`; `GET /bindings/{id}/activity` |
| Approvals | `GET /approvals`; `POST /approvals/{id}/decide` (approve is step-up, session only); `GET /approvals/{id}` (also bearer, for the binding that owns the request, state only, never a URL) |
| Transfers | `POST /transfers/in/check`; `POST /transfers/in`; `GET /transfers/{id}`; `POST /domains/{fqdn}/transfer-out` (step-up); `DELETE /transfers/{id}` (cancel) |
| Cards | `GET /cards`; `POST /cards/{fqdn}/publish` (step-up); `DELETE /cards/{fqdn}/publish` (free) |
| Device flow | `POST /oauth/device/code`; `POST /oauth/token` (device_code and refresh_token grants); `POST /oauth/device/approve` (session only, step-up); `POST /oauth/revoke` (RFC 7009, a bearer can revoke itself) |
| Account | `POST /account/export`; `POST /account/close` |
| Email links | `GET /email-actions/{token}` (shows a page); `POST /email-actions/{token}` (acts) |
| Webhooks and hooks | `POST /webhooks/stripe`, `/webhooks/resend`, `/webhooks/registrar/{provider}`, `/hooks/github-secret-scanning` (each: signature, timestamp tolerance, dedupe in `webhook_events`) |
| Cron and health | `GET /api/cron/tick` (bearer `CRON_SECRET` only); `GET /api/health/ticks` |

**Tenancy.** Ids identify; they never authorize. Every handler resolves the parent first (fqdn to the caller's `domain_id`, else 404) and loads children by `id AND domain_id`; a bare-id route joins to the owning row and filters by `user_id`. An unowned and a nonexistent resource return the same status, headers and body through one code path; a bearer request applies scope and ownership, so out of scope on an owned resource is 403 and unowned is 404. Row-level security uses `set_config('app.user_id', $1, true)` inside every transaction and fails closed when unset (4.4 Tenant isolation). Scope strings are display names, not identities: a binding stores the resolved `domain_id`, and every request checks that the domain is owned by the binding's user and not released.

**Scope vocabulary.** Grammar `capability:resource[:env]`, lower-case ASCII, no globs. The resource is `*` or a domain from the caller's own domains, stored as its `domain_id` and shown as its normalized `fqdn_ascii`; `register.propose` takes `*` only. `env` (`dev`, `preview`, `prod` or `*`) is required for `secrets.read`, `secrets.write`, `recipes.apply` and `nest.names`, optional for `recipes.plan`, and forbidden elsewhere; `secrets.read:*:*` is refused. Anything else (a missing env where required, a glob, `*` as a capability, upper case, a trailing dot, an unnormalized or punycode-variant name, an unowned domain) is a 422 at creation, and unknown scopes fail closed. Capabilities: `domains.read`, `dns.read`, `dns.write`, `nest.names`, `secrets.read`, `secrets.write`, `recipes.plan`, `recipes.apply`, `register.propose`, `renew.propose`, `transfer.status`. Examples: `dns.write:example.com`, `secrets.read:example.com:dev`, `register.propose:*`. Production secrets need an explicit `:prod`; a missing scope is 403, not an empty list. **Widening.** `PATCH /bindings/{id}` computes coverage, not string inclusion: a change is a narrowing only if every proposed (capability, resource, env) is covered by a stored scope (`*` covers any specific value), the cap is not higher and the expiry not later; anything else, including any parse doubt, is a widening and needs `agent.token.widen`. **Lint** at creation and at any widening, token-wide: a binding holding any `secrets.read` together with any `dns.write` is refused (record values are public, so an injected agent could publish a secret), and `secrets.read` with resource `*` and env `prod` is refused; the default env is `dev` only. **Recipes.** `recipes.apply` is an authorization to run a plan, not a bypass: the server derives the plan's touched set from the hash-checked plan (every DNS write, every variable target mapped to `dev`, `preview` or `prod`, every provider connection used) and requires `dns.write` for each DNS step and `secrets.write` for each environment written, and use of a connection is folded into `recipes.apply` for that domain; any touched environment outside the scopes, and any `prod` target without an explicit `:prod`, is 403 and applies nothing. Provider credentials are never returned to the caller. A step that creates a provider resource, or whose cost is unknown, needs `dns.sensitive.approve` binding the plan hash, whatever the principal. Agents have no capability that executes a purchase: `*.propose` creates an `agent_requests` row and a notification, and only a passkey-approved human decision creates the order. `transfer_in` is not offered to agents in v1 (a transfer needs the human-entered authorization code).

**Agent purchases and the approval card.** A proposal is one transaction under a per-user advisory lock: an idempotent insert on (binding, request hash) while pending; a reservation `reserved_minor + spent_minor + quoted_minor <= spend_cap_minor` (else 403 `cap_exceeded`), where `quoted_minor` is the frozen server quote with tax for the longer of the years asked and the extension's minimum term; pending limits and hourly limits as in the table; expiry 72 hours, never later than the binding's expiry; reservations released on decline, expiry, void and failure by a sweeper. The cap covers the token's lifetime and Mosshatch purchases only. Approval is `agent.purchase.approve`; it sets `pending` to `approved` only if the quote is unchanged and the binding live, moves the reservation to spent, and creates the order in `checkout_open` with one Stripe Checkout Session. **It binds no payment method and charges nothing**; the button says "Approve" with the line "You pay on Stripe next. Nothing is charged until you do." The Checkout URL goes only to the approving cookie session, is never stored and never reaches the agent; the agent sees a state derived from the order (`approved_awaiting_payment`, `payment_authorized`, `registering`, `registered`) and never "paid" before `registered`. Off-session charging of a stored card is not used for agent purchases, including `renew.propose`, in v1. **The approval card** shows server fields only, as text nodes: the binding name (chosen by the owner under step-up; `clientInfo`, the User-Agent and any agent text are never rendered), the domain in Unicode and punycode with a mixed-script flag, years, first-year and renewal price at equal size, the maximum total including tax, cumulative spend against the cap and the token's expiry date, request age and expiry, and whether the network is new. `agent_requests.params` is a closed typed schema with no free-text field. One assertion approves one request (`request_id` is in the bound params and `decided_by_action_id` is unique). The person types the domain when the larger of the first charge and one renewal exceeds an owner-set threshold (default USD 50, own target), on a binding's first approval and for an extension the account has not bought before. The route sends `frame-ancestors 'none'`, is reached by in-app navigation, and no email links to it.

**CLI (`mosshatch`).** `login` (RFC 8628 device flow), `logout` (`POST /oauth/revoke`), `whoami`, `domains`, `pull <domain> --env <env>` (requires `--out <file>` or `--stdout`; with neither it exits 2; `--out` is `O_EXCL`, mode 0600, and refuses symlinks and paths under `.git`; values are written by a dotenv encoder that double-quotes and escapes backslash, quotes, newline, carriage return and `$`, and `--format shell` single-quotes), `push <domain> --env <env> [file]`, `run <domain> --env <env> -- <cmd>` (injects into the child process environment only, forwards signals, returns the child's exit code, never writes a file; preferred over `pull` because values never pass through an agent's tool result). Tokens are stored in the OS keychain through `@napi-rs/keyring` (keytar was last released in 2022), pinned to the Secret Service store on Linux with the failure caught on every call, and otherwise fall back loudly, not silently, to a mode-0600 file; non-interactive use reads `MOSSHATCH_TOKEN` from the environment. Never accept a token on the command line. **Device flow.** `device_code` is 256-bit and stored hashed; `user_code` is 8 characters from a 20-letter alphabet (`XXXX-XXXX`, about 34.5 bits), `expires_in` 600 seconds, `interval` 5 seconds with `slow_down`; `verification_uri_complete` is not offered and the approval page ignores any code in the URL, so the person types the code shown in their own terminal (device-code phishing is a documented attack, RFC 8628 section 5.4). Wrong entries are counted per signed-in account and per source address (5 in a lifetime locks approval). The page shows only facts the server observed (address, coarse location, time) and the scopes being granted; anything the device reports appears under "reported by the device, not verified". Approval is `device.approve` with a passkey, binding the request id and the exact scopes. The default grant is `domains.read`, `nest.names`, `secrets.read` and `secrets.write` on `dev` and `preview`; the server caps any requested scope at what the page showed, and `prod` appears only when named on the page (never `*`). The access token (kind `cli`) lasts 60 minutes; the refresh token (kind `clr`) rotates on every use with 30 days idle and 90 absolute (own targets), and reuse of a rotated token revokes the family. Every grant writes an audit row and emails every notification address with a revoke link that opens a confirm page. An account setting disables device login. **Variable names** are validated by the server, not only the CLI: `[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*`, at most 128 characters, and not on a versioned reserved list served by the API (`PATH`, `HOME`, `SHELL`, `ENV`, `BASH_ENV`, `IFS`, `PROMPT_COMMAND`, `LD_*`, `DYLD_*`, `NODE_OPTIONS`, `NODE_PATH`, `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, `PYTHON*` startup and path variables, `PERL5OPT`, `RUBYOPT`, `JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS`, `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `GIT_ASKPASS`, `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `CURL_CA_BUNDLE`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` and the rest of the list in the repository, compared case-insensitively). PUT, `push` (atomically) and recipe apply return 422 for a violation and write an audit row; `run` refuses a stored legacy match and `pull` omits it; there is no per-user override in v1, so legitimate uses such as `NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size` cannot be stored. The list cannot cover every variable a child program reads, and `--help` says so. Output masking on by default when stdout is not a terminal is best effort (base64, JSON-escaped and split output defeat it), and `--help` also states the honest limit that environment variables are readable by same-user processes and by root. The CLI is published only from CI with npm trusted publishing and provenance, no install scripts, one bundled file with a published SHA-256, and look-alike package names reserved.

**MCP.** Target the 2026-07-28 specification (stateless: no `initialize`, single POST endpoint) with `@modelcontextprotocol/server` 2.x; the SDK handler does no Origin or authorization checks, so we add an Origin allow-list and token validation in front of it. Streamable HTTP endpoint `/mcp`, bearer binding token (Claude Code, Cursor and VS Code accept a static `Authorization` header; claude.ai and Desktop connectors need OAuth, so an OAuth authorization server with Client ID Metadata Documents and a DCR fallback is built in Phase 5, D-019). Tools mirror scopes: `search_names`, `get_quote`, `list_domains`, `get_domain`, `dns_list`, `dns_upsert`, `nest_names`, `secrets_get` (only with scope, one value per call), `secrets_set`, `recipe_plan`, `recipe_apply`, `propose_registration`, `propose_renewal`, `get_proposal` (state only, for the calling binding's own requests, never a URL), `transfer_status`. No bulk-injection tool exists in MCP. Tool results never include secret values unless the binding has the matching `secrets.read` scope; `propose_*` returns `{status: "pending_human_approval", approval_id}` (stored as `pending`) and never a receipt. Tool annotations set `readOnlyHint` and `destructiveHint` accurately, and **no tool that can return a secret value is ever marked read-only** (VS Code and Anthropic's directory rules skip confirmation for read-only tools). "Needs human approval" is enforced by the server (a `pending_human_approval` result plus a first-party passkey page), never by MCP elicitation, which Claude Code lets a hook auto-answer and which legacy clients cannot receive. Every call is authorized against the presented binding's scopes and never against the server's own authority; a token not minted for this server is refused and no token is passed through. An agent's `secrets_set` on `prod` keeps the prior version, emails the owner at once and offers a restore. Rate limits key on the token id, not the IP address: claude.ai connector traffic shares one address range. Descriptions are static strings we author (no user-controlled text in tool descriptions), and tool outputs that echo user-supplied strings (domain names, record values) are marked as data.

**Tokens.** Format `mh_<kind>_<32 base62 characters>+<CRC32>` (kinds `live`, `cli`, `clr`; no `test` kind until a sandbox exists), so leaks are recognisable to scanners. Stored as SHA-256 of the token (adequate for at least 128 bits of randomness), compared in constant time, looked up by an indexed prefix, shown once. Scopes, expiry and revocation are checked in the database on every request; there are no long-lived self-contained JWTs. Starting values (own targets): binding default expiry 30 days and maximum 90; CLI access token 60 minutes; refresh token 30 days idle and 90 absolute. Secret-scanning: register the prefix with GitHub's partner programme (email secret-scanning@github.com; custom patterns only detect leaks in your own repositories); enrolment, lead time and eligibility for a pre-launch company are unverified, so this is a dependency to apply for, not a guarantee, and the primary controls are short expiry and per-token limits. The receiver `POST /hooks/github-secret-scanning` verifies the signature, revokes once and emails. Idempotency keys are required on every mutating endpoint that can spend money, with a stored request hash (409 on a mismatch).

### 4.6 Threat model

**Method.** List assets, trust boundaries and adversaries; then for each area give the attack, the preventive mitigation, the detection and response, and the security tests (`ST-nn`) that prove it. Mitigations are requirements on later phases, not aspirations. Residual risk is stated where it exists. The table has 46 rows (numbered 1 to 46) and the catalogue below it has 155 tests (`ST-01` to `ST-155`), numbered in row order so that every test belongs to one row; both counts are taken by counting.

**Assets, most sensitive first.**
1. Secret plaintext (customer API keys, database URLs, stored third-party credentials) and the KMS permission to unwrap data keys.
2. Control of customers' domains: registrar API credentials, transfer authorization codes, nameserver settings, and the registrant mailbox and end-user interface at the upstream.
3. Authenticators and sessions: passkey credential records, session cookies, recovery codes.
4. Agent and CLI bearer tokens.
5. Payment flow integrity: prices, order state, the prepaid registrar balance, the Stripe webhook secret and API keys.
6. Registrant PII (contact data) and audit log integrity.
7. Mosshatch's own domains (`mosshatch.com` is the passkey RP ID), platform secrets, and the keys themselves (their loss loses every secret).

**Trust boundaries.** Browser (untrusted) to the `web` project; `web` to Neon; `web` to KMS through OIDC; `web` to the `registrar` project through a signed RPC, and `registrar` to the upstream registrar; inbound webhooks (Stripe, Resend, registrar, GitHub) and the cron tick; public internet to `hatchkind.com` (separate registrable domain, separate project, no cookies, read-only database role over a view); agents and the CLI to the API and MCP with bearer tokens (untrusted principals, server-side scope enforcement); Mosshatch to third-party providers with stored customer credentials (recipes).

**Adversaries.** Unauthenticated internet attacker; an authenticated customer attacking another tenant; a phisher or session thief; a compromised or prompt-injected agent holding a valid token; a malicious dependency or CI compromise; a compromised registrar account or registrar-side social engineering; an insider with database access; an operator with production deploy rights; whoever controls a registrant's mailbox; an XSS attacker.

**Honest limits.** The vault is not zero-knowledge: anyone who can run code in the production `web` project can decrypt what the service can decrypt until the key is denied, because agents, the CLI and recipes need server-side decryption (a client-held-key tier, passkey PRF, is a later option). Vercel (TLS termination, function memory) and AWS (data keys in KMS) can technically observe plaintext in normal operation. A value on screen can be seen by extensions, clipboard managers and screen capture; a value returned to an agent or printed by the CLI leaves Mosshatch's control; deleted secrets remain in encrypted database history for the retention window; and loss of the key is loss of every secret. The Nest help text repeats this list, and no public statement may say staff cannot read secrets (D-004).

| # | Area | Attack | Preventive mitigation | Detection and response | Test |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Secrets at rest | Database dump, backup or point-in-time history exfiltrated; a database writer swaps names, versions or rows. | Only `secret_versions` and `connection_credentials` hold ciphertext. Per-secret AES-256-GCM data key wrapped by KMS; the AAD is the length-prefixed concatenation of `format_version`, `alg_id`, `domain_id`, `secret_id`, `name`, `env` and `version`, so a ciphertext cannot move between rows or names; a 16-byte tag is enforced on both sides; values are capped at 16 KiB; `secrets.pointer_mac` (a KMS HMAC) is checked on every read, so a database writer cannot re-point a secret at an older, still-decryptable version. Off-Neon backups are encrypted under the backup key. | Alert on bulk reads of `secret_versions` by any role but the vault role. Deleting a secret nulls its ciphertext at once; encrypted copies stay in Neon history for the retention window (the delete dialog says so), and key destruction is the only real delete. | ST-01 to ST-05 |
| 2 | Secrets, KMS and code execution | Code running in `web` (or a leaked cloud identity, or a production build step) calls KMS Decrypt for many wrapped keys. | No static cloud keys: OIDC federation pinned to the Team issuer, to `sub` for project `web` in production and to a random 32-byte custom audience (a Sensitive variable, the IAM provider's only audience); 1-hour maximum session, 900-second requests, `AWS_REGION` pinned. Two vault KEKs and roles: `vault-prod` for `prod` secrets and `vault-nonprod` for `dev` and `preview`; the class comes from the stored row, never the request. Each role may call only `GenerateDataKey` and `Decrypt` on its own KEK with encryption-context conditions, with explicit denies on `CreateGrant`, `PutKeyPolicy`, `ScheduleKeyDeletion` and `DisableKey`; human principals are denied `Decrypt`. These stop wrong-shape and cross-class calls. They do not stop code that reads `owner_id` and `secret_id` from the database, and an in-process decrypt counter is not a control against code execution. Production builds probably hold the same OIDC identity as functions, so `npm ci`, the `allowScripts` policy and reviewed lockfile updates protect the build too. | Job `audit.kms_reconcile` joins CloudTrail Decrypt events (about 5 minutes late; the trail never excludes KMS events) to reveal and read rows in `audit_log` and alerts on any Decrypt with no row; those rows are inserted only by the vault database role, so compromised app code cannot forge a match. Alarms on Decrypt volume, 10 distinct secrets in 10 minutes (own target), unknown `sub` or `aud` and AssumeRole failures. An unmatched Decrypt attaches an explicit Deny on `kms:Decrypt` to the vault role through a separate identity (reversible, unlike `DisableKey`). **Residual risk, stated plainly:** the service can decrypt what it serves, so code execution in `web` exposes secrets until the deny is attached; it is detected, not prevented. The vault is not zero-knowledge, because agents, the CLI and wire-it recipes need server-side decryption. Moving the Decrypt-capable identity into its own small project is decided in Phase 4 (D-026), and the vault and step-up code get an independent review (D-032). | ST-06 to ST-13 |
| 3 | Secrets in transit and caches | A secret is cached by a browser, CDN, service worker or proxy, or appears in a URL, referrer or history. | Reveal and read are `POST`, PUT responses carry no value, and every secret-bearing response has `Cache-Control: no-store, private` and `Pragma: no-cache`; no secret in any URL or query string; no service-worker handler for `/api/*`; `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin` (with `no-referrer`, Chromium sends `Origin: null` on same-origin form POSTs, which would break the Origin-based CSRF check). The response-header set of 4.3a applies to every route. | Automated header check on every route class and on every secret-bearing route. | ST-14 to ST-15 |
| 4 | Secrets in logs, errors and telemetry | Plaintext, names or credentials appear in server logs, error reports, analytics, Stripe metadata, email, audit rows, CI artifacts or the Zustand store, most often on a failure path. | Secret routes log an error code and a request id, never a body or `error.message` from parsing, KMS or the database (V8 puts a slice of the input in `JSON.parse` errors, and Postgres echoes values in constraint errors); allow-listed structured logging; no third-party analytics or error SDK that captures bodies; emails and Stripe metadata are built from typed templates; secret names match `^[A-Z][A-Z0-9_]{0,127}$` and a name that matches a scanner rule is rejected with a generic 422; the bundle scan is inside the `build` command; trace, video and screenshot capture is off for any spec that reveals. | Canary gate (`test:canary`) with four canaries (a value, a name, a fake `mh_live_` token, a fake `x-vercel-oidc-token` header sent on every request), forced-failure cases, and a grep of every sink: server logs through a test log drain, `audit_log.detail`, `email_log`, the fake Resend transport, the CSP-report sink, console, HAR, localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, Cache Storage and a heap snapshot. The value may appear only in reveal and read response bodies. | ST-16 to ST-22 |
| 5 | Web reveal abuse | A stolen session reveals everything; scripted bulk reveal. | Each web reveal is a `secret.reveal` step-up bound to `(secret_id, env)`, one secret per assertion. `secret.reveal` is held 24 hours after a passkey is added and for the recovery holds in 4.5. Limits (own targets): 5 a minute and 30 an hour per user and session, 3 a minute per secret; agent traffic never counts against the human's bucket.
