| 35 | Rescue abuse | Transfer-in is used to pull a domain the person does not control, or to launder a payment. | OFAC screening and velocity limits, the provider's pre-transfer check, confirmation by the registrant email, no saved card from a Rescue, refusal when a DS record needs removal first (4.3b DNS), and a transfer-in order captured at submission and refunded on failure. | Abuse queue; suspend on report. | ST-126 |
| 36 | Hostile DNS record changes | MX, apex or DKIM changes intercept mail or certificates; dangling records enable subdomain takeover; a whole-zone replace deletes records; a recipe writes outside its plan. | Recipes come from a versioned, code-reviewed registry; the applied set must equal the previewed plan (hash-checked). Sensitive records are classified server-side by owner name first (any label starting with an underscore, apex, `www`, wildcards, `autoconfig`, `autodiscover`, `mta-sts`) then by type (MX, NS, DS, SRV, apex A, AAAA, CNAME), computed on the difference between a fresh adapter read and the desired zone and re-checked at commit; an agent or recipe change gets a pending `dns.sensitive.approve` that binds the before and after hashes (D-011, D-034). Every write takes a per-domain lock, refuses to delete more than 5 records or any MX, TXT or SRV the intent does not name, reads back, and keeps a 30-day snapshot with one-click rollback. Disconnecting a service deletes the recipe's records; `dns.dangling_scan` probes recipe targets weekly with provider fingerprints. **Residual risk (D-034):** a human cookie session is not gated, so a stolen session can still edit these records; a change by a human session emails every address and can be rolled back from the snapshot. Closing the gap costs a passkey on every MX, SPF or DKIM edit by the owner and is the owner's call. | Drift detection compares the registrar's live records with the cache; the daily digest and an email per new hostname; the dangling scan flags a target that no longer answers. | ST-127 to ST-131 |
| 37 | Abusive registration through our own API | Bulk or malicious registrations by new accounts or agents (ICANN's DNS Abuse work names unrestricted API access for new customers as a gap); stolen cards drain the prepaid balance. | Verified email before checkout; OFAC screening at checkout; per-account and per-agent velocity and spend limits; a new account (first 30 days) is limited to USD 300 of wholesale exposure in flight and 5 registrations a day, and any order above USD 50 wholesale waits during a risk review (own targets, 4.3b Review, refund and dispute); three accounts sharing a card fingerprint go to review; an abuse contact with a documented takedown path (the registrar stays liable for its reseller). | Abuse queue; suspend on report; an alert when unseasoned exposure nears its cap. | ST-132 to ST-133 |
| 38 | Availability and cost abuse | Flooding search exhausts the registrar quota or runs up the lookup fee; a bad-token burst from a shared connector address range throttles every Claude user; an attacker with a session locks the owner out. | Search results cached; anonymous limits 30 searches per 10 minutes per IP and 100 an hour per /24, then a proof-of-work challenge; verified users 300 an hour; `tlds` limited to the six launch extensions (400 otherwise); the global lookup budget is split 70% anonymous search and 30% checkout, renewals and agents, so the checkout check cannot be starved; a daily ceiling at 50% of the fee threshold agreed with OpenSRS; a circuit breaker with a plain-language degraded mode ("availability unconfirmed", never an invented "available"). Limits key on identities an attacker cannot exhaust: token id for authenticated calls, (source address, token prefix) for authentication failures, never a shared address-only bucket for connector traffic; step-up prepare is limited per (user, session); cancel, revoke and freeze paths are never rate-blocked (all own targets). | Budget and quota alarms; counters hold an HMAC of the address for 24 hours, not search text. | ST-134 to ST-136 |
| 39 | Webhook, cron and hook endpoints | Public endpoints invoked, forged or replayed by an attacker. | `/api/v1/webhooks/stripe`, `/webhooks/resend`, `/webhooks/registrar/{provider}` and `/hooks/github-secret-scanning` require a valid signature and a timestamp tolerance and dedupe by event id in `webhook_events`; `/api/cron/tick` requires the cron bearer secret and is idempotent; each route accepts only its declared principal and other methods are rejected. Resend bounces and complaints update `email_log`; a hard bounce to a registrant email restarts the 15-day verification clock. | Alert on repeated failures; the tick heartbeat is watched by an external dead-man's switch (page at 3 minutes). | ST-137 to ST-138 |
| 40 | Supply chain and CI | A malicious package, install script or pipeline takeover; a look-alike or compromised CLI package steals tokens. | Lockfile with `npm ci`; install scripts disabled by default; provenance and audit in CI; dependency updates delayed and reviewed; third-party scripts blocked by CSP; GitHub Actions pinned by commit; least-privilege deploy tokens; protected branches and required review; push protection for secrets. The CLI is published only from CI with npm trusted publishing and provenance (no long-lived token), 2FA on the npm organisation, no install scripts, one bundled file with a published SHA-256, at most 5 runtime dependencies pinned to exact versions, and look-alike names (`mosshatch-cli`, `mosshatch-secrets`, `mosshatch-env`) reserved. | `npm audit` and the bundle scan in the build; Dependabot-style alerts. | ST-139 to ST-141 |
| 41 | Insider and operator access | Staff read customer secrets or alter the audit trail; an operator with production deploy rights ships code that decrypts. | No routine operator path to plaintext: no admin screen, query or export returns secret values, and production database access is break-glass and logged. An operator with production deploy rights can still ship code that decrypts whatever the service can decrypt (4.3b Secrets, Blast radius); KMS does not prevent it. Compensating controls: Deployment Policies allow production deploys only from the one repository and block CLI and Deploy Hooks; branch protection with required review, and a second approver for the vault module from the day a second maintainer exists (until then the founder is the sole operator and that is stated as residual risk); deploy notifications to a mailbox outside the deployer's control; human IAM principals denied `Decrypt`; alerts on `PutKeyPolicy`, `CreateGrant`, `DisableKey` and any Decrypt with no audit row. The audit table is insert-only for the app role, does not belong to it, and is MAC-chained with a KMS HMAC key; chain heads are anchored daily as a signed root to a write-once bucket in a separate account. Support sees metadata only. | Chain verification job; alert on a gap or an anchor older than 25 hours; a restore is an audited incident. | ST-142 to ST-144 |
| 42 | Public gallery abuse (`hatchkind.com`) | Cards are used for phishing lures, brand impersonation or spam, or leak owner data. | Cards exist only for domains registered through Mosshatch and only after the owner publishes (`card.publish`, held after recovery); a card contains computed traits and the domain string as text, never free text; no outbound links; a separate origin (`cards` project) with no cookies and a read-only database role over a view of published, unreleased cards; `noindex` until the owner opts in (D-035); takedown and abuse contact; block list for known brands and homoglyphs. | Abuse queue; unpublish on report. | ST-145 |
| 43 | Email as an attack channel | Spoofed "Mosshatch" mail; link scanners trigger state changes; approval-bypass links; secrets in mail retained by the provider for 30 days. | SPF, DKIM and strict DMARC on the sending domain; emails link only to the fixed origin and never contain vault secrets, bearer tokens or an approval link. The only tokens allowed are protective ones that can only reduce access (freeze, recovery cancel, turn off auto-renew) and the emailed recovery code: each is at least 128 bits, stored hashed in `email_action_tokens`, bound to one purpose and one event, single-use and expiring (freeze 72 hours, recovery cancel until the end of the cool-off plus 24 hours, recovery code 24 hours; own targets). A GET or HEAD of the link shows a page and changes nothing (no-referrer, noindex, no-store); a confirm button sends a POST with the token in the body. Resend click and open tracking are off for these emails. Freeze never unlocks or changes nameservers and does not block renewals; unfreezing needs a passkey sign-in and resumes each paused binding through `agent.token.widen`. | DMARC reports. | ST-146 to ST-149 |
| 44 | Environment confusion | A preview or staging deployment touches the live registrar, live Stripe or the production KEK; a test event is processed as live. | `livemode` on money tables; the boot guard `assertModeConsistency()` exits unless the Stripe key mode, the adapter mode, `VERCEL_ENV`, the database host and the KMS alias agree; no live key outside production; no Production variable also scoped to Preview or Development; preview builds get no registrar credentials, no webhooks and a disabled vault; sample amounts are refused at live checkout (4.3b Environments). | A Stripe event whose `livemode` differs from its order is rejected and alerted. | ST-150 to ST-151 |
| 45 | Loss of the key or account, and a restore that rewinds | A compromised administrator schedules key deletion, the KMS account is lost, or a point-in-time restore rewinds orders and the audit chain while Stripe and OpenSRS keep newer truth, or resurrects erased data. | Loss radius controls (4.3b): multi-Region keys with a replica; a service control policy denying `ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `DisableKey` and `PutKeyPolicy` to all but a hardware-key break-glass role; 30-day deletion windows; separate production, staging and log-archive accounts; nightly encrypted off-Neon backups with Object Lock. A restore is an audited incident with a runbook (pause writes, restore to a new branch, replay Stripe events, compare OpenSRS inventory, re-run the erasure purge from the external ledger, verify the chain to the last anchor). The Terms say the Nest is not a backup of record (D-026). | Alarms on `ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `DisableKey`, `PutKeyPolicy` and `CreateGrant`; a monthly restore drill on a scratch project. | ST-152 to ST-154 |
| 46 | Support and abuse operations abused | Someone socially engineers support into bypassing the recovery delay, or files false takedowns and floods the abuse form. | Support sees metadata only, cannot unlock, transfer, change nameservers or contacts, add a passkey or bypass the recovery delay; an identity claim by email or phone never replaces a passkey ceremony; every support action writes an audit row with `actor_kind = 'support'`; abuse reports enter a queue with a reviewed decision and are rate limited; registrar-directed holds and legal requests are acknowledged within 4 hours (internal target). | The abuse queue and the audit trail. | ST-155 |

**Security test catalogue.** Each `ST-nn` becomes an automated test where it can be (unit, integration or end-to-end) and a manual checklist item where it cannot (marked "manual checklist"). Numbers run in row order; each entry names its row and the phase in which it is first due (section 5 repeats the lists as exit criteria). Tests that a later phase must re-run because new routes or principals appear are named as re-runs in section 5. The test count is 155.

**Row 1, Secrets at rest.**
- ST-01 Changing any one AAD field (format, algorithm, domain, secret, name, env, version) makes decrypt fail (Phase 4).
- ST-02 A tampered ciphertext, a flipped nonce bit, a tag truncated to 4 bytes and a `wrapped_dek` swapped between rows each fail (Phase 4).
- ST-03 Swapping the names of two secrets in one domain and environment fails to decrypt (Phase 4).
- ST-04 Pointing `current_version_id` at an older version fails the pointer MAC check (Phase 4).
- ST-05 Deleting a secret nulls `ciphertext` and `wrapped_dek` at once, re-wrap keeps the plaintext, and a value over 16 KiB gets 413 (Phase 4).

**Row 2, Secrets, KMS and code execution.**
- ST-06 The KMS policy denies a wrong principal, a human SSO role and the `web` role's own Decrypt outside the vault role (staging account) (Phase 4).
- ST-07 A token with the wrong audience, the default audience, a preview `sub` or another project's `sub` cannot assume the role (Phase 4).
- ST-08 The runtime role is denied everything outside its allowed set, including the explicit denies (Phase 4).
- ST-09 A `prod` wrapped key sent to `vault-nonprod` is AccessDenied, and a `dev`- or `preview`-scoped CLI or agent token cannot obtain a `prod` client (Phase 4).
- ST-10 The Decrypt-volume and secret-diversity alarms fire, and an unmatched Decrypt in staging raises an alert within 15 minutes (own target) (Phase 4).
- ST-11 The auto-deny attaches, blocks Decrypt for an existing session and is removable (Phase 4).
- ST-12 The `web` database role cannot insert reveal or read audit rows (Phase 4).
- ST-13 With the KMS stub down, reveal, read, write and re-wrap fail closed with 503 `vault_unavailable` and log no context (Phase 4).

**Row 3, Secrets in transit and caches.**
- ST-14 The response-header set of 4.3a is present on every route class (app pages, API, MCP, webhooks, cron, CSP report) in CI (Phase 1).
- ST-15 Every secret-bearing route (`reveal`, `read`, `/actions/{id}/commit`, secret PUT responses) is non-cacheable, carries no secret in a URL, and no service worker caches `/api/*` (Phase 4).

**Row 4, Secrets in logs, errors and telemetry.**
- ST-16 Names, tokens and the OIDC header canaries appear in no log, audit row, email, CSP report, console, HAR, browser storage or heap snapshot across the end-to-end suite (Phase 2).
- ST-17 The value canary appears only in reveal and read response bodies across the end-to-end suite (Phase 4).
- ST-18 Forced failures leak nothing: malformed, truncated, oversized and non-UTF-8 bodies to secret PUT, read and every MCP secret tool; KMS AccessDenied, throttling and timeout; a unique violation on a secret name; a CLI crash and `--debug` (Phase 4).
- ST-19 A truncated reveal response leaves no canary in the console, the network log or a report (Phase 4).
- ST-20 A secret named with a canary, an `sk_live_` string or 200 characters is rejected and the input appears in no log, audit row or email (Phase 4).
- ST-21 The bundle secret scan (17 tests) runs inside the `build` command, fails a deliberately leaking preview deployment, exits non-zero when a required server variable is not visible at build time, and covers `apps/web/dist`, `apps/cards/dist`, the CLI tarball and source maps (Phase 1).
- ST-22 A transfer authorization code canary appears in no job payload, audit row, log, MCP result or database column (Phase 3).

**Row 5, Web reveal abuse.**
- ST-23 A web reveal without a committed step-up returns 403, and one assertion reveals one secret (Phase 4).
- ST-24 `secret.reveal` is refused during the 24-hour hold after a passkey is added and during the recovery holds, with the hold end in the response (Phase 4).
- ST-25 Reveal limits hold and agent traffic never counts against them (Phase 4).
- ST-26 The audit row commits before KMS is called, a failed insert stops the reveal, and a KMS failure after commit does not reopen the challenge (Phase 4).
- ST-27 A reveal sends the notification email, and the reveal-rate alert fires above its threshold (Phase 4).

**Row 6, Bearer reads of secrets (the second read path).**
- ST-28 A cookie session on `/secrets/{env}/read` is rejected, a token without the matching scope gets 403 rather than an empty list, and `prod` needs an explicit `:prod` scope (Phase 4).
- ST-29 Every bearer read writes one audit row per secret before decrypt, and the read fails if that write fails (Phase 4).
- ST-30 Token read limits return 429 and the hourly digest email is sent (Phase 4).
- ST-31 A CLI login grant is capped to `dev` and `preview`, and a requested `secrets.read:*:prod` is refused (Phase 4).
- ST-32 A binding holding `secrets.read` with `dns.write` is refused at creation and at widening, across domains, while `secrets.read` with `recipes.apply` is allowed (Phase 4).
- ST-33 A scripted `mosshatch run` leaves the canary in no log or audit row (Phase 4).
- ST-34 `tools/list` for a token without `secrets.read` contains no secret-returning tool, `secrets_get` is never annotated read-only, no MCP tool returns a bulk map, and an MCP session leaves the canary in no log or audit row (Phase 5).
- ST-35 An agent write to `prod` keeps the prior version, emails at once and can be restored in the Nest (Phase 5).

**Row 7, XSS on a page that renders secrets.**
- ST-36 The CSP is present, enforced and violation-free in the end-to-end run (Phase 1).
- ST-37 Lint bans `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` and string-built HTML; the store contract holds (strict wire schemas, opaque `SecretValue`, import boundaries, `persist` limited to `{calm, sound, rehideSeconds}`) (Phase 1).
- ST-38 Trusted Types is enforced with zero violations before the Nest ships (Phase 4).
- ST-39 After a reveal, hide and forced garbage collection, a heap snapshot shows the canary retained by nothing in the store, `world/` or the React tree (Phase 4).

**Row 8, Clipboard and shoulder surfing.**
- ST-40 The copy action copies the command by default, a `prod` value copy is off until enabled, the page never calls `readText()`, and a manual Safari and Firefox checklist passes (Phase 4).

**Row 9, Account takeover: phishing, pre-hijacking and CSRF.**
- ST-41 Login is origin-bound: a wrong origin or RP ID fails (Phase 2).
- ST-42 An unverified account cannot register a passkey, and a victim's later verified sign-up ignores an attacker's pending row (Phase 2).
- ST-43 A `same-site` request, a login verify without the pre-auth cookie and a cross-origin form POST are each rejected on the cookie routes (Phase 2).
- ST-44 The session cookie has the `__Host-` prefix, Secure, HttpOnly and SameSite=Lax, and no route mutates on GET (Phase 2).

**Row 10, Account takeover: recovery.**
- ST-45 Recovery holds apply: codes plus emailed code holds the held actions 24 hours; email-only waits 72 hours then holds 72 hours; every id marked held in 4.5 is refused during a hold (Phase 2).
- ST-46 Email-only recovery is refused for an account with no second verified channel, and any legitimate sign-in cancels a pending request (Phase 2).
- ST-47 Recovery notices reach every notification address, and a flood of starts for one address does not stop the owner redeeming the emailed code or cancelling from a new device (Phase 2).
- ST-48 Undo: replaced credentials stay suspended for 30 days, and an assertion from one restores them, revokes the recovery's credentials and sessions, and re-applies the hold (Phase 2).
- ST-49 Recovery codes are 128 bits and stored hashed, and verification is limited to 10 attempts an hour without blocking passkey sign-in (Phase 2).
- ST-50 Filling the class A mail bucket does not suppress the "recovery started" notice (Phase 2).

**Row 11, Session theft.**
- ST-51 A pre-login session id is invalid after login, revoke-all invalidates every session within one request, and logout sends `Clear-Site-Data` (Phase 2).
- ST-52 Idle (15 minutes) and absolute (8 hours) expiry are enforced server-side (Phase 2).

**Row 12, Synced-passkey compromise.**
- ST-53 The backup-eligible and backup-state bits are stored and surfaced, a flip alerts, and hardened mode refuses backup-eligible credentials (Phase 2).
- ST-54 An unsupported-algorithm credential is rejected at registration (Phase 2).

**Row 13, Step-up forged with another account's passkey.**
- ST-55 User B's valid passkey cannot complete user A's action even with A's cookie and challenge; a revoked credential, a mismatched `userHandle`, a backup-eligible flip and a credential outside `allowCredentials` each fail (Phase 2).
- ST-56 `credential_id`, `uv`, `be`, `bs`, `client_data_json`, `authenticator_data` and `signature` are non-null on every committed action (Phase 2).
- ST-57 Route walk: no handler calls the step-up gate with an id outside the thirteen in 4.5, and every id has a gated route (Phase 2).

**Row 14, Step-up replay and races.**
- ST-58 A replayed assertion and a changed parameter between prepare and commit fail; the challenge is valid at 119 seconds and refused at 121; a failed verification leaves the challenge consumed (Phase 2).
- ST-59 Twenty parallel commits of one action succeed once (Phase 2).
- ST-60 Killing the process between registrar success and job completion, or after commit and before the job runs, leaves the detector quiet after reconciliation, and two queued actions on one domain execute in commit order (Phase 3).
- ST-61 Approve racing expire, single-binding revoke, revoke-all and a price change each leave one terminal state and at most one order, and two tabs approving one request create one order (Phase 5).

**Row 15, Agent and CLI tokens.**
- ST-62 Tokens are stored as SHA-256 only, shown once and compared in constant time, and a token idle past its limit is rejected (Phase 4).
- ST-63 The scope parser rejects a missing env, a glob, `*` in the capability position, upper case, a trailing dot, a punycode variant, an unowned domain and `register.propose` with a domain id (Phase 4).
- ST-64 The widening classifier (coverage, expiry, cap, any parse doubt is a widening, a bearer PATCH is rejected) holds under a property test (Phase 4).
- ST-65 Scope matrix (token x route x env), including `secrets.read` on `:prod` (Phase 4).
- ST-66 Revoke-all and "Send all visitors home" revoke every binding and decline pending requests in one transaction (Phase 4).
- ST-67 Route walk over every handler and principal: a bearer token on a session-only route, a cookie plus `Authorization` header, and the cron secret presented as a binding token are each rejected (Phase 2).
- ST-68 The secret-scanning webhook revokes a reported token once and emails the owner (Phase 5).

**Row 16, Device-flow code phishing.**
- ST-69 Wrong user-code entries are limited per account and per IP, and the sixth attempt in the window is refused even if correct (Phase 4).
- ST-70 Approval without the typed code fails, and `/device?user_code=` neither prefills nor approves (Phase 4).
- ST-71 Reuse of a rotated refresh token revokes the family, and after `/oauth/revoke` the access token fails on the next request (Phase 4).
- ST-72 Approved scopes equal the scopes shown, a grant emails every notification address and writes an audit row, and with device login disabled `/oauth/device/code` refuses (Phase 4).

**Row 17, Agent purchase and escalation.**
- ST-73 A bearer token cannot approve, exceed its cap or reach `/actions/*` (Phase 5).
- ST-74 Fifty concurrent proposals on one binding never exceed the cap, sixty across many bindings of one user never exceed 10 pending, and lowering the cap keeps existing requests and blocks new ones (Phase 5).
- ST-75 A duplicate pending proposal returns the existing request and reserves nothing, and reservations return to their prior value on decline, expiry, void and failure (Phase 5).
- ST-76 Approval alone never creates a charge or an authorization, no Checkout URL appears in any bearer response, log or audit row, and a Checkout total above the tax ceiling voids the order (Phase 5).
- ST-77 The agent-visible state order is approved_awaiting_payment, payment_authorized, registering, registered and never "paid" before "registered"; a price rise between proposal and approval voids the request; an approval after expiry fails (Phase 5).

**Row 18, Approval UI spoofing.**
- ST-78 Hostile client names, request text and unknown `params` keys never appear on the approval card or in email (Phase 5).
- ST-79 One assertion cannot approve two requests, and a replayed `action_id` fails (Phase 5).
- ST-80 Typed confirmation is required above the threshold, on a binding's first approval and for a new extension, and the approval route sends `frame-ancestors 'none'` (Phase 5).

**Row 19, MCP tool poisoning and prompt injection.**
- ST-81 Hostile strings in tool outputs are marked as data, and tool descriptions are static (Phase 5).
- ST-82 Scope-denied bursts are audited and raise an alert (Phase 5).

**Row 20, MCP confused deputy.**
- ST-83 An MCP call is authorized by the binding's scopes and never by the server's, and a token minted for another audience is refused (Phase 5).
- ST-84 The Origin allow-list and token check run before the SDK handler, and an OAuth client cannot use another client's consent or an unregistered redirect (Phase 5).

**Row 21, Recipe as confused deputy.**
- ST-85 A token holding only `recipes.apply` causes no DNS or variable write (403, nothing applied), and a `dev` token whose plan targets Vercel production or preview is denied (Phase 4).
- ST-86 A billable or unknown-cost step without a plan-hash-bound approval fails for user, CLI and agent principals, and the applied set must equal the previewed plan hash (Phase 4).
- ST-87 No route, tool or command returns a stored provider credential, and only `recipe.apply` and `connection.check` decrypt it (Phase 4).

**Row 22, Vault write becomes code execution on a developer machine.**
- ST-88 Every reserved name, in upper, lower and mixed case, is rejected on secret PUT, push (atomically) and recipe apply with a 422 and an audit row, and malformed names are rejected (Phase 4).
- ST-89 `run` refuses a legacy stored reserved name and `pull` omits it; hostile values (newline, quote, backslash, `$(`, backtick, NUL, `#`, `=`) round-trip through the dotenv writer unchanged (Phase 4).
- ST-90 `pull` with neither `--out` nor `--stdout` exits 2, and `--out` refuses symlinks and paths under `.git` (Phase 4).

**Row 23, Tenant isolation.**
- ST-91 Cross-tenant matrix generated from the route table: two users, one bearer per user, every method and every id, plus swapped-id fuzz on nested routes; CI fails if a route has no entry; extended and re-run each phase (Phase 2).
- ST-92 The same matrix with RLS disabled proves the application layer alone; direct SQL as the runtime role with `app.user_id` set to user B, or unset, returns none of user A's rows on any RLS table (Phase 2).
- ST-93 An unowned and a nonexistent resource give identical responses, and the transfer check does not reveal another account's ownership (Phase 2).

**Row 24, Domain changes hands.**
- ST-94 After a name leaves (transfer-out then transfer-in, or lapse then registration by another user), the old binding, secrets, cards and connections are invisible to the new owner, and a previously used name can be registered or transferred in again (Phase 3).
- ST-95 Each release cause sets `released_at` and triggers revoke, cancel, unpublish and destruction after the hold, with export working during the hold, and nothing is released while the name is restorable (Phase 3).
- ST-96 An old binding stays dead when its former owner registers the same name again (Phase 3).

**Row 25, Payment and order abuse.**
- ST-97 A client-supplied price is ignored, and the amount charged equals the amount shown (Phase 2).
- ST-98 Webhook signature, replay and out-of-order events are handled, and order state moves only on verified events (Phase 2).
- ST-99 A double submit creates one order, and the same key with different parameters returns 409 (Phase 2).
- ST-100 The authorized-amount guard rejects a low, high, wrong-currency, wrong-order or open-review PaymentIntent (Phase 2).
- ST-101 A scheduled renewal and a Renew now click produce one PaymentIntent (Phase 3).
- ST-102 A dispute test card flags the account and pauses renewals, and the refund cap applies (Phase 3).

**Row 26, Duplicate or lost registrar writes.**
- ST-103 Two workers on one order produce exactly one `SW_REGISTER` (Phase 2).
- ST-104 `timeoutAfterAccept` and `workerDeath` resolve through reconciliation without a resend, and two customers buying one name void the loser (Phase 2).
- ST-105 A lease that expires mid-job cannot commit the first worker's result, and with 10,000 queued `domain.sync` jobs `order.fulfil` still starts within 60 seconds (Phase 2).
- ST-106 When the registrar is down at `capture_before` minus 24 hours the PaymentIntent is cancelled and the customer emailed, and `capture_failed` walks its ladder (Phase 2).
- ST-107 Every generated OpenSRS request carries the explicit parameters, a `defaultPeriod2` mock fault fails any call that omits `period`, and a debit that differs from the quote pauses sales of that extension (Phase 3).
- ST-108 `forced_pending` is never `registered`, and a void cancels the upstream order (Phase 3).

**Row 27, Prepaid-balance exhaustion.**
- ST-109 The sell gate refuses orders and renewals below the threshold, renewals rank ahead of new registrations, and a voided order leaves no pending upstream order (Phase 3).
- ST-110 A renewal charged but failing upstream retries every 15 minutes for 24 hours, then hourly to E-1, and refunds only on request or if unrenewed at E-1 (Phase 3).

**Row 28, Takeover, lapse or DNS tampering of the brand domains.**
- ST-111 The daily external check compares the NS, MX and apex A of both domains with the stored expectation and fires on a difference, and the expiry alarms fire at 90, 60 and 30 days (Phase 2).
- ST-112 Both domains publish the expected DMARC, SPF, null-MX and CAA records (Phase 2).

**Row 29, Registrar-account compromise.**
- ST-113 The nightly posture job asserts `auto_renew=0`, the expected lock and privacy state for every domain, and that a Horizon test profile's login to the end-user interface redirects (Phase 3).
- ST-114 The detector fires within its cadence on a simulated out-of-band change to lock, nameservers, DS, contact email hash, `auto_renew`, `let_expire`, privacy state or a transfer (Phase 3).
- ST-115 The sixth code issue in an hour is refused and alerts, and a command outside the allow-list is rejected (Phase 3).
- ST-116 The OpenSRS key rotation drill (pause, generate, update, redeploy, smoke `GET_BALANCE`, lift) completes against Horizon, and the kill switch fails closed (Phase 3).

**Row 30, Registrar credential exposure through Vercel.**
- ST-117 The production registrar key is absent from Preview, Development and `web`, and a preview deployment cannot reach the live registrar (Phase 3).
- ST-118 A `web` to `registrar` call fails without a valid signature, with a replayed nonce or outside the time window (Phase 3).

**Row 31, Registrar-side social engineering.**
- ST-119 Manual checklist: the provider account has the strongest 2FA it offers and a recovery email off both domains, the incident contact is recorded and the questions to the provider are answered or accepted in writing (Phase 3).

**Row 32, Registrar's end-user interface and the registrant mailbox.**
- ST-120 The end-user interface redirects or hides codes and blocks password resets, codes are re-randomised at re-lock and after 24 hours, and a stored code is never read back (Phase 3).
- ST-121 The registrant email differs from the login email by default and a match shows a warning; a contact change needs `domain.contact.change` and notifies every address (Phase 3).

**Row 33, Hostile nameserver change or unlock.**
- ST-122 Unlock, nameserver change and contact change return 403 `step_up_required` without a committed action, and a DS record blocks a nameserver change to unsigned DNS (Phase 3).
- ST-123 Every unlock, code issue, nameserver change and contact change emails every notification address with a freeze link (Phase 3).
- ST-124 Transfer-out and code issue return 403 `step_up_required` without a committed action (Phase 5).

**Row 34, Transfer-away started at another registrar with a leaked code.**
- ST-125 An unrequested pending transfer-away raises the alert within 10 minutes, the state is needs attention, and the Stop button re-locks, re-randomises the code and opens the ticket (Phase 3).

**Row 35, Rescue abuse.**
- ST-126 Rescue applies screening and velocity limits, runs the pre-transfer check, asks the registrant email to confirm, never stores a card, and refuses when a DS record needs removal (Phase 5).

**Row 36, Hostile DNS record changes.**
- ST-127 Classification table: `sel._domainkey.x`, `_acme-challenge.www.x`, `*.sub.x`, upper case, trailing dot, punycode, `@` and a DKIM TXT without `v=DKIM1` are all classified sensitive (Phase 3).
- ST-128 A human-session change to a sensitive record emails every address and can be rolled back from the snapshot; a write that would delete unrelated records is refused; a rollback restores the snapshot (Phase 3).
- ST-129 A human-started recipe plan with a sensitive record needs one approval that binds the plan hash, and a recipe or batch cannot hide a sensitive record among benign ones (Phase 4).
- ST-130 A fixture target returning the provider's not-found page is flagged within one scan, and disconnecting a service deletes the recipe's records (Phase 4).
- ST-131 An agent write of a sensitive record gets a pending approval and no write (Phase 5).

**Row 37, Abusive registration through our own API.**
- ST-132 Velocity limits and OFAC screening block a scripted bulk registration by new accounts (Phase 2).
- ST-133 The same limits hold for a scripted bulk registration by agents (Phase 5).

**Row 38, Availability and cost abuse.**
- ST-134 10,000 unique labels from 200 addresses stay within the registrar budget while a checkout check still succeeds, and 500 `tlds` entries return 400 (Phase 2).
- ST-135 An attacker holding a session cannot stop the owner's step-up, freeze link or revoke-all (Phase 2).
- ST-136 A burst of bad tokens from the connector address range does not deny a valid token (Phase 5).

**Row 39, Webhook, cron and hook endpoints.**
- ST-137 Each endpoint rejects a missing or invalid signature, a stale timestamp, a replayed event and a wrong method, and `/api/cron/tick` rejects any secret but `CRON_SECRET` (Phase 2).
- ST-138 An external check pages when no tick has completed in 3 minutes (Phase 2).

**Row 40, Supply chain and CI.**
- ST-139 Install scripts are disabled and the dependency audit is clean (Phase 1).
- ST-140 Lockfile integrity and commit-pinned Actions are verified in CI (Phase 1).
- ST-141 The packed CLI tarball has no install scripts and its dependency list equals the reviewed allow-list (Phase 4).

**Row 41, Insider and operator access.**
- ST-142 Concurrent appends produce one linear audit chain, editing a row and recomputing SHA-256 does not verify without KMS, TRUNCATE is rejected, and the app role cannot UPDATE or DELETE (Phase 2).
- ST-143 A simulated point-in-time restore is detected against the external anchor and ends with the erasure purge re-run (Phase 2).
- ST-144 Support tooling shows metadata only and cannot unlock, transfer, change nameservers or contacts, add a passkey or bypass the recovery delay (Phase 2).

**Row 42, Public gallery abuse (`hatchkind.com`).**
- ST-145 The public view exposes only published, unreleased cards, cards carry no free text or outbound links, and the origin sets no cookies (Phase 6).

**Row 43, Email as an attack channel.**
- ST-146 SPF, DKIM and DMARC records are verified on the sending domain before any receipt is sent (Phase 2).
- ST-147 A repeated GET or HEAD of every email link changes nothing; a link works once, only for its own purpose and event, and fails after expiry (Phase 2).
- ST-148 Freeze locks the domain, revokes sessions and pauses bindings without unlocking or changing nameservers, unfreezing needs a passkey sign-in, and a renewal still succeeds while frozen (Phase 3).
- ST-149 Emails contain no vault secret, bearer token or approval link (Phase 2).

**Row 44, Environment confusion.**
- ST-150 A live Stripe key with the mock adapter, or a test key with a live adapter, fails to boot; a preview cannot reach the registrar, KMS or live Stripe; a sample amount is refused at live checkout (Phase 2).
- ST-151 A Stripe event whose `livemode` differs from its order is rejected and alerted (Phase 2).

**Row 45, Loss of the key or account, and a restore that rewinds.**
- ST-152 Restore drill: last night's off-Neon backup restores into a scratch project, the chain verifies to the last anchor with the expected gap, the erasure purge re-runs, and the time is recorded (Phase 2).
- ST-153 Restore drill with the vault: a canary secret decrypts in the scratch project, and a multi-Region replica decrypts under a simulated regional failure (Phase 4).
- ST-154 The key-deletion service control policy denies `ScheduleKeyDeletion`, `DisableKey` and `PutKeyPolicy` to all but the break-glass role, and the key-event alarms fire (staging account) (Phase 4).

**Row 46, Support and abuse operations abused.**
- ST-155 A support action writes an audit row with `actor_kind = 'support'`, and a lost-passkey request through support does not bypass the recovery flow (Phase 2).

### 4.7 Compliance checklist

The full checklist has 73 rows and lives in `docs/COMPLIANCE.md` (each row: requirement with version or date, who owes it, how Mosshatch meets it, phase, whether counsel must confirm, and the source). Summary by area:

| Area | Rows | IDs |
|---|---|---|
| Transfers and locks | 10 | C-01 to C-10 |
| Registrant data, RDAP and verification | 10 | C-11 to C-20 |
| Abuse, disputes and sanctions | 5 | C-21 to C-25 |
| Lifecycle, renewal and refunds | 5 | C-26 to C-30 |
| Consumer law | 6 | C-31 to C-36 |
| Payments and card networks | 5 | C-37 to C-41 |
| Tax | 6 | C-42 to C-47 |
| Privacy and security law | 6 | C-48 to C-53 |
| Accessibility | 3 | C-54 to C-56 |
| Per-TLD rules | 7 | C-57 to C-63 |
| Public card host | 8 | C-64 to C-71 |
| Legal documents and legal process | 2 | C-72 to C-73 |

#### Orientation

This section consolidates the verified Phase 0 compliance research as of 2026-09-29 for a US launch on Stripe hosted Checkout, with OpenSRS/Tucows assumed as the upstream registrar and .com .dev .app .studio .io .ai as the starting TLDs. Duties fall into four groups. First, duties on the ICANN-accredited upstream registrar, which Mosshatch does not perform but must verify and feed with data and actions (RDAP, verification enforcement, TEAC, UDRP locks). Second, duties Mosshatch takes on by contract as a reseller, because RAA 3.12 makes the registrar answer to ICANN for its resellers and requires a written flow-down (registration agreement content, fee and notice disclosure, sponsor identification, abuse handling, records). Third, duties that bind Mosshatch directly as a business under US, EU, UK and card-network rules (auto-renewal consent, tax, sanctions, privacy, PCI, accessibility). Fourth, duties of the public card host (hatchkind.com), which is not a registrar service but publishes owner-chosen domain names to the open web (DMCA safe harbour, EU DSA notice and action, take-down, screening, indexing). ICANN's regime reaches only gTLDs (.com .dev .app .studio): .ai and .io are ccTLDs outside the RAA, the Registration Data Policy, the Transfer Policy, ERRP, the AGP Limits Policy and URS, so their rules come from the registry's own terms and the upstream contract, and most of those are unpublished or unverified. Corrections from the verification passes are applied in the text; where a verifier could not settle a point the row says so. This is research, not legal advice: the Counsel column and the numbered questions mark what needs a lawyer or accountant.


#### Questions for a lawyer or accountant

Ordered by what blocks the most work first: items 1 to 5 shape the Phase 2 to 5 design for a US launch, items 6 to 10 must be answered before the first paid sale, items 11 to 15 gate enabling non-US customers or carry lower risk, and items 16 to 19 are gated by an event: names before any brand spend (16), documents before the first outside account (17), the card host before hatchkind.com goes public (18) and legal process before the Nest opens to anyone but you (19). The rows they touch are in brackets.

1. **Upstream reseller agreement (lawyer; C-08, C-11, C-12, C-14, C-18, C-62).** Question: "Please review the OpenSRS Master Services Agreement, the standalone Master Domain Registration Agreement and the May 2018 Data Processing Addendum against RAA 3.12.1-3.12.7, RAA 3.18, the ERRP, the Accuracy Specification and our checklist. Which clauses leave Mosshatch owing something it cannot perform, and are the sole-remedy clause (21.6), the indemnity (22.1), immediate termination for an ICANN-policy breach (3.9), unilateral amendment (23.3), the assignment bar (23.4) and the absence of a wind-down duty (17.4) acceptable for a business whose customers own their names? Will Tucows commit in writing to redact registrant data in RDAP and WHOIS by default at no charge for US registrants (the Registration Data Policy requires redaction only where law requires it), state who sends the Losing FOA, and agree a daily availability-lookup ceiling (MSA 3.2 mentions an unquantified excessive-use fee)?" Why it matters: RAA 3.12 makes Tucows answer to ICANN for us, so it will push duties down, and the only exit is an ICANN transfer-out of every domain; the "WHOIS privacy: free" promise rests on OpenSRS's own default.
2. **Transfer Policy I.A.5.3 against passkey gating (lawyer; C-02, C-04).** Question: "Does gating the transfer code and unlock behind a WebAuthn step-up comply with Transfer Policy I.A.5.3 ('not more restrictive than the mechanisms used for changing any aspect of the Registered Name Holder's contact or name server information') if contact and nameserver edits use the same step-up? The 5-calendar-day duty in I.A.5.1-5.2 applies only where the registrar offers no self-service: does a reseller-provided facility count for Tucows, and does a built-in wait defeat 'facilities'? May we add an owner-chosen security delay, or the brief's 'cooling-off', by default given I.A.5.3 and I.A.3.10, and once the reformed policy takes effect can an agent token ever be a 'designated representative' (TAC Rec 10.1), or today a 'Designated Agent' for a Change of Registrant (II.A.1.2)?" Why it matters: it shapes the Gate in Phase 5, and a breach is ICANN Compliance exposure for Tucows and a breach of our reseller contract. The approve-or-decline step on an outbound transfer sits on OpenSRS's own page, outside our gate.
3. **Payment assurance and seller of record (lawyer; C-39, C-41).** Question: "RAA 3.7.4 lets a credit-card charge or equivalent suffice 'provided that the obligation to pay becomes final and non-revocable by the Registered Name Holder upon activation'. Does that duty, which is the accredited registrar's, reach Mosshatch at all, given that with an OpenSRS API account Tucows is paid from our prepaid balance before it activates a name (MSA 12.1, 12.7)? Does a Stripe authorisation hold on a manually captured PaymentIntent meet the proviso, so we may authorise, register, then capture, or must we capture before the EPP create and refund on failure? Separately, are we the seller of record rather than a payment facilitator under Stripe's restricted-business terms, given wholesale-plus-flat-fee pricing and any prepaid agent balance?" Why it matters: it decides the Phase 2 order state machine (D-002), whether every failed registration costs the Stripe fee, and whether Stripe approves the account; no card-network guidance on a high ratio of cancelled authorisations was found.
4. **Auto-renewal consent and notice mechanics (lawyer; C-31 to C-34, C-38).** Question: "Is a user-verified WebAuthn assertion plus a stored terms hash sufficient express informed consent under ROSCA, California BPC 17602, Vermont 2454a, New York GBL 527-a, Virginia 59.1-207.46 and Minnesota 325G? With the charge at E-10, notices at E-43, E-32, 8 days before the charge and E+1, and a price-change reminder 21 days before the charge, does each state's window hold; can the opt-in confirmation serve as the notice when fewer than 35 days remain; do Colorado, Illinois and Massachusetts add duties; do ROSCA and the Vermont, New York, Virginia and Minnesota laws protect business buyers (California's definition covers individuals only); and what do Mastercard's recurring rules and Visa Table 5-22's regional scope require?" Why it matters: every annual renewal for every US customer runs through it; California's rules apply to renewals since 1 Jul 2025 and New York's since 5 Nov 2025, and the FTC rule is not in force but ROSCA is.
5. **ERRP notices for auto-renewed names (lawyer; C-26).** Question: "Are the two ERRP pre-expiry notices (about one month and one week before expiry) owed for auto-renew domains that renew successfully before expiry, given ERRP's unconditional 'any gTLD registration' wording, our charge at E-10 and OpenSRS's own auto-renew job about 30 days out? How is 'within five days' after expiry measured?" Why it matters: no ICANN guidance exists; sending both anyway is cheap but changes the mail cadence, and ICANN Compliance reaches us through Tucows.
6. **US state nexus and registration (accountant; C-42, C-43).** Question: "In which states does Mosshatch have physical presence today (founders, contractors, hosted infrastructure, agent activity), which states should we register in and when, given at least 23 gross-receipts states, the 200-transaction prong in about 16 jurisdictions and a $15 average order, and do we need zero returns or voluntary registration before the first sale? How do Washington B&O and Texas franchise-tax nexus apply?" Why it matters: nexus can arrive at about $3,000 of revenue in some states, and Stripe Tax silently returns zero tax where no registration exists.
7. **Taxability, principal or agent, resale certificates (accountant; C-44, C-45).** Question: "Is registration, renewal and transfer of a domain name taxable in each state as it comes into scope (consumer and business), which Stripe product tax code applies to each, is Mosshatch principal (tax on the full price) or agent, and does OpenSRS require or accept a resale certificate (none found in its published terms)?" Why it matters: no state revenue-department page names domain registrations and Stripe has no domain tax code, so a default code risks over- or under-collecting.
8. **Data-protection roles and paper (lawyer; C-48, C-49, C-52).** Question: "Is Mosshatch a controller, joint controller or processor relative to Tucows, the registry and the escrow agent for registrant data? How should the RAA 3.7.7.5 'consent' be worded against GDPR Art 6 bases; is the May 2018 Tucows DPA adequate or must we negotiate a new DPA and SCC modules; do we need EU and UK representatives for a US-only launch when a registrant may live in the EU or UK; and are we below the CCPA thresholds and out of other US state privacy laws?" Why it matters: RDP s7 makes registry transfers conditional on DPAs, and this paperwork gates collecting registrant data in Phase 2.
9. **Retention against erasure (lawyer; C-19).** Question: "Which retention periods can we rely on after an erasure request: RDP 15 months, RAA term plus 2 years, 180-day logs, OpenSRS's 3-year records, California's 3-year consent evidence and 10-year EU VAT evidence? Does a contractual duty qualify under GDPR Art 17(3)(b), or must we ask Tucows for an ICANN data-retention waiver? May a pseudonymous, hash-chained audit log with opaque ids be kept after erasure, and how long may erased data persist in database history and vendor logs?" Why it matters: the clocks conflict, and the Phase 2 deletion job needs one schedule.
10. **OFAC scope (lawyer; C-25).** Question: "What is Mosshatch's OFAC status (jurisdiction unknown)? What screening (name, organisation, country; at signup, contact change and payment) and country blocks are required, may we serve customers in Iran, Cuba, North Korea or occupied Ukrainian regions, and how can we freeze or deny a transfer-out for a sanctioned holder when the Transfer Policy has no sanctions-based denial reason?" Why it matters: sanctions liability is strict, ICANN gives no cover, and the design belongs in Phase 2.
11. **EU and UK withdrawal right (lawyer; C-35).** Question: "For CRD Art. 16(a) and UK CCR reg. 36, is a domain registration or renewal a service 'fully performed' at registration? What proportionate amount may we keep after a 14-day withdrawal that falls outside the 5-day registry window (who bears the sunk .io and .ai cost), and what express-request and acknowledgement wording is valid?" Why it matters: it decides the EU and UK refund promise and gates enabling those markets; Gandi's contract treats the service as executed with a waiver, the Commission's guidance says a terms clause alone may not suffice, so both positions are contestable.
12. **EU and UK VAT classification and other non-resident regimes (VAT advisor; C-46, C-47).** Question: "Is a domain registration an electronically supplied service under Regulation 282/2011 Art. 7 and Annex I, and a digital service for UK VAT, for consumers and for businesses? If yes, which Member State for Non-Union OSS and what B2B evidence standard (VIES or HMRC valid only)? Which other non-resident regimes (Norway, Canada, Australia, Switzerland, Japan, India) need action before those markets are enabled?" Why it matters: it decides whether EU and UK consumer sales need registration at all; no Commission, VAT Committee or HMRC text names domains.
13. **NIS2 reach (lawyer; C-51).** Question: "Does a US reseller storefront count as an 'entity providing domain name registration services' under NIS2 Art 6(22) in the Member States where customers live, is an Art 26(3) representative required, and which national regime applies?" Why it matters: the definition names resellers and applies regardless of size, with 72-hour access replies; national transposition is unverified.
14. **Public commitments and terms wording (lawyer; C-29, C-36, C-53, C-59, C-60).** Question: "Please review the wording of the no-front-running and no-sale-of-search-data commitments (they must stay true while the registrar and registries see queries), the renewal-refund-deletes-the-domain term, the .io and .ai non-refundable, two-year-term and sovereignty disclosures, and the 'one flat fee, no upsells' claim once tax is added." Why it matters: deceptive-claim and unfair-terms exposure, and the brief promises honest money.
15. **Accessibility scope (lawyer; C-54, C-56).** Question: "Is Mosshatch a microenterprise under Recommendation 2003/361/EC in each target market (counting owner-managers, partner and linked enterprises), does domain registration count as an EAA e-commerce service, and what is our US demand-letter process and insurance stance?" Why it matters: the EAA exemption ends on growth, a voluntary accessibility statement is cheap, and US web-accessibility suits are rising.
16. **Trade-mark clearance for both names (trade-mark attorney; C-69).** Question: "Please run full clearance for MOSSHATCH and HATCHKIND in classes 9, 35, 42 and 45 (US, EU, UK), starting from CROSSHATCH (US Reg. 7836851, WO 1766322), the crowded HATCH-formatives such as HATCH LABS and the PyPA developer tool Hatch, and advise on filing strategy." Why it matters: the knockout found no exact hits but is not a clearance search (D-012); brand spend should wait for the answer.
17. **Entity, terms and the legal document set (lawyer; C-12, C-13, C-14, C-27, C-48, C-72).** Question: "Which entity and jurisdiction should contract with customers, Tucows and Stripe, and please draft or review the documents in the legal document set: Terms of Service, the Domain Registration Agreement with per-TLD addenda, the Privacy Notice, the refund and cancellation policy, the fee and notification page and the auto-renewal text." Why it matters: Stripe's live review and OpenSRS's per-registration assent both need them before the first live sale, and the hash and version of each is stored per acceptance.
18. **Public card host (lawyer; C-64 to C-67, C-69, C-70).** Question: "For hatchkind.com: what does DMCA safe harbour require beyond the designated agent and a repeat-infringer policy; is a US-facing, not EU-targeted card host within the DSA as a hosting service or an online platform, and is an Art. 13 legal representative needed; does hosting a technical share page rather than a marketed brand change infringement exposure; and is a domain name that identifies a person, shown on a public card, personal data with a lawful basis and an erasure path?" Why it matters: safe harbour is a condition, not a mandate, and the answer gates the public launch of the card host (Phase 6).
19. **Legal process for customers' secrets (lawyer; C-73).** Question: "The vault is server-decryptable. What legal process (subpoena, court order, authority request) must or may we honour for a customer's plaintext secrets, what notice must we give the customer, and what should the Terms and Privacy Notice say?" Why it matters: a promise of secrecy that legal process can defeat must say so, and the policy must exist before the Nest holds anyone's secrets (Phase 4).

Current ISO 3166 status unchecked. | Keep the purchase-time disclosure and revisit long prepay. | https://www.iana.org/domains/root/db/io.html; https://www.icann.org/en/blogs/details/the-chagos-archipelago-and-the-io-domain-14-11-2024-en; TLD s2.4 +V |
