| Quarterly | Diff ICANN's consensus-policies page, RAA global amendments (none after 5 Apr 2024), OFAC programme list, DPF status and upstream notices. | Standing compliance-review task. | https://www.icann.org/en/contracted-parties/consensus-policies; RDO s7 (item 14), C13 |


#### Where the research disagrees with the brief or with common belief

- **Transfer-out "cooling-off" (brief, Gate).** Transfer Policy I.A.5.1-5.2 require the code and the unlock within 5 calendar days of the holder's request only where the registrar offers no self-service. Mosshatch plans self-service through OpenSRS, so for .com, .dev, .app, .studio and .ai the binding limits are I.A.5.3 (no mechanism more restrictive than the ones for contact or nameserver changes), I.A.5.4, I.A.3.10, and the 5-day .com pending window once a transfer starts. .io codes are issued by OpenSRS support, so there the 5-day duty applies as written. A default cooling-off is therefore an open counsel question, not a settled prohibition. Until counsel answers, the delay is owner-chosen, stays inside 5 calendar days, and applies to contact and nameserver changes as well as to unlock and code release (D-009). The upstream also constrains "cancel any time": OpenSRS has no API to start or approve an outbound transfer, and the registrant approves through OpenSRS's emailed link, outside our passkey gate.
- **Registrar call "only after a verified payment_intent.succeeded" (brief).** The order-flow research recommends authorise, register, then capture (cancelling is free and unreported to card networks, while refunds keep Stripe's fee) and gating on a verified requires_capture state (D-002). The registrar-data research reads RAA 3.7.4 as needing the charge first. The clause's own condition is that the obligation to pay becomes final and non-revocable on activation, the duty is the registrar's (Tucows, paid from our prepaid balance), and whether it reaches Mosshatch or a hold meets the proviso is unresolved (question 3). A hold also cannot cover asynchronous registrations.
- **"WHOIS privacy free" (brief).** For gTLDs the Registration Data Policy requires redaction only where applicable law requires it (s9.2.1) and lets the registrar redact elsewhere; OpenSRS's KB says contact details are not published by default, so the promise is honest only once OpenSRS confirms in writing that it redacts by default for US registrants at no charge, although it still lists privacy at $3 a year. .ai and .io differ: OpenSRS offers no privacy for either and .ai shows contacts in WHOIS.
- **Transfer timing.** "Up to 5 calendar days" is the registry's pending window, one stage of the flow. Through OpenSRS the longest seen is about 2 weeks, and for .ai and .io the registry sets the timing and it cannot be predicted, so the copy says "several days and sometimes about two weeks" and never a cap.
- **"No pre-checked boxes" against a default transfer lock.** A default clientTransferProhibited at registration is allowed only with express consent in the registration agreement (I.A.5.1), so make it a clause accepted with the terms, not a pre-ticked box.
- **The reformed Transfer Policy is not in force.** Blogs that say the TAC took effect on 19 Nov 2024 conflict with ICANN's own pages: only the 21 Feb 2024 policy is in force, the reform was adopted on 7 Jun 2026 with no effective date, and the planning date is 2028 or later (Mar 2027 is a floor, not a forecast). The 2024 update was also not terminology-only: it removed the Administrative Contact as an approver and added the I.A.2.1.1 carve-out and new AuthInfo duties (I.A.5.7, I.A.6.1).
- **ERRP is not new in 2025.** Its duties have bound registrars since 31 Aug 2013; only the terminology revision had the 21 Aug 2025 deadline. Two duties are narrower than usually stated: the expired-name page duty applies only if the registrar points the name at its own page, and the 30-day Redemption Grace Period is the registry's.
- **RAA facts.** The text in force is 21 Jan 2024, "Reseller" is section 1.26 (one dossier said 1.24), and no global amendment exists after 5 Apr 2024.
- **"Click-to-cancel is the law".** It is not: the 8th Circuit vacated the 2024 amendments on 8 Jul 2025, the old rule was restored on 12 Feb 2026, and only an ANPRM is open. ROSCA and state laws bind, and New York's law is in force (since 5 Nov 2025), not pending. Whether these laws protect business buyers is verified only for California.
- **Nexus rules of thumb.** "$100,000 or 200 transactions" is stale in NC and ME; Kansas counts gross receipts (at least 23 such states, not 22); Washington's WAC does mention domain registration (no taxability holding); Register-for-me needs a US bank account only in states that charge fees or require banking details.
- **EU withdrawal.** The research inference that an EU consumer "probably can withdraw" is unsupported either way; market practice (Gandi) is the opposite with a waiver, and both are contestable.
- **Stripe numbers.** "Network programmes are unreachable at launch" holds only for the Excessive tiers (Visa's Non-compliant tier of 5 events and 0.5% is reachable); "never use the Tax API" fails above a $100 tax-inclusive order; a lost countered $10 dispute is a $40.00 balance debit and a $30.59 net loss, not $40.59 plus $10; float arithmetic gives $0.73 where Stripe charges $0.74 at $15, so use integer cents.
- **Availability checks.** RDAP 404 or NXDOMAIN does not mean a name is available; registry RDAP terms restrict high-volume automated use; and OpenSRS's contract mandates Tucows' lookup with an unquantified excessive-use fee.
- **Retention.** "Sponsorship plus 2 years" is not the whole rule: 15 months for the defined RDP data set, the RAA term plus 2 years for records, 180 days for logs, and 3 years in the OpenSRS MSA.
- **Names in Mosshatch's own name.** OpenSRS MSA 3.10 does not itself require the customer to be the registrant (that comes from the MDRA, RAA and RDP), but the customer must be the registrant, and holding names for customers would make Mosshatch a Proxy Service.
- **EAA micro-enterprise exemption.** It is not lost "as soon as" a threshold is passed; the Recommendation 2003/361/EC test uses two consecutive periods and counts owner-managers, partners and linked enterprises. EN 301 549 v3.2.1 is the practical reference, not a statutory one.
- **Premium names.** They look like a revenue line, but the OpenSRS terms forbid markup on aftermarket premium and caching of premium data and give no refund or auto-renew for registry premium, and OpenSRS may change registry-premium fees without notice, so premium is not sold at launch (D-031).
- **A public card gallery is not duty-free.** No registrar rule covers hatchkind.com, but publishing owner-chosen names to the open web brings DMCA safe-harbour conditions, EU DSA notice-and-action duties if the host offers services in the Union, and trade-mark and personal-data questions (C-64 to C-71).
- **Per-TLD surprises.** The .ai 2-year minimum predates the 2026-03-05 price step, and expired .ai names now go to a registry auction through Namecheap, not Dynadot. .io: treaty signed 2025-05-22 and not in force; the UK confirmed in April 2026 that it was pausing the plans and on 22-23 Sep 2026 ministers said the deal will be reworked while payments stay on hold and it cannot go ahead without US support (AFP, Guardian, BBC, as read by the TLD verifier); IANA's .io record is unchanged since 2023-01-18, so the direction is stall, near-term loss is unlikely and the question stays open; any retirement would run 5 years (maximum 10).

### 4.8 Names: trademark knockout and the hatchkind.com proposal

This is research, not legal advice. A real clearance search by a trademark attorney must follow before anything is spent on the brand.

**What was queried (2026-09-29).** USPTO Trademark Search (its own JSON API, through headless Chromium; https://tmsearch.uspto.gov), EUIPO eSearch plus (https://euipo.europa.eu/eSearch/), TMview (https://www.tmdn.org/tmview/: 143 million marks across 81 offices, including the UK, WIPO, Canada, Australia, Japan and China; feed dates vary by office: Russia and Jordan were last updated in 2022, nine offices including India in May 2026, and CNIPA on 2026-09-03; none affects a US launch) and, for Mosshatch, the WIPO Global Brand Database (https://branddb.wipo.int). Not queried: the UK IPO directly (https://trademarks.ipo.gov.uk/ipo-tmtext, whose Cloudflare challenge host is blocked by egress policy; UK marks were read through the TMview GB feed), the WIPO database for Hatchkind (a captcha loop), and most of the open web (the search tool budget of 200 calls was exhausted early, so open-web and common-law coverage is thin and marked as such).

| | MOSSHATCH | HATCHKIND |
|---|---|---|
| Identical or near-identical marks | None found (also Moss Hatch, Mosshatched; exact, contains, wildcard, fuzzy and phonetic modes) | None found. Closest: HATCHKINZ (US Reg. 7054458, plush toys, Class 28) and two dead HATCHKINS filings. "Hatch Kind" and "Hatchkinds" return nothing |
| Closest collision | **CROSSHATCH** (Olympus Technologies Inc., US Reg. 7836851, registered 2025-06-17, Class 42 data-storage and API software as a service; also WO 1766322, which EUIPO lists as "IR accepted", indicating an EU designation): two letters different and rhymes. The site https://crosshatch.io/ says "We shut down July 2025" but never names the registrant, so linking it to this registration is our inference and needs a company or assignment check. The registration is live, the risk is not US-only, and three years of non-use is prima facie abandonment (15 U.S.C. 1127), which would arise in mid-2028 at the earliest if use ended in July 2025 | The wider word HATCH is crowded: 23 solo HATCH marks in Classes 9, 35, 36, 42 and 45 in the US (18 registered, 5 pending) and about 60 more live marks containing HATCH; 2025 to 2026 filings include HATCH LABS (Classes 35, 36, 42, 45) and HATCHIFY (an AI-agent software as a service, filed 2026-08-11). Hatch Ltd. has pending HATCH filings at EUIPO (019402520) and the UK IPO (UK00004423741) in Classes 35, 36, 37, 42 and 45. Developer-tool collision: the PyPA project manager Hatch and its hatchling build backend (PyPI `hatch` and `hatchling`; an unrelated npm package named hatch also exists) |
| Class for domain registration | **Class 45.** The USPTO ID Manual (entry 42239, https://idm-tmng.uspto.gov/id-master-list-public.html; the API cannot pin an edition, and 13-2026 is the Nice edition label) lists "Domain name registration services" in Class 045 and notes that resellers may use that wording. Class 42 covers the software as a service, hosting, vault and authentication. Class 9 covers the downloadable CLI | same |
| Risk read | Class 45 low for a unitary MOSSHATCH mark, but HATCH-alone naming is not free there (HATCH LABS holds US applications in Class 45, Hatch Ltd. has pending EU and UK filings in Classes 42 and 45; none is close to MOSSHATCH); Class 42 medium (CROSSHATCH plus crowded formatives); Classes 9, 35, 36 and 38 low. **No blocker found; proceed to professional clearance** | Medium: "Hatch" is crowded; needs counsel |
| Other formatives | MOSS: Nufin GmbH (fintech software as a service, Classes 9, 35, 38, 42) and Polyarc (creature VR game, Classes 9 and 41). Avoid "Moss" or "Hatch" alone as a product or feature name | |
| Meaning | Coined compound of moss and hatch; not in Wiktionary | |

**The two domains.** RDAP (https://rdap.verisign.com/com/v1/domain/mosshatch.com and https://rdap.verisign.com/com/v1/domain/hatchkind.com; bootstrap https://data.iana.org/rdap/dns.json; accessed 2026-09-29) shows `mosshatch.com` and `hatchkind.com` were both created 2026-09-29 at 17:48:38 UTC (about 75 minutes before the check), expire 2027-09-29, sit at NameCheap, Inc. (IANA 1068) with status `client transfer prohibited`, use the registrar's default name servers and forwarding MX and SPF, have no DMARC and no DNSSEC, and point at parked pages (HTTPS to the apex fails, so share links cannot work until hosting and certificates exist). The registrant is not in public RDAP, so ownership by the user cannot be verified from public data; it is consistent with what the brief states. Consequences: both are "New Domains" (under 30 days) in Cloudflare Gateway's category until about 2026-10-29 (https://developers.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-one/traffic-policies/domain-categories/), and ICANN lets the registrar deny a transfer within 60 days of creation (https://www.icann.org/resources/pages/transfer-policy-2016-06-01-en; for `.com` the registry blocks it), so neither can move to another registrar before about 2026-11-28. Both labels are unregistered under `.net`, `.org`, `.io`, `.ai`, `.dev`, `.app` and `.studio` (RDAP 404 against same-server controls; `.co` has no usable RDAP). The `.io` check uses an Identity Digital endpoint that is not in the IANA bootstrap, and an RDAP 404 does not show reserved or premium status, so availability and price come from the registrar in Phase 2. Package and handle names for `mosshatch` on npm, PyPI, crates.io, RubyGems, NuGet, YouTube, TikTok, X, Bluesky and the Play Store looked free; GitHub, Instagram and the npm scope could not be verified.

**Protecting the two domains (D-033).** Passkeys are bound to the RP ID `mosshatch.com`, which cannot change after launch without re-registering every passkey, so custody of both names matters more than their creation date. Renew both for at least five years (the `.com` maximum is ten) with auto-renew on; hold them in a registrar account whose recovery email is not on either domain and that uses the strongest two-factor sign-in the registrar offers; keep the transfer lock on; add DNSSEC and a CAA record if the registrar supports them; and use registry lock where offered (ask OpenSRS whether resellers can have it for their own names). After 2026-11-28 decide whether to move both to the OpenSRS account. Threat row 28 in 4.6 covers takeover, lapse and DNS tampering.

**Proposal: `hatchkind.com` as the public gallery and share-card host, at `hatchkind.com/<domain>`. Recommendation: yes, as a separate registrable domain, under conditions.**
- It is cross-site and cross-origin from `mosshatch.com`, so cookies, storage and passkeys are isolated (https://web.dev/articles/same-site-same-origin). But path URLs put every card in one origin, so the host must be **credential-free, cookie-free and read-only**: a separate deployment with its own database role that can see only a view of published cards.
- The real risk is user-generated content: cards display arbitrary domain names, which invites phishing lures and brand impersonation. Gate publishing (`card.publish` passkey step-up, a Google Web Risk check, since the Safe Browsing API is non-commercial only, and a typosquat and homoglyph screen), render names as plain text and never as links, show punycode, set `noindex` by default, and provide a report link, a DMCA agent and an EU DSA Article 16 notice-and-action route. The DMCA agent is designated on the service and with the Copyright Office and renewed every three years (USD 6 per the Cornell copy of 37 CFR 201.3, to be confirmed on copyright.gov); whether the DSA applies to a US-facing card host is a question for counsel. Cards are only ever for domains registered through Mosshatch and contain computed traits, never free text. Rarity is cosmetic, computed from the name hash, and never changes price, fee or availability. Sources, accessed 2026-09-29: https://developers.google.com/safe-browsing ; https://cloud.google.com/web-risk/docs/overview ; https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/17/512 ; https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/HTML/?uri=CELEX:32022R2065.
- Search visibility: published cards are `noindex` until the owner ticks "Let search engines list this card"; the sitemap lists only the Find page and opted-in cards. This reconciles the brief's Phase 6 sitemap with the `noindex` default (D-012).
- `hatchkind.com` sends no email: `v=spf1 -all`, a null MX and a DMARC `p=reject` (https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7208.txt ; https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7505.txt). Mosshatch mail is sent from a `mosshatch.com` subdomain.
- Passkeys are bound to the RP ID `mosshatch.com`; `hatchkind.com` has no sign-in. If it ever needs one, use a redirect login (Related Origin Requests exist but are limited to five labels in Chrome).
- Make the share host a configuration value so that a rename is a redirect, not a rewrite, and do not build public brand equity in HATCHKIND until counsel has cleared it (decision D-012).

### 4.9 Reference-site study (2026-09-29)

None of the four is a registrar. They are a sports and party-game site (https://rallywild.com), two mahjong game sites (https://airmahjong.com and https://lightningdragon.com) and a food-ordering prototype (https://puestofijo.com). Each was studied once by a single analyst on 2026-09-29, by design without an independent verifier, in a software-rendering sandbox behind an egress proxy, so timings are indicative. The brief's statement that "these sites share" one place is only verifiable per site; no site links to a sibling. What they share is a construction style: procedural three.js (r170 to r186) with no 3D model or texture downloads, and Vercel hosting. The rest varies: three of four synthesize audio, three of four send readable HTML, and airmahjong.com and lightningdragon.com use a photographic or baked hero still. rallywild.com's privacy page names Vercel, Neon, Resend and Stripe, the same stack as the Mosshatch plan.

| Site | What it proves | Weight and stack | Best idea to adopt | What to avoid |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| rallywild.com | A live 3D scene as hero background, playable with no account (only a two-checkbox age and safety dialog), short punchy copy, a purpose-built failure screen | Vite, vanilla TypeScript, three r170, no React; about 400 kB over 36 requests to a live hero; audio synthesized, no files | Prerendered readable HTML first; a classic-script "boot sentinel" (inline on rallywild.com, an external `/boot.js` in Mosshatch because our CSP forbids inline code) that separates "no WebGL" from "the chunk did not arrive" and self-heals; adaptive quality tiers; sound only after a gesture; native `details` and `dialog` | Canvas with no text alternative; the WebGL-missing screen is a dead end; brand and codename split; fixed mobile layout clips a card at 390 x 844; sound settings not persisted |
| airmahjong.com | "Play before signup" (guests play with an optional name), a lesson route that works without WebGL, three in-brand no-WebGL messages (desktop, iOS, script failure) with exits | Lobby 24 requests, about 631 kB (a 302 kB hero photo is the bulk); three r170 (141 kB brotli) only on game routes; every asset served `max-age=0` | An ambient hero with Pause and Replay controls that honours reduced motion and Save-Data; a sound comfort dialog with separate levels; 44 px targets and 3 px focus rings | No `<main>` or skip link; canvas without a text alternative; no caching on hashed assets |
| lightningdragon.com | A tiered hero: blurred placeholder, then baked still, then 2D ambience, then live 3D behind a capability gate and a frame-time watchdog, so the 138 kB brotli three chunk never loads on phones or reduced-motion | Desktop lobby 21 requests, about 343 kB; mobile 322 kB; reduced motion 278 kB; no JavaScript 17 kB | The tiered hero; a sentinel and panel that say why 3D is missing (built external in Mosshatch, because our CSP forbids inline code) and offer exits that still work | Everything client-rendered (empty root, no visible `<h1>` in the served HTML; the only one sits in the hidden failure panel); server room created before the WebGL check; no skip link; only HSTS as a security header |
| puestofijo.com | A scroll story over a full-viewport procedural street scene with one strongly named place; reservation needs only a first name; a whole 3D city generated in code (608 kB JavaScript raw, about 158 kB on the wire, lazy after hydration, no model or texture downloads) | Next.js prerender on Vercel; 0.69 MB over 28 requests by 10 s; fonts are 46% of transfer | A story that reads as real DOM copy over the scene; honouring reduced motion in both CSS and JavaScript | A fabricated "N watching" counter (`viewerOpens + 3`, fallback 21); horizontal overflow at 390 px; hero copy held for a 4.5 s intro; no-JavaScript users stuck on a veil; no in-brand WebGL-unavailable message |

**Patterns Mosshatch adopts.**
1. A real-HTML first paint of the Find page: `<h1>`, the search field and the price list are static HTML and CSS, so the no-JavaScript and no-WebGL pages are the same page. The price list in the static HTML is generated by a scheduled build from the adapter's `quote` results at least daily, carries an "as of <date>" line, and is replaced by live quotes as soon as the script runs; in Phase 1 it shows labelled sample prices from `MockRegistrar`.
2. A tiered hero: live scene on capable devices behind a capability gate and a frame-time watchdog, a baked still and 2D ambience otherwise, and never a blank screen.
3. A boot sentinel served as an external classic script, `/boot.js`, with three in-brand messages (no WebGL2, iOS-specific, script failure) that each keep a working plain search and price list. It is external because the enforced CSP (`script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'`, section 4.3a) blocks inline script and style, which the reference sites use (lightningdragon.com, for one, sets no security header but HSTS). It is a parser-blocking classic script (no `defer`, no `async`, not a module) placed before the module script, so its `error` listener and marker check are attached before the module runs. The panel is pre-rendered `[hidden]` markup with its styles in the external stylesheet; the script only removes `hidden` and sets `textContent`, never `innerHTML`, which also keeps Trusted Types Report-Only quiet, and the panel stays legible if the stylesheet fails to load. `/boot.js` is unhashed, so it is excluded from pattern 8 and served with revalidation. The Phase 1 exit criterion is that the fallback works with the CSP enforced and zero violations, and the first-paint measurement in 4.10 is repeated with `/boot.js` in the critical path. Only Chromium was run for CSP behaviour in Phase 0; Firefox and Safari are read from specifications (4.10).
4. Adaptive quality tiers and a persisted Calm setting.
5. Sound off by default and only after a gesture, behind the one header toggle the brief asks for, with a persisted setting; nothing is announced by sound alone. The reference sites' comfort dialogs and separate levels are not adopted.
6. An Arrival demo labelled "Demo" that any input cancels, plays once per visit, never autoplays in Calm mode and has Pause and Replay, honouring reduced motion and Save-Data.
7. Real landmarks: `<main>`, a skip link, one `<h1>`, native `details` and `dialog`.
8. Immutable caching for hashed assets, which three of the four sites got wrong (rallywild.com, airmahjong.com and lightningdragon.com send `max-age=0, must-revalidate`; puestofijo.com sends `max-age=31536000, immutable` for its hashed chunks, per a reviewer's header check of 2026-09-29; its dossier recorded only the HTML header).
9. No invented counters and no copy held behind an intro.
10. A 390 px overflow check in the end-to-end suite.

### 4.10 Performance and accessibility budgets, and the test plan

#### Performance budget

Numbers marked (measured) come from a build made in this session (Vite 8.3.1, React 19.3.0, zustand 5.0.15, three 0.186.1; see `docs/research/measure-bundle-size.md`); (sourced) numbers come from the cited vendor or standards text; (brief) numbers are requirements given in the project brief; (own target) numbers are our choice and are not claimed as facts. Frame times cannot be measured meaningfully in this container (software rendering), so CI enforces structural budgets and real-device frame times are a manual protocol.

| Budget | Value | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| Frame time | 16.7 ms (60 fps) target on a mid-range phone (brief); a 30 fps quality tier at 33.3 ms (own target, a proposed default in D-018) | Brief; WebKit halves `requestAnimationFrame` under Low Power Mode and aggressive thermal mitigation, so a 30 fps tier must exist (sourced) |
| Adaptive resolution | Step DPR down when the frame average exceeds about 22 ms; cap DPR at 1.75 (own targets) | Brief; about 1.0 to 1.25 megapixels of drawing buffer on a phone at the cap (sourced arithmetic) |
| Draw calls | at most 60 in any view (`renderer.info.render.calls`) | Cap: brief. Plan: our own design, unmeasured, worst case with every item counted at once. Sky 1 (the moon is drawn in the sky-dome shader, as the brief specifies), pool 1, ground 1, merged trees 2, props 2, grass 1, lanterns 3 plus halos 1, fireflies 1, pollen 1, bursts 1 (shards and sparks), eggs up to 6, wisps 1 = 22; charms 7 (one `InstancedMesh` per shape type, shared by all creatures), Armored shells 1 (instanced; the only blended mesh), hatch beam 1, crate and gate 2, distant-silhouette instances 1 = 34; plus one mesh per animated creature, at most 24 = 58 of 60, so the cap leaves 2 calls. Charms and shells must be instanced or the cap fails. The card snapshot is a second `render()` into a render target and is counted separately. How a creature's parts animate (a procedurally generated float data texture of part matrices, which is data written in code and not an image asset so it stays within "no model files or textures", or the dossier's `InstancedMesh` per part type) is chosen in Phase 1 by a spike; if `InstancedMesh` per part type wins, recount as part types times families instead of 24. A `mat4` uniform array indexed per vertex is avoided because Arm advises "Do not dynamically index into uniform arrays" (sourced, `docs/research/tech-webgl-three.md`). Arm's native guidance of under 500 draw calls is approximate and native, so it is context, not a WebGL limit. The count is asserted per view in CI |
| Fill rate | average overdraw at most 2.5 (own target, taken from the 2.5 average-overdraw factor in Arm's fragment-shader budget method, which is an assumption for estimating cycles per fragment, not an Arm-recommended ceiling); MSAA off as an engineering judgement (Arm recommends 4x MSAA on tile-based GPUs, so both are tested on real devices); no `discard`; no blending on full-screen layers; hatching computed only on geometry, not as a post-process | Arm mobile guidance (sourced) for the no-`discard`, no-blending and 4x-MSAA clauses. A screen-space hatching shader is fill-rate bound, so this is the main risk |
| Initial JavaScript (gzip) | target 110 kB, hard 130 kB (own target) | Measured shell (React + zustand) 68.7 kB gzip (measured); the lazily loaded scene chunk is 134.9 kB gzip, of which 128.4 kB is the unshakeable `WebGLRenderer` core (measured); web.dev's under-170 KB critical-path guide covers all resource types and was last updated 2018-11-05 (sourced, dated) |
| Scene chunk (lazy, gzip) | hard 150 kB (own target) | Measured 134.4 kB as the lazy chunk and 134.9 kB for the `three` import set alone (measured); the cap leaves 15.6 kB above the measured chunk for real shaders, more geometry and creature code. The plan has not yet said which chunk holds `src/audio` |
| Passkey chunk (lazy, gzip) | hard 5 kB (own target) | Measured 2.9 kB as a lazy chunk and 3.1 kB standalone (measured) |
| Total JavaScript (gzip) | target 270 kB, hard 300 kB (own target) | Measured 206.6 kB with the shell, three and WebAuthn, before app code and shaders (measured) |
| Fonts | at most 45 kB WOFF2 for both families (own target) | Measured subset: Young Serif 18.4 kB + Atkinson Hyperlegible Next variable (wght 400 to 700) 20.4 kB = 38.8 kB (measured); 45 is our headroom. The original Atkinson pair as two static files is 22.2 kB, 40.7 kB in total, also inside the cap (D-029) |
| First paint | Real content (hero line, search field, price list) is static HTML and CSS that paints before the scene chunk is requested. Gate: FCP at most 1.2 s target and 1.5 s hard on Slow 4G with a 4x CPU throttle (own target); the scene import is gated on a `PerformanceObserver` paint entry (`buffered: true`), not on `requestAnimationFrame` | The numeric gate comes from `docs/research/measure-bundle-size.md` section 6. Measured on a client-rendered React shell, not on static HTML (headless Chromium 141, CDP throttling, software WebGL, not a real phone; median of 5 cold-cache runs per cell): FCP 0.82 s with the lazy split versus 1.96 s with a static bundle (1.6 to 2.0 s across two run sets). The scene chunk was requested after FCP in 45 of 45 runs (3 lazy variants x 3 network profiles x 5); without the FCP gate it was requested at about 95 ms, before FCP at about 308 ms (single unthrottled run). Pre-rendered static HTML is expected to be faster and is not measured; the double-`requestAnimationFrame` fallback branch of the gate was written but not tested |
| Long tasks | no single task over 50 ms after first paint outside shader compilation; compile via `compileAsync` | Own target |
| Idle cost | Ledger view renders at a reduced rate; hidden tab renders nothing | Brief |
| Memory / triangles | 24 animated creatures and all props under 150k triangles | Own target; verified on the debug route with `renderer.info` |

#### Accessibility budget

Target: **WCAG 2.2 Level AA** (W3C Recommendation; ISO/IEC 40500:2025). WCAG 3 is still a Working Draft. The full 58-gate checklist is in `docs/research/tech-a11y-standards.md` section 8; the gates that shape the design:

- **Contrast (computed with a script, not estimated):** Lantern on Night 9.61:1; Ink on Lantern 10.51:1; Shell on Night 14.31:1; Shell on the frosted panel at least 6.29:1 even over pure white. **Failing pairs to avoid:** Lantern on Shell 1.49:1 and Shell on Pool 2.80:1. Focus ring is three-banded (Night, Lantern, Night) so it holds at least 3.10:1 on any background. Over the same worst case (the panel over pure white) Lantern text is 4.25:1, below 4.5:1, and the Pool and sleeping dots are 2.26:1 and 2.98:1, below 3:1. Lantern text therefore sits only on panels over the dark grove (9.61:1 on Night), and because a dot never carries state alone (text and shape always accompany it) the 3:1 non-text requirement is met by the text; a test checks that no state dot appears without its label.
- **State colour is never alone.** The state hues have almost identical lightness (needs-attention against traveling is 1.01:1), so every state needs a shape and text as well as a dot.
- **Hold-to-reveal.** WCAG's Understanding text for 2.1.1 names "a key must be held down for an extended period" as a failing timing. The plan therefore ships hold-to-reveal as an enhancement and always offers a single-activation path (activate, confirm, reveal), which is how the keyboard and switch-access route works. This departs from the brief's "hold Space/Enter" wording and is recorded as decision D-010.
- **Auto re-hide after 10 seconds** is a time limit (2.2.1): it is adjustable (up to 10x), and the revealed value is never placed in a live region.
- **Motion.** `prefers-reduced-motion` starts Calm; Calm is also an in-page control and a real pause (2.2.2). The Arrival demo plays once and can be paused.
- **Targets** are 44 px, which exceeds the 24 px minimum of 2.5.8. The Ledger is not cited as the 2.5.8 "Equivalent" exception, because that exception needs the alternative control on the same page; the 44 px rule carries it.
- **Canvas.** Every chip, tag and control is a real button or link; the Ledger is the full table alternative; status changes go through polite live regions, with no announcements for ambient animation.
- **Sound and the Arrival demo (D-036).** Sound is off by default, starts only after a gesture, sits behind the one header toggle the brief asks for, and nothing is ever announced by sound alone. The Arrival demo is labelled "Demo", is cancelled by any input, plays once per visit, and does not autoplay in Calm mode.
- **European Accessibility Act.** An online domain shop selling to EU consumers is very likely an in-scope e-commerce service, but microenterprises (fewer than 10 staff and turnover or balance sheet at most EUR 2M) providing services are exempt (Art. 4(5)). Launch-stage Mosshatch is probably exempt and the exemption ends as it grows: counsel to confirm.
- **Tooling limits.** axe-core 4.13.0 has 105 rules; only `target-size` maps to a WCAG 2.2 criterion and it is off by default, so tests enable it and the `wcag22aa` tag explicitly. Text over the WebGL canvas comes back as "incomplete", not pass, so those cases are listed for manual review. Automated tools cannot judge hold, drag, focus-obscured or announcement quality.
- **Screen-reader matrix** (WebAIM survey #11, July to August 2026, n=1,780): JAWS with Chrome first (31.2% of primary pairs), NVDA, VoiceOver on Safari; mobile VoiceOver and TalkBack.

#### Test plan

| Layer | What | Tooling and rules |
|---|---|---|
| Unit | `deriveTraits` golden tests (same domain gives the same creature in browser and Node; rarity is cosmetic and never an input to price, fee or availability), `deriveCreatureState` precedence table, money math (integer minor units, no floats), scope parser and widening classifier, envelope encryption (each AAD field, tampered ciphertext, name swap and pointer rollback fail; re-wrap keeps plaintext), step-up challenge derivation, audit MAC chain | Vitest; property tests for the order machine |
| Contract | One shared adapter contract suite run against `MockRegistrar` (with its fault modes: `timeoutAfterAccept`, `workerDeath`, `duplicateSubmit`, `sameNameTwoUsers`, `insufficientFunds`, `async250`, `registryMaintenance`, `renewDraft`, `defaultPeriod2`) and the chosen provider's sandbox; real Horizon responses are recorded and replayed in CI | Sandbox limits are recorded per provider (OpenSRS Horizon cannot simulate transfers, redemption or `.ai`) |
| Integration | Postgres on a Neon branch or local; Stripe test mode with the Stripe CLI forwarding webhooks; duplicate, out-of-order and replayed webhooks; registrar timeout (`outcome_unknown`); quote changed at fulfilment | Runtime DB role created with SQL, not the console owner role (which bypasses row-level security); preview and test branches come from a seeded, anonymised branch, never from production, and use the non-production key class only |
| End to end | Playwright against a `mode=test` build that includes the debug route, with WebGL on: every creature state (`?debug=states`) and every view at desktop and phone sizes; console and page errors fail the test | Pin `@playwright/test` `~1.63.0` (the validated manifest, D-021). In this Phase 0 container only, Chromium 141 is preinstalled, so pass `executablePath` or use 1.56.1 and do not run `playwright install`; developer machines and CI run `npx playwright install chromium`. Generate baselines in the same image that runs them, because cross-machine pixel identity is unverified. Never pass `--disable-gpu`; pass `--enable-unsafe-swiftshader` |
| Visual regression | Deterministic screenshots via `?t=` (frozen time uniform), `?seed=` (seeded RNG) and an on-demand render loop; byte-identical across 5 launches in the verified experiment | `toHaveScreenshot` with `scale: 'device'` and `threshold: 0` (the defaults hide real changes); `page.clock` is not deterministic enough; pin GL flags because they change 0.1% of pixels |
| Accessibility | axe via `@axe-core/playwright` with `wcag22aa` and `target-size` enabled; scripted tab-order snapshots; manual keyboard pass and screen-reader pass per phase | Text over the canvas is reviewed manually |
| Security | Every `ST-nn` in 4.6 (155 tests over 46 rows, counted), each due in exactly one phase (section 5) with re-runs named; the canary gate (`test:canary`) with forced-failure cases; the bundle secret scan inside the `build` command; a CSP violation-free run; header checks on every route class; a route walk and the cross-tenant matrix, both generated from the route table so CI fails if a route has no entry; manual checklist items where a test cannot be automated | The scan script and its 17 tests were prototyped in this session (`docs/research/measure-secret-scan.md`), are wired into `build` in Phase 1, and have not yet been exercised on Vercel |
| Performance | Bundle size (size-limit), draw calls and triangles on the debug route, first-paint order and the FCP gate (1.5 s hard in the Slow 4G profile), lazy-chunk timing | Structural budgets in CI; frame times only on real devices |
| Real-device pass (manual) | 60-second frame-time capture, five-minute thermal soak, touch feel, Safari/iOS behaviour | Device list agreed in Phase 1; results reported per phase |
| Operational drills | Restore (Phase 2, then monthly), webhook-secret roll (Phase 2), OpenSRS key rotation against Horizon (Phase 3), KMS compromise or loss with a timed run (Phase 4), the live rehearsal with founder-owned names (Phase 6) | Each drill has a runbook with the template in section 5 and records its time; results are reported per phase |

**Supported browsers.** The passkey floor (Chrome and Edge 108, Safari and iOS 16, Firefox 122; caniuse via `docs/research/tech-webauthn-passkeys.md` F51, accessed 2026-09-29) intersected with WebGL2. Below it the Find page and price list still work, and sign-in shows: "This browser can't use passkeys. Update it or use another."

#### What cannot be verified in this container

Real-device GPU behaviour and shader precision on Mali, Adreno and Apple GPUs; thermal throttling and battery; Safari and iOS WebGL (no WebKit installed; Playwright's WebKit is not Safari); touch feel; audio output; WebGPU (no adapter); cross-machine pixel identity; live calls to any registrar sandbox, Stripe, Resend, KMS or Neon (no credentials exist in this session); Vercel behaviour (nothing was deployed); Firefox and Safari behaviour of the CSP, Trusted Types, SameSite and `__Host-` cookie results (only Chromium 141 was run; the rest is read from MDN and compatibility data); the fallback branch of the FCP-gated scene import. Every number in the budget table above is labelled measured here, sourced from a dated page, taken from the brief, or our own target.

### 4.11 Research limits

The search tool had a 200-call budget that ran out early in the session; later research used direct fetches of primary pages, and every dossier says which sources it could not reach. Blocked or unreachable and not worked around: Namecheap and ResellerClub legal and pricing pages (Cloudflare challenge whose challenge host is blocked by egress policy), the UK IPO trade mark search, the W3C site behind Cloudflare (substituted with the W3C GitHub mirror), Mastercard's rulebook, GoDaddy's API terms, `domains.opensrs.com`, some tax-authority pages (Australia, India), GitHub HTML pages (this session is bound to its configured repository), and several vendor pricing pages behind bot checks. No credentials for any provider exist in this session, so **no live API call was made to a registrar, Stripe, Resend, KMS, Neon or Vercel**, and nothing was deployed. Where a verifier could not confirm a claim it is marked unverifiable in the dossier and in `docs/research/verification-digest.md`. Only Chromium 141 was run for browser behaviour; Firefox and Safari results are read from specifications, MDN and compatibility data.

## 5. Phases 1 to 6: scope, exit criteria, risks

Every phase ends with the report the brief requires: what shipped, what is still rough, screenshots of the states touched, test results, and the decisions needed. Nothing from Phase 2 onward starts before the plan is approved, and each phase waits for "go".

**How exits work.** Each exit is measurable and lists (a) the security tests first due in that phase (every one of the 155 `ST-nn` tests in 4.6 is due in exactly one phase, and a CI job that parses those lists fails if any catalogue id is missing from Phases 1 to 6), (b) the compliance rows built there, taken from the Phase column of docs/COMPLIANCE.md (a row tagged with two phases is built in the earlier and finished in the later), and (c) the runbooks and drills that phase owes. Tests that must be re-run when a later phase adds routes or principals are named as re-runs. **Every exit also requires: every compliance row tagged for the phase is built, or deferred with your written approval.** A runbook skeleton is written in the phase where its asset first exists, with one template: trigger, severity, first 15 minutes, decision owner, customer-notice template, upstream and regulator clocks (Tucows within 4 hours of a compromised credential, MSA 2.8; GDPR 72 hours where in scope; ICANN through Tucows within 7 days), evidence to preserve, and an exit test.

### Phase 1: the place (no backend)

**Scope.** Vite app and workspace scaffold; design tokens; the hatch shader (three ink layers in screen space, moss overlay, rim light, speckle, desaturation, sleep dimming, attention pulse, shedding band, fog, sway); sky dome; pool with ripples; merged trees, stones and grass; lanterns with flicker; fireflies and burst particles; goal-and-damp camera with the four views and fit-to-aspect; four creature families built from primitives plus the two palette reuses; all eight states; Find view with eggs, sleepers, struck-through chips, alternatives and "The deal"; the Arrival demo; the Hatch sequence and the card snapshot; WebAudio sound; Calm mode; the WebGL fallback page; mobile layout; `MockRegistrar` with deterministic availability and prices labelled "sample price"; `?debug=states` (excluded from `dist`).

**Also in Phase 1 because they gate everything later:** `packages/core` (`deriveTraits`, `deriveCreatureState`, money), the store contract with its four enforcement layers, the CSP and header baseline (external `/boot.js` fallback working under the enforced CSP), the three-project scaffold (`web`, `registrar`, `cards`) and the environments matrix with the mode guard stub (4.3b), the bundle-size and draw-call budget checks in CI, the bundle secret scan wired into the `build` command and proven by a deliberately failing preview deployment, a fresh install and audit of the pinned versions, the Playwright and axe harness, and the CI job that checks every `ST-nn` is due in one phase.

**Exit criteria.** Budgets in the Performance section met on the debug route (draw calls, initial JavaScript, FCP gate, DPR step-down works); zero console errors; axe clean on every view; keyboard pass done; the same domain hatches the same creature in the browser and in Node (golden test); first paint shows real content before the scene loads; the fallback page works with WebGL disabled and with JavaScript disabled; the deliberately leaking preview build is refused by Vercel; ST-14, ST-21, ST-36, ST-37, ST-139, ST-140 pass. Compliance rows built here: C-53, C-55.

**Main risks.** Fill-rate of the full-screen hatch on phones (mitigation: hatch only on geometry, cheap sky and pool, adaptive DPR, quality tiers); draw-call budget with many creatures (mitigation: charms and shells instanced, the creature part-animation method chosen by a Phase 1 spike, and a cap on animated creatures in the Grove); shader precision differences on mobile (mitigation: hash functions that avoid large `sin` arguments, real-device pass listed as not testable here).

### Phase 2: accounts and money

**Scope.** Neon schema and migrations (Launch plan until real customer money); email-verified sign-up, passkey sign-in and recovery with the holds and the second notification address (4.5); sessions and the CSRF guard; step-up (`actions` prepare and commit) with the gate for every id whose route exists; Stripe test-mode Checkout; verified webhooks; the order state machine with `order_operations`, the authorization-window rules and idempotency; `MockRegistrar` with its fault modes behind the real machine; receipts through Resend with SPF, DKIM and DMARC verified first; the jobs table with priorities, leases and the cron tick and its dead-man's switch; the audit log with its MAC chain and the daily anchor; rate limits in Postgres; the mode guard; account closure, export and erasure designed (built in Phases 3 and 4); support and abuse inboxes as real mailboxes. **The AWS account, the KMS HMAC and PII keys and the write-once anchor bucket exist from this phase**, and an erasure ledger outside the database. Legal and money scope: terms of registration and per-registration assent capture (C-12, C-14), the registrant contact form (C-15), sanctions screening (C-25), the fee and notification page (C-27), the tax-region gate and separate Stripe products per operation (C-42, C-44), the effective-dated wholesale price table with quote pinning (C-63), statement descriptor, Radar and the dispute-rate alarm (C-39, C-40), SAQ A eligibility (C-37). **Restore drill.** Restore last night's off-Neon backup into a scratch project, verify the chain to the last anchor, re-run the erasure purge and record the time against the 4.3b targets.

**Runbooks and drills.** Stripe key or webhook-secret exposure, and payment mismatch; drill in staging: roll the webhook secret with its 24-hour overlap.

**Exit criteria.** Order machine tests including duplicate webhooks, out-of-order events, registrar timeout (`outcome_unknown`), two workers on one order, two customers buying one name, quote-changed-at-fulfilment and the authorization-window guard; the price shown equals the amount charged in every test; the restore drill recorded (target: reads within 4 hours, writes within 8, own targets, or the targets are revised); the webhook-secret drill done; ST-16, ST-41 to ST-59, ST-67, ST-91 to ST-93, ST-97 to ST-100, ST-103 to ST-106, ST-111, ST-112, ST-132, ST-134, ST-135, ST-137, ST-138, ST-142 to ST-144, ST-146, ST-147, ST-149 to ST-152, ST-155 pass; the cross-tenant matrix (ST-91) runs over every route that exists. Compliance rows built here: C-01, C-12 to C-15, C-18, C-19, C-25, C-27 to C-30, C-36, C-37, C-39 to C-45, C-48 to C-50, C-55, C-57, C-58, C-61, C-63, C-72. Decided with counsel before this phase (P0 rows): C-09, C-11, C-12, C-17, C-45, C-49.

**Main risks.** The step-up commit path and the order machine are the two places a subtle bug loses money or trust, so both get the property tests above before any Phase 3 work builds on them.

### Phase 3: my domains

**Scope.** Grove and Ledger bound to real data; Overview and DNS tabs; the first real adapter against OpenSRS Horizon (OpenSRS activation, Horizon credentials, ACH enrolment and the float are your actions at the start of this phase, after counsel's MSA review and the answers to the written questions in 4.1); state derived from adapter data; sync, expiry and renewal jobs and emails on the E-43, E-32, C-8 and E+1 schedule; opt-in auto-renew with off-session charging; drift detection and the unattributed-change detector with its cadence; the nightly posture job; DNS write safety (per-domain lock, snapshots, rollback) and a Horizon test of whole-zone versus per-type overwrite; the gates for unlock, nameserver and contact changes; `transfer.poll` for hostile transfers; domain release; the sell gate and the balance job; registrant verification (C-16), Change of Registrant handling (C-07), DS records (C-20), ERRP notices (C-26), refund rules (C-29) and the dispute lock state (C-23); a Stripe sandbox test that cancelling an authorization works as documented.

**Runbooks and drills.** Registrar credential exposure or unattributed change (drill: pause writes, rotate the OpenSRS key, redeploy, verify, tell OpenSRS within 4 hours); registrar outage; Stop a hostile transfer (4.3b Gate).

**Exit criteria.** The shared adapter contract suite passes against `MockRegistrar` and Horizon (real responses recorded and replayed in CI); every creature state is reachable from adapter fixtures; renewal notices at E-43, E-32, C-8 and E+1 are sent from the jobs table in staging; the detector fires within its cadence on a simulated change; the OpenSRS-key rotation drill is rehearsed against Horizon; ST-22, ST-60, ST-94 to ST-96, ST-101, ST-102, ST-107 to ST-110, ST-113 to ST-123, ST-125, ST-127, ST-128, ST-148 pass, and the cross-tenant matrix (ST-91) re-runs on the domain routes. Compliance rows built here: C-03, C-07, C-16, C-20, C-23, C-26, C-28 to C-34, C-38, C-53, C-55, C-59 to C-62.

**Main risks.** Horizon cannot simulate transfers, redemption or `.ai`, so those paths are proven only by `MockRegistrar` until the live rehearsal in Phase 6.

### Phase 4: the Nest

**Scope.** KMS-backed envelope encryption with the two vault KEKs and the loss-radius controls (4.3b), secrets CRUD by environment, hold-to-reveal with a single-activation path, step-up and re-hide, the audit trail and reveal notification, the second read path (bearer reads), **the CLI with cli-bindings** (`login` by device flow, `pull`, `push`, `run`) including token hashing, scopes, revoke and `POST /bindings/revoke-all`, wire-it recipes for hosting, Postgres and email (a human-started plan with a sensitive record needs one plan-hash approval), stored provider credentials, Trusted Types enforced, the CLI published only from CI, and the canary walk. Measurements owed here: KMS latency from an `iad1` function, the build token's `sub` decoded in a throwaway production build, and the sandboxed reveal frame decision (D-026).

**Runbooks and drills.** KMS compromise or loss, with a timed drill as an exit criterion: revoke, attach the deny, disable the key in staging, scope from CloudTrail, produce the per-customer list of affected secrets, send the notice, migrate to a new KEK with `ReEncrypt`.

**Independent review (D-032).** A written review of the vault module and the step-up commit path by a paid reviewer who did not write them; every finding closed or accepted in writing before the exit.

**Exit criteria.** ST-01 to ST-13, ST-15, ST-17 to ST-20, ST-23 to ST-33, ST-38 to ST-40, ST-62 to ST-66, ST-69 to ST-72, ST-85 to ST-90, ST-129, ST-130, ST-141, ST-153, ST-154 pass; the canary gate is green for values, names, tokens and the OIDC header; the recipe preview equals what is applied; the CLI works against staging with the device flow and a `dev` grant; the route walk (ST-67) and the scope matrix re-run because this is the first phase with a bearer principal; the cross-tenant matrix re-runs over secrets, connections, audit, recipes, device flow and CLI bearer; the timed key-compromise drill is done; the independent review is closed. Compliance rows built here: C-55, C-58, C-73.

**Main risks.** The vault is the product's promise, so a bug in AAD canonicalisation or nonce handling is the worst case (mitigation: the AAD tests, the independent review, fail-closed behaviour); values printed by the CLI or returned to an agent leave Mosshatch's control (mitigation: reserved names, `--out`/`--stdout`, `run` preferred, plain help text).

### Phase 5: transfers and agents

**Scope.** Rescue (transfer in) and the Gate (transfer out) on the honest transfer state machine, built on what the upstream allows (with OpenSRS: unlock and code issue by API, transfer start by the gaining registrar, approval by email; see 4.3b); agent bindings (create and widen by step-up, the lint, the coverage-based widening classifier); the visitor experience; the approval flow with the approval card, spend-cap reservations and the payment path through Stripe Checkout (4.5); the MCP server and REST; the OAuth authorization server for MCP (D-019); the secret-scanning receiver; "Send all visitors home"; velocity limits for agents (C-24).

**Runbooks and drills.** Account takeover through recovery abuse; agent token leak.

**Exit criteria.** No transfer is shown as complete before the adapter confirms; ST-34, ST-35, ST-61, ST-68, ST-73 to ST-84, ST-124, ST-126, ST-131, ST-133, ST-136 pass; a scripted agent session proposes a purchase, waits, is approved with a passkey, is paid by the human on Stripe test-mode Checkout, registers in the sandbox and only then captures; the cross-tenant matrix re-runs over bindings, approvals and transfers, and the route walk over the MCP and agent routes. Compliance rows built here: C-01 to C-06, C-08 to C-10, C-23, C-24, C-55.

**Main risks.** Transfers cannot be exercised in Horizon (mitigation: `MockRegistrar` modes now, the live rehearsal with spare domains in Phase 6); an agent driving a human's logged-in browser remains a stated residual risk.

### Phase 6: hatchkind.com and launch

**Scope.** Publish flow, public gallery and card pages on the separate origin; static pre-render of the Find page and public pages; sitemap; status and error pages (the status page on a provider other than Vercel and AWS); the whole-system tabletop; legal documents in place; `/.well-known/security.txt` (RFC 9116) with a disclosure policy and safe harbor, checked on a real deployment (D-032); **an external penetration test with a retest** (authentication and recovery, step-up, agent tokens and MCP, reveal, `hatchkind.com`). **Live rehearsal and soft launch, before the public switch:** (1) with founder-owned names through the live path: one `.dev` and one `.com` bought with a real card; a refund inside the add-grace window; the authorize-register-capture path and a forced registrar timeout; a renewal through the mandate at E-10 (scheduled by hand); lock and unlock; a DNS write and its propagation detection; a transfer-out to a second registrar and a transfer-in back (both unverifiable in Horizon); a redemption restore if the upstream allows; the dispute runbook in test mode with Stripe's dispute test cards (about USD 100 to 150, own estimate); (2) a Stripe live-mode parity checklist (live webhook endpoint with the pinned API version, restricted key, tax registrations, statement descriptor, Radar rules, payout schedule, receipts); (3) the egress decision (D-005) executed and the OpenSRS IP rules set, Neon Scale, the break-glass role tested, the on-call sheet and status page, the backup drill result, Node 24 confirmed; (4) an invite-only beta of 30 days with an account allow-list, `limits.daily_registrations` of 5 and the float from 4.2.

**Exit criteria.** ST-145 passes and the whole suite (ST-01 to ST-155) is green or waived in writing; every row of COMPLIANCE.md tagged P6 is closed and every row with Counsel Y is answered by counsel or accepted in writing, and no row is closed for the first time in Phase 6 unless tagged P6; the penetration-test findings are closed or accepted in writing; 14 days of the beta without an unresolved `outcome_unknown`, reconciliation clean and no unacknowledged S1 alert; the Phase 6 report lists each unknown from the written questions in 4.1 as verified or still open. Compliance rows built here: C-13, C-17, C-19, C-21, C-22, C-35, C-40, C-43, C-46 to C-48, C-50 to C-52, C-54 to C-56, C-64 to C-73.

**Main risks.** The first live use of OpenSRS, Stripe, production KMS keys and real mail would otherwise be public launch (mitigation: the rehearsal and the capped beta); a pen-test finding late in the schedule (mitigation: Phase 4's independent review and the per-phase tests).
